These formulations should not be treated as neutral contemporary anthropology or as evidence that some demographic groups are psychologically higher than others. At most, they show Jung's attempt to describe unequal individual capacities for psychological differentiation using the hierarchical language available to him. The hierarchy itself is historically and ethically problematic.

The Laboratory therefore retains Jung's central developmental claim, that people differ in how far and in what ways they become conscious, while rejecting racialised or civilisational ranking as a universal psychological principle.

---
## Part VIII - Post-Jungian Relational Developments

### 50. Jung versus Young-Eisendrath

[POST-JUNGIAN RELATIONAL REVISION]

Polly Young-Eisendrath's _Love Between Equals: Relationship as a Spiritual Path_ (2019) is a later relational and psychospiritual theory that draws on psychoanalysis, Jungian thought, and Buddhism. It should not be read backward into Jung.

### Jung

Jung's relationship model centres on consciousness versus unconscious identity, projection, parental complexes, archetypal image, complementarity, developmental asymmetry, persona, and individuation. His central movement is from a collective or projection-organised relation toward a more individual psychological relationship. [Primary: Jung, CW 17, §§324-345]

### Young-Eisendrath

Young-Eisendrath makes equality and dialogue much more explicit than Jung does. She describes personal love as a relationship between equals in which each person is treated as a particular other rather than as an object to control, complete oneself, or confirm a fixed story. Projection and projective identification remain important, but her practical emphasis falls on mindfulness, dialogue, becoming an accurate witness, taking responsibility for one's own subjectivity, and developing what she calls mature dependence or interdependence. [Post-Jungian: Young-Eisendrath, _Love Between Equals_, chs. 1-6]

Her model also makes the problem of idealisation more explicit. Falling in love can involve idealising projection, and subsequent disillusionment can create an opportunity to recognise the partner more accurately. Yet her own claims about projection and personal love are sometimes stated more universally than this Laboratory would treat as established empirical fact. The important historical point is that her model is a **later relational development**, not a quotation of Jung.

### What she develops or corrects

- Jung's differentiation from projection becomes an explicit practice of dialogue and witnessing.
- Jung's largely intrapsychic account of unconscious image becomes a two-person account of how subjectivities shape one another.
- Equality becomes a stated relational value rather than an implication inferred from person-to-person encounter.
- Dependence is reformulated as potentially mature interdependence rather than something simply overcome.
- The partner's otherness becomes a central ethical and spiritual focus.
- Her Buddhist framework introduces assumptions not present in Jung's marriage essay.

### Comparison 6: Jung and Young-Eisendrath

| Dimension | Jung | Young-Eisendrath |
|---|---|---|
| Primary problem | Unconscious identity, projection, collective role, one-sidedness | Projection/projective identification, domination, misrecognition, failure of dialogue |
| Developmental aim | More individual psychological relationship within individuation | Love between equals, dialogue, witnessing, mature dependence, psychospiritual practice |
| Otherness | Emerges as projection is differentiated | Explicit ethical and dialogical centre |
| Equality | Not developed as a modern relational doctrine | Explicit and normative |
| Dependence | Ambivalent; can reflect contained/container asymmetry | Reframed as mature dependence/interdependence |
| Method | No structured couple practice | Explicit dialogical and mindfulness practices |

[Primary: Jung, CW 17; Post-Jungian: Young-Eisendrath, chs. 1-6]

### 51. Jung versus Schwartz-Salant

[POST-JUNGIAN RELATIONAL REVISION]

Nathan Schwartz-Salant's _The Mystery of Human Relationship_ (1998) begins from Jungian projection and alchemy but moves toward a field model. He argues that relationship cannot be adequately understood only by asking who projects what onto whom. He proposes an **interactive field** or **third area** created and encountered between people, with subjective, relational, and archetypal dimensions. [Post-Jungian: Schwartz-Salant, _The Mystery of Human Relationship_, chs. 1, 4-5]

### Jung

Jung's key relationship mechanism is still largely organised through subject, object, unconscious content, projection, and withdrawal of projection. His transference psychology becomes reciprocal and alchemical in later work, but the marriage essay itself does not offer a developed relational field theory.

### Schwartz-Salant

Schwartz-Salant adds the idea that mutual process can acquire a dynamic life that is not usefully reduced to separate intrapsychic projections. He draws on projective identification, mutual subjectivity, embodied experience, and alchemical symbolism to conceptualise the "in-between." In his model, transformation may require partners or analyst/analysand to experience the field itself rather than simply correct false attributions.

This is a substantial shift. Projection remains relevant, but the unit of analysis is no longer only the individual psyche in relation to an object. The relational field becomes psychologically consequential in its own right.

### Comparison 7: Jung and Schwartz-Salant

| Dimension | Jung | Schwartz-Salant |
|---|---|---|
| Basic unit | Subject, unconscious content, object, projection; later reciprocal transference | Interactive field/third area between participants |
| Projection | Central mechanism | Necessary but insufficient for relational depth |
| Projective identification | Not Jung's primary construct | Explicitly incorporated from later psychoanalysis |
| Mutual process | Present, especially in transference, but less systematised in marriage essay | Central |
| Alchemy | Symbolic model of psychic/transference transformation | Explicitly extended to human relationships |
| Main innovation | Differentiation from projection toward individual relation | Field process that can transform both participants |

[Primary: Jung, CW 16; CW 17; Post-Jungian: Schwartz-Salant, chs. 1, 4-5]

### 52. Jung versus Knox

[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENTAL REVISION]

Jean Knox's _Archetype, Attachment, Analysis_ (2003) revises analytical psychology through developmental psychology, cognitive science, psychoanalysis, and attachment theory. Her project is not simply to add attachment vocabulary to Jung. It changes the developmental account of how psychic structures and meanings emerge. [Post-Jungian: Knox, _Archetype, Attachment, Analysis_, chs. 2-7]

### Jung

Jung explains partner perception through personal complexes, inherited archetypal predispositions, unconscious image, projection, and the life-course process of individuation. Development is important, but the mature theory often treats archetypal structures as a priori organising factors.

### Knox

Knox emphasises emergent developmental structures, image schemas, internal working models, patterns formed through early relationship, reflective function, and the relational origins of defences. This gives a more detailed account of how repeated interpersonal experience can shape expectations of self and others.

The comparison is especially useful for partner expectations. A Jungian projection and an attachment-based internal working model can both influence what a person expects from a partner, but they are theoretically different. Projection concerns unconscious attribution of psychic content to an object. An internal working model is a learned/developed representation and expectation concerning self, other, and attachment relations. They can interact without being identical.

### Comparison 8: Jung and Knox

| Dimension | Jung | Knox |
|---|---|---|
| Developmental architecture | Personal complexes plus archetypal/collective structures | Emergent developmental mind shaped by relationships and cognitive-developmental processes |
| Partner expectation | Projection, imago, complex, archetypal image | Internal working models and relational schemas |
| Early experience | Important, especially parental complexes | Central to development of symbolic and relational structures |
| Reflective function | Not a Jungian core term | Explicitly important |
| Attachment | Not a formal theory in Jung | Integrated as a developmental framework |
| Change | Consciousness, differentiation, symbolic integration | Reflective capacity, new relational experience, revised meanings and models |

[Post-Jungian: Knox, chs. 3-7]

### 53. Jung versus Contemporary Jungian Couples Therapy

[CONTEMPORARY JUNGIAN COUPLES]

Elizabeth Eowyn Nelson and Anthony Delmedico's _The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy: An Introduction_ is a contemporary clinical extension, first published by Routledge in 2026 in the Project EPUB. Its chapters explicitly organise couple work around the Self and individuation, complexes, archetypal life, typology, shadow, alchemy, diversity, and the therapist. [Contemporary Jungian couples: Nelson & Delmedico, _The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy_, chs. 1-8]

This source matters because it demonstrates the distance between Jung's relationship theory and a modern couple-treatment model. Jung wrote about marriage, projection, transference, personality, and individuation, but he did not codify a structured method for assessing a couple, interrupting interaction cycles, staging enactments, or conducting conjoint therapy.

Nelson and Delmedico bring concepts that were scattered across Jung's individual and analytic psychology into an explicit couple setting. This is an extension. Where they align Jungian complexes with concepts from other therapies, the Laboratory still preserves theoretical boundaries. For example, a complex may interact with an attachment threat, but a complex is not simply another name for an attachment strategy.

The source is also historically significant because its 2026 chapter on diversity explicitly faces relationship forms and social realities absent from Jung's original marriage essay. This does not retroactively make Jung's theory inclusive; it shows contemporary Jungian clinicians revising the field.

### 54. Samuels as a Post-Jungian Critical Bridge

[POST-JUNGIAN SYSTEMATISATION]

Andrew Samuels's _Jung and the Post-Jungians_ (1985), especially the chapter "Gender, sex, marriage," is useful as a bridge between primary Jung and later revisions. Samuels reconstructs the contained/container discussion, highlights the translator's warning that the sex assignments can be reversed, and places Jung's marriage theory inside broader post-Jungian debate about gender, anima/animus, and individuation. [Post-Jungian: Samuels, _Jung and the Post-Jungians_, ch. 7]

Samuels is not primary evidence for what Jung said when the CW text is available. His value is diagnostic: he shows where later analytical psychology recognised theoretical tensions, especially Jung's sex-linked assumptions and the tendency of later writers to make marriage itself a more explicit path of individuation than Jung's original essay warrants.

---

## Part IX - Comparison with Attachment and Contemporary Couple Theory

### 55. Jung versus Adult Attachment Theory

[CONTEMPORARY ATTACHMENT THEORY]

Adult attachment theory begins from a different organising problem. In Mikulincer and Shaver's 2007 synthesis, the attachment behavioural system regulates proximity to significant others under conditions of threat, supports safe-haven and secure-base functions, and is organised through mental representations of self and others. Individual differences are commonly described through attachment anxiety and avoidance, with hyperactivating and deactivating regulatory strategies. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, _Attachment in Adulthood_, chs. 1-2, 5-10]

Jung's relationship model is not a security-regulation theory. Its characteristic problems are unconscious identity, projection, collective role, complexes, one-sidedness, and individuation. Dependency is psychologically important, but Jung does not build a behavioural-system model around proximity, safe haven, secure base, and emotion regulation.

The two traditions can describe overlapping episodes from different angles. A partner's withdrawal during conflict might be formulated in attachment theory as a deactivating strategy under attachment threat. A Jungian formulation might ask whether a complex has been constellated, whether a projected image of engulfment or criticism is active, whether introverted one-sidedness is involved, or whether the person is differentiating from a role. These are alternative hypotheses, not translations.

### Psychological relationship ≠ secure attachment

Secure attachment concerns confidence in the availability and responsiveness of attachment figures, flexible proximity seeking, safe haven, secure base, and relatively adaptive regulation. Jung's psychological relationship concerns consciousness, differentiation, projection, individuality, and relation to the unconscious. A couple could plausibly be secure in attachment terms while still containing significant projections, and a psychologically differentiated relationship is not automatically secure by attachment criteria. The sources do not establish a one-to-one mapping.

### Detailed comparison 9: Jungian relationship model and adult attachment theory

| Dimension | Jungian relationship model | Adult attachment theory |
|---|---|---|
| Main organising problem | Unconscious identity, projection, complexes, collective role, one-sidedness, individuation | Regulation of proximity and felt security in relation to attachment figures, especially under threat |
| Developmental basis | Personal history and parental complexes plus archetypal/collective assumptions; life-course individuation | Evolutionarily framed attachment behavioural system shaped by caregiving and relationship experience |
| Partner expectation | Images, complexes, projection, persona expectations, conscious values | Internal working models/representations of self and others |
| Projection | Core explanatory process | Not a core technical mechanism |
| Regulation | Compensation, complex autonomy, conscious/unconscious relation; no formal co-regulation system | Safe haven, secure base, hyperactivation, deactivation, interpersonal regulation |
| Dependency | Can be collective, unconscious, asymmetrical, or consciously lived; not automatically pathological | Attachment needs and proximity seeking are normative across life |
| Autonomy | Individuation and differentiation from collective identity; not individualism | Exploration and autonomy are supported by felt security; autonomy is not detachment |
| Relationship development | Movement from unconscious/collective relation toward more differentiated individual relation; second-half themes | Working models and regulatory strategies can remain stable or change through relationships and experience |
| Change | Recognition/withdrawal of projections, encounter with complexes and unconscious, differentiation, symbolic integration | Increased security, revised expectations, flexible regulation, corrective relational experience |
| Typical evidence tradition | Clinical observation, phenomenology, symbolic/hermeneutic interpretation, comparative cultural material | Developmental and social-personality research, self-report/observational methods, experiments, longitudinal and clinical studies |

The Project attachment volume is the **2007 first edition**, so it should not be treated as a complete summary of evidence published after 2007.

### 56. Projection versus Attachment Expectation

# Nathan Schwartz-Salant: Projection, Relational Field, and Transformation

## Source and Edition Note

This file reconstructs Nathan Schwartz-Salant's *The Mystery of Human Relationship: Alchemy and the Transformation of the Self*. The Project source identifies the book as first published by Routledge in 1998. The Project PDF reproduces the Taylor & Francis e-Library edition issued in 2005. The source is a text-bearing PDF rather than an image-only scan. Its internal printed pagination is recoverable and is used throughout this reconstruction. Extraction is generally strong, although occasional line-break, ligature, header, and figure-caption artifacts occur. Page references below refer to the book's printed pagination, not the PDF viewer's page count.

The book contains a preface, ten chapters, bibliography, and indexes. Its chapter sequence is:

1. Alchemy and transformation in human relationships, pp. 1-17
2. Activating the experience of the field, pp. 18-33
3. Mad parts of sane people, pp. 34-58
4. The dynamics of the interactive field, pp. 59-74
5. The transformative power of the interactive field, pp. 75-91
6. The alchemical view of madness, pp. 92-118
7. The central mystery of the alchemical process, pp. 119-142
8. The alchemical attitude to the transformation of relationship, pp. 143-167
9. Union, death, and the resurrection of the self, pp. 168-208
10. Appreciating the mystery of relationship, pp. 209-221

The book is the Laboratory's principal source for Schwartz-Salant's move from a mainly one-person account of projection and transference toward a model in which the relationship itself becomes an object of observation, imagination, and transformation. It is especially useful because it places three levels in sustained tension: individual psychic structure, interpersonal process, and an imaginal or alchemical account of an emergent "interactive field." It therefore forms a bridge between Jung's work on projection, transference, and the coniunctio and later relational or field-oriented Jungian thinking. It should not be read as empirical validation of an objectively existing interpersonal field. Schwartz-Salant is offering a clinical, phenomenological, symbolic, and in places explicitly speculative model.

[Schwartz-Salant: *The Mystery of Human Relationship*, title/copyright pages, contents, Preface, pp. vii-ix]

## Coverage of This File

This is an exhaustive structured reconstruction rather than a short summary. It follows Schwartz-Salant's argument through the whole book, preserves the chapter order, and then reorganises the material by major concepts so that the source can be queried in two ways: historically, by chapter sequence, and analytically, by topic.

The reconstruction gives particular weight to:

- projection and withdrawal of projection;
- projective identification as distinct from projection;
- the shift from subject-object analysis to an interactive field;
- the "third area," "third thing," "unconscious dyad," and "subtle body" as Schwartz-Salant uses those terms;
- bodily, affective, and imaginal experience in relationship;
- psychotic or "mad" sectors of otherwise non-psychotic personalities;
- the coniunctio-nigredo sequence as his central alchemical model of relational transformation;
- the difference between union and fusion, and between differentiation and emotional distance;
- analyst participation, countertransference, error, repair, and the ethics of concrete behaviour;
- comparison with Jung, Knox, Young-Eisendrath, contemporary Jungian couples work, adult attachment theory, Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy, and Emotion-Focused Therapy;
- cautious reflective applications that preserve uncertainty and do not diagnose real people.

## How to Use This File

For questions about **what Schwartz-Salant himself argues**, prioritise Parts I-VI and the chapter reconstruction. For questions about **how he differs from Jung or other post-Jungians**, use Part VII. For **attachment, Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT), or Emotion-Focused Therapy comparisons**, use Part VIII. For reflective or couple-dynamics questions, use Part IX only after reading Part X's limits and guardrails.

Five epistemic levels are kept separate throughout:

1. **[JUNG PRIMARY]** Jung's own published theory.
2. **[SCHWARTZ-SALANT DEVELOPMENT]** Schwartz-Salant's extension and reorganisation of Jungian ideas.
3. **[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]** later Jungian or psychoanalytic comparison.
4. **[ATTACHMENT / EFCT / EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY]** contemporary clinical frameworks with different theoretical and evidentiary bases.
5. **[LABORATORY SYNTHESIS]** cross-source heuristic integration created for this corpus.

No equivalence should be inferred merely because two frameworks describe similar-looking relational phenomena.

---

# Part I - Schwartz-Salant's Relational Project

## 1. The Psychological Problem of Relationship

Schwartz-Salant starts from a practical observation: persistent relationship conflict is often not adequately understood by asking who is right, who is wrong, or which partner's personal history caused the problem. Ordinary explanations tend to divide an interaction into two separate psychologies. One partner is angry because of one history, the other withdraws because of another. That level of explanation can be valid, but for Schwartz-Salant it is incomplete when the interaction itself develops a compelling atmosphere, logic, and momentum that neither person experiences as voluntarily chosen.

His basic move is therefore from **two separate persons who influence each other** toward **two persons plus an emergent relational process**. He repeatedly describes this additional dimension through terms such as *interactive field*, *third area*, *third thing*, *unconscious dyad*, and, in his alchemical vocabulary, *subtle body*. The terms overlap but are not perfectly interchangeable. "Interactive field" is his broadest clinical term for the jointly engaged process. "Third area" or "third thing" emphasises that the process can be regarded as something neither person simply owns. "Unconscious dyad" usually names a relational form or pair of positions discerned within that field. "Subtle body" belongs to his alchemical account of an intermediate realm between mental and material categories.

The point is not simply that partners affect each other. That would be uncontroversial. His stronger claim is that, in deep relational work, people may experience the relation itself as having an autonomous or partly autonomous organisation. Affects, images, bodily sensations, and shifts of identity can seem to belong to the encounter before they can be confidently allocated to one person. Schwartz-Salant asks the analyst or partner to tolerate this period of uncertainty rather than immediately assigning ownership.

This is why the book cannot be reduced to "Jung for couples." It is an attempt to develop a general psychology of relationship, first from analytic work and then by extension to intimate, institutional, and creative relationships. Alchemy supplies him with a vocabulary for processes that combine, separate, dissolve, die, and reform. It also supplies a way of taking chaos seriously without assuming that every chaotic state is merely meaningless failure.

At the same time, Schwartz-Salant does not entirely reject causal-developmental psychology. He explicitly contrasts scientific, developmental, and alchemical forms of understanding and argues that each catches something the other misses. Developmental explanation is needed to understand trauma, defences, and personal history. Alchemical thinking is used to describe the lived, imaginal, and transformative qualities of an in-between process. The tension between these modes is central to the book.

[Schwartz-Salant: Preface, pp. vii-ix; ch. 1, pp. 1-17; ch. 10, pp. 209-221]

## 2. Why a One-Person Model Is Insufficient for His Purposes

Classical projection theory can explain how a person sees disowned or unconscious content in another. A subject projects, an object carries the projection, and psychological work may involve recognising the content as one's own. Schwartz-Salant accepts this as indispensable. Yet he argues that it can become too simple when applied to intense relationship states.

First, the recipient is not inert. The other person has a psyche, history, body, defences, and unconscious life. What is projected may find what analytical psychology often calls a "hook": something in the other that makes the projection plausible or dynamically effective. Second, projections can induce responses, and projective identification gives a psychoanalytic language for some of this interpersonal induction. Third, once both people are activated, the interaction can become recursive. Each person's state alters the other's perception and behaviour, which in turn alters the first person's state. Fourth, there are clinical moments in which neither person can confidently say whose fear, hatred, emptiness, erotic charge, or deadness is being experienced.

For Schwartz-Salant, a strict one-person formulation can become defensive if it permits the analyst to say, in effect, "this is the patient's projection," without examining the analyst's own participation. The same problem appears in intimate relationships when one partner treats the other as the sole carrier of pathology. A field formulation is intended to interrupt this premature certainty.

However, a field formulation introduces a different danger: it can erase individual responsibility. Schwartz-Salant's strongest clinical examples do not support that erasure. His own countertransference errors matter. Actual abuse matters. An untrustworthy partner's behaviour matters. A field can contain mutual activation without making each person equally responsible for what happens. This distinction is essential for responsible use of the model.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 1, pp. 1-17; ch. 3, pp. 34-58; ch. 5, pp. 75-91; ch. 9, pp. 177-187]

## 3. Jung's Transference and Alchemical Work as Foundation

[JUNG PRIMARY]

Schwartz-Salant's main Jungian foundations are Jung's theory of projection, the dialectical conception of psychotherapy, *The Psychology of the Transference* in CW 16, and the larger alchemical project developed in CW 12 and CW 14. Jung's transference study reads the analyst-patient relationship through the *Rosarium Philosophorum* and its sequence of union, immersion, conjunction, death, ascent, purification, return, and rebirth. Jung's work already treats analysis as more than detached observation: physician and patient are two psychic systems in reciprocal reaction, and the analyst is changed by a genuinely dialectical process.

[Primary Jung: CW 16, "Principles of Practical Psychotherapy" and "The Psychology of the Transference"; CW 12, *Psychology and Alchemy*; CW 14, *Mysterium Coniunctionis*]

[SCHWARTZ-SALANT DEVELOPMENT]

Schwartz-Salant takes the relational implications further. His reading of Jung's transference quaternity is that it implicitly opens a space larger than ego-to-ego interaction, but Jung tends to return to analysing the projections and psychic components carried by the persons. Schwartz-Salant makes the in-between process itself a principal object of attention. His question becomes not only "What is A projecting into B?" but also "What form is taking shape between A and B, how is it experienced, and what does its transformation require?"

The shift is therefore from **alchemy as an analogy for intrapsychic and transferential transformation** toward **alchemy as a model for the transformation of the relational field itself**. That is the book's distinctive contribution.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 2, pp. 18-33; chs. 7-9, pp. 119-208]

## 4. Alchemy as a Relational Method of Seeing

Schwartz-Salant does not treat historical alchemy as chemistry in modern scientific terms. He uses it as a disciplined symbolic tradition for attending to intermediate processes that modern subject-object thinking tends to split apart. Alchemy repeatedly asks what happens when opposites are combined, what new form appears, why a union decomposes, and how a more stable form might emerge from that decomposition.

For him, this is relevant to relationship because partners repeatedly form temporary structures. They become lover and beloved, pursuer and rejecter, accuser and accused, superior and inferior, rescuer and dependent, contemptuous and enraged, fused and distant. These structures can feel real and compelling while they last. Yet they are not necessarily final truths about either person. Alchemical thinking helps Schwartz-Salant imagine these forms as transient configurations within a longer transformation.

The alchemical attitude differs from the urge to solve a conflict immediately. It may require staying with confusion long enough for a pattern to become visible, allowing an old form to die rather than restoring it, and resisting premature interpretation when interpretation itself would be a defence against uncertainty. The *coniunctio-nigredo* sequence becomes his most important formula: a meaningful union or new organisation is followed by loss, darkness, or decomposition; that death may eventually permit a more differentiated and durable form.

This should not be converted into a rigid therapy-stage model. Schwartz-Salant repeatedly presents the sequence as recurrent, spiral-like, and nonlinear. Nor should ordinary relationship distress be romanticised as alchemical work. His own cases show that some states are dangerous, some relationships are not workable, and some forms of fusion or trance require more differentiation rather than deeper immersion.

[Schwartz-Salant: Preface, pp. vii-ix; ch. 1, pp. 1-17; ch. 7, pp. 119-142; ch. 8, pp. 143-167; ch. 9, pp. 168-208]

---

# Part II - Projection, Projective Identification, and Subject-Object Relation

## 5. Projection in Schwartz-Salant's Model

Projection remains a foundation of the entire book. Schwartz-Salant uses the Jungian sense in which an unconscious content is experienced as belonging to an object rather than recognised as part of the subject's own psyche. The subject does not deliberately choose to project. The process is inferred from its effects: disproportionate fascination, certainty, fear, idealisation, contempt, demonisation, or repeated attribution that exceeds what can be established from the other person's actual conduct.

Projection has at least four functions in his relational model.

**First, it distorts.** The projected image can obscure the actual person. Attraction may become devotion to an idealised figure; conflict may become battle with a demonised figure. Withdrawal of projection therefore allows better perception of the real other.

**Second, projection discovers.** A projection may contain psychologically important material. The fact that a perception is projective does not mean it is meaningless or entirely false. The task is to distinguish the subject's contribution from the object's actual qualities.

**Third, projection creates relationship dynamics.** Once a partner is treated as though the projected image were true, the partner may respond to that treatment. A person repeatedly treated as rejecting may become defensive or distant. A person idealised as omnipotent may begin to occupy a superior position. Projection thus enters enactment.

**Fourth, projection can become material for field transformation.** Schwartz-Salant's distinctive move is to ask whether the projected opposites can be experienced not only as properties of A or B but as a dyadic form in the space between them. This does not abolish ownership. It temporarily suspends it to explore the interaction before differentiation is re-established on a new basis.

A key discipline follows: **projection withdrawal does not mean deciding that the other did nothing.** Schwartz-Salant repeatedly preserves the difference between psychic distortion and concrete reality. In one case he explicitly retains the fact that the external partner had genuinely unreliable or ethically questionable qualities even as the analysand's idealising and demonising projections were transformed. In work with trauma he also warns against interpreting actual abuse away as fantasy.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 1, pp. 1-17; ch. 3, pp. 34-58; ch. 5, pp. 75-91]

## 6. Jungian Projection versus Schwartz-Salant's Relational Development

| Dimension | Jungian projection | Schwartz-Salant's development |
|---|---|---|
| Basic process | Unconscious content is experienced in an object rather than recognised as the subject's own. | Retains this process but tracks how projection enters reciprocal interaction and a jointly experienced field. |
| Primary question | What unconscious content of the subject is carried by the object? | What is projected, what is actually present in the other, what is induced or reciprocally activated, and what form is emerging between the participants? |
| Other person | Carrier may provide a real "hook," but analytic emphasis is often on withdrawing projection. | Other is an active participant whose own unconscious and embodied responses alter the process. |
| Therapeutic movement | Recognition and differentiation of subject from object. | Recognition and differentiation plus possible transformation of the relational configuration itself. |
| Risk | Mistaking projection for objective reality. | Same risk, plus the opposite risk of treating a vague field impression as objective proof. |
| Endpoint | More adequate subject-object relation and assimilation of projected content. | More adequate subject-object relation, altered field, greater capacity to move between intimacy and separateness, and increased tolerance of opposites. |

[Primary Jung: CW 7, *The Relations Between the Ego and the Unconscious*; CW 9ii, *Aion*; supporting comparison: C. A. Meier, "Projection, Transference, and the Subject-Object Relation in Psychology"]

[Schwartz-Salant: chs. 1-2, pp. 1-33; ch. 5, pp. 75-91]

## 7. Projective Identification

Schwartz-Salant also draws on the psychoanalytic concept of **projective identification**, especially Klein-derived and object-relations accounts in which projected material is not merely attributed to another but participates in an interpersonal process that may evoke or induce a corresponding state in the recipient. His bibliography and discussion engage writers such as Hanna Segal, James Grotstein, Thomas Ogden, and other relational/object-relations figures.

In his clinical use, projective identification helps describe experiences in which the analyst feels pressured into a role or affect that seems related to the analysand's unconscious organisation. The analyst may feel unusually stupid, hateful, dead, seductive, controlling, helpless, or compelled to speak in a certain way. Those experiences can supply information, but they are never self-validating. The analyst's own complexes, defences, character, fatigue, and desires remain active alternative explanations.

Schwartz-Salant's field model both uses and exceeds projective identification. A projective-identification formulation still usually asks how material is transmitted from one person into another, even if the process is reciprocal. The interactive-field model asks whether some states are more fruitfully held for a time as properties of the relation. In his language, the analyst may move from "this has been put into me" toward "this anger, blankness, or dyad is currently a quality of our field." This move is intended to reduce premature blame and rigid ownership, not to deny that later differentiation is necessary.

One of his most important cautions comes from his own mistake. In a case of severe trauma and dissociation, he interpreted an induced sadomasochistic process too quickly and gave voice to a shocking fantasy. What he thought the field could contain was experienced by the analysand literally and persecutorily. Retrospectively he judged that he had overestimated the stability of the shared imaginal field and underestimated the patient's psychotic or dissociative level. The case becomes a warning against romanticising analyst intuition.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 3, pp. 34-58; ch. 9, especially pp. 177-187]

### Projection versus projective identification

| Question | Projection | Projective identification |
|---|---|---|
| Core operation | A content is unconsciously located in or attributed to another. | A projected content participates in an interpersonal process in which the recipient may be pressured, induced, or recruited into a related state or role. |
| Requires recipient response? | No. The projection can persist even if the recipient does not enact it. | The concept normally concerns an interactive consequence in the recipient. |
| Main Jungian lineage | Jung's projection and subject-object differentiation. | Psychoanalytic/object-relations lineage; Schwartz-Salant adapts it into Jungian field thinking. |
| Clinical evidence | Disproportionate attribution, fascination, certainty, repeated image. | Therapist or partner experiences unusual pressure or state, but alternative explanations must be considered. |
| Risk of misuse | "It is only your projection," which can erase real behaviour. | "You made me feel this," which can convert subjective experience into accusation. |
| Relation to field | Projection can be one element in a field. | Projective identification can help generate or stabilise a field, but the two constructs are not identical. |

[Schwartz-Salant: chs. 1, 3, 5, 9]

## 8. Subject-Object Relation and the Actual Other

[JUNG PRIMARY / MEIER COMPARISON]

C. A. Meier's discussion of projection and transference is a useful classical bridge. He emphasises that projection is initially unconscious, that the carrier often has a "hook" for what is projected, and that analysis involves both recovering the projected content and discovering what actually belongs to the other person. Subject-object differentiation increases as projection is withdrawn. Meier also points to reciprocity: projections can have effects on the receiving person, and transference-countertransference complicates any simple sender-receiver model.

[Post-Jungian supporting source: C. A. Meier, "Projection, Transference, and the Subject-Object Relation in Psychology," especially pp. 21-29 in the Project scan]

[SCHWARTZ-SALANT DEVELOPMENT]

Schwartz-Salant keeps that differentiation but adds a temporary third standpoint. In some encounters, he argues, it is psychologically useful to move through three positions:

1. **subject-object differentiation:** What belongs to me? What belongs to you? What objectively occurred?
2. **field participation:** What affective, bodily, or imaginal pattern is emerging between us when ownership is not forced too quickly?
3. **renewed differentiation:** After the field has been explored, what can each person now recognise, own, refuse, or change?

This sequence matters because field work without renewed differentiation can slide into merger, mystification, or shared delusion. Conversely, differentiation without field participation can become sterile, defensive, or blind to mutual enactment.

[Schwartz-Salant: chs. 2, 4, 5, 8; pp. 18-33, 59-91, 143-167]

## 9. Attraction, Fascination, Idealisation, and Demonisation

Schwartz-Salant treats strong attraction and repulsion as potential signs of psychic activation, not as proof of archetypal destiny. Fascination may indicate that the other person carries projected value, feared shadow, erotic expectation, or an archetypally amplified image. The intensity itself therefore raises a question: **what is being seen, and what is being added by the perceiver's psyche?**

His clinical material repeatedly shows oscillation between idealisation and demonisation. A lover can become beautiful and salvific in one state and witch-like or persecutory in another. A therapist may be experienced as omniscient or malevolent. For Schwartz-Salant, the shift is not resolved simply by choosing which image is true. The work is to recognise the polarity, recover projected contents, investigate actual conduct, and eventually tolerate a more complex other who is neither angel nor demon.

Attraction can also be embodied and field-like. People feel magnetised, enlivened, flooded, or disorganised. Schwartz-Salant uses the coniunctio as a symbolic model for some such states, but he also insists that union contains a dark side. Intensity can carry fusion, incestuous fantasy, domination, psychotic anxiety, or loss of judgement. The more numinous the experience, the greater the need for differentiation and behavioural reality-testing.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 5, pp. 75-91; ch. 7, pp. 119-142; ch. 9, pp. 187-208]

---
# Part III - The Interactive Field and Third Area

## 10. The Interactive Field: Definition and Scope

The **interactive field** is Schwartz-Salant's central relational construct. He uses it to name a domain of experience that arises when two people affect one another consciously and unconsciously and when the resulting process is treated as more than a sum of two independent inner worlds.

A minimal formulation would be:

> two persons remain distinct, yet their encounter generates patterns of affect, image, bodily response, attention, role, and meaning that can be investigated as properties of the relationship as well as properties of each individual.

This definition is deliberately weaker than a metaphysical claim that an independently existing substance literally occupies the space between people. Schwartz-Salant sometimes uses language stronger than this, especially when drawing on alchemy, the collective unconscious, subtle-body traditions, or Jung's more speculative formulations. For Laboratory use, three levels should be distinguished:

- **phenomenological level:** participants actually experience a distinctive relational atmosphere or pattern;
- **clinical-theoretical level:** the therapist treats that pattern as an analytic object because doing so yields useful understanding or change;
- **ontological or metaphysical level:** the field is asserted to exist as an objective or quasi-objective reality beyond the persons.

The first two levels are repeatedly demonstrated by the book's clinical descriptions. The third is an interpretive commitment within Schwartz-Salant's alchemical psychology and should not be presented as established psychological fact.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 2, pp. 18-33; ch. 4, pp. 59-74]

### What enters the field

Across the book, the interactive field may include:

- conscious statements and behaviour;
- transference and countertransference;
- projection and projective identification;
- bodily tension, deadness, heat, constriction, attraction, fear, or movement;
- spontaneous images and fantasies;
- changing experiences of distance and closeness;
- experiences of being mentally blank, flooded, persecuted, or enlivened;
- complementary and opposing roles;
- states of fusion and separation;
- what Schwartz-Salant calls an unconscious dyad;
- symbolic or alchemical forms through which the participants imagine the process.

The field is therefore not merely "emotion in the room." It is his name for an organised relational process that can contain emotion but also symbolic, somatic, and structural dimensions.

### What the field is not

The field is not, by definition:

- proof that two people literally share one mind;
- proof that a particular image corresponds to the other person's unconscious;
- proof of telepathy;
- an excuse to disregard observable conduct;
- a synonym for attachment bond;
- a synonym for an EFCT negative interaction cycle;
- a synonym for countertransference;
- evidence that two partners are equally responsible for an interaction;
- evidence that an intense relationship is psychologically "meant to be."

These exclusions are necessary because Schwartz-Salant's language can become numinous and easily invites over-literalisation.

## 11. From Intersubjectivity to a Field with an Objective Dimension

In chapter 2 Schwartz-Salant reviews nearby psychoanalytic ideas including Winnicott's transitional or potential space, Andre Green's analytic object, Robert Stolorow's intersubjective field, and Thomas Ogden's analytic third. He recognises these as important moves away from a one-person psychology. Yet he sees many of them as remaining primarily within the combined subjectivities of two people.

His own proposal is stronger. He wants the field to include an **objective** dimension in the Jungian sense of an objective psyche or collective unconscious. This does not mean "objective" in the ordinary empirical sense of independently verified fact. Rather, it means a transpersonal layer that is not reducible to either ego's intentions or biography. Alchemical symbols are important precisely because he believes they can give form to such transpersonal relational processes.

This is one of the points at which Schwartz-Salant most clearly departs from a strictly scientific psychology. The move is theoretically coherent inside analytical psychology, because Jung's archetypes and collective unconscious already posit structural factors not reducible to personal experience. But it does not become empirically established merely because a clinical image seems to fit an alchemical motif.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 2, pp. 18-33]

## 12. Interactive Field Map

[LABORATORY SYNTHESIS: heuristic map, not Schwartz-Salant's literal diagram]

```text
person A
   ↕
projection / affect / body / image / behaviour
   ↕
INTERACTIVE FIELD / THIRD AREA
   ↕
projection / affect / body / image / behaviour
   ↕
person B
```

The vertical arrows indicate reciprocal influence, not symmetric causation. One person's behaviour may carry much more causal or moral weight than the other's. The centre names a domain of inquiry, not a literal shared organ or substance.

A more detailed version is:

```text
A's history, complexes, body, expectations
                ↓
        A perceives and acts
                ↓
      observable interaction  ↔  B perceives and acts
                ↑                       ↑
       A's felt response         B's history, complexes,
       to B's response           body, expectations
                ↘               ↙
       emergent atmosphere / dyadic form
                ↕
        images, roles, polarities,
        states of union or non-linking
                ↕
       interpretation and behaviour
                ↕
     either stabilisation of the pattern
     or differentiation / transformation
```

This map should always be read alongside the question: **what objectively happened?** Field language is an additional lens, not a replacement for behavioural description.

## 13. The Third Area / Third Thing

Schwartz-Salant's "third area" is the most concise way of naming what he believes classical projection analysis misses. If person A and person B are the first two terms, the "third" is the relational process that becomes experientially available when neither person's individual psychology is treated as the whole explanation.

The third is constituted through participation and attention. It is not simply there as a static object waiting to be measured. In his clinical descriptions, the participants have to notice the atmosphere, allow bodily and imaginal impressions to emerge, suspend immediate certainty about ownership, and sometimes deliberately imagine affects as existing between them. The field then becomes something both people can be "in" and, at least intermittently, observe.

The paradox is essential: if a person is entirely merged with the field, it cannot be reflected upon. If a person stands completely outside it, the lived process disappears into abstraction. Schwartz-Salant therefore values a double position: **participant and observer**. Alchemy supplies images of containers, vessels, pairs, and transformations that help sustain this double position.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 2, pp. 18-33; ch. 5, pp. 75-91; ch. 10, pp. 216-219]

### Third area versus Winnicottian potential space

Schwartz-Salant explicitly engages Winnicott but does not simply adopt Winnicott's construct. Potential space concerns the intermediate area of experience associated with play, symbolisation, and the transition between subjective and objectively perceived reality. Schwartz-Salant's field is specifically relational and Jungian-alchemical: it can include an unconscious dyad, archetypal pattern, somatic resonance, coniunctio, and transformation of a jointly engaged field.

The concepts overlap in valuing an intermediate domain, but their theoretical lineages and claims differ.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 2, pp. 18-33; supporting source: D. W. Winnicott, *Playing and Reality*, as engaged by Schwartz-Salant]

## 14. The Unconscious Dyad

The **unconscious dyad** is a particularly important sub-construct. It is Schwartz-Salant's way of describing a pair of relational positions or figures that appears to organise the interaction beneath the conscious couple.

A consciously affectionate couple, for example, may be organised at another level by contempt and rage. An analyst and analysand who consciously value cooperation may be caught in a sadist-masochist pair. The dyad is not necessarily assigned permanently to one person. One of Schwartz-Salant's claims is that, when the field becomes sufficiently visible, the participants may discover that either person can occupy either pole. The man who initially appears only as the angry pole may discover his own contempt; the woman who initially appears only as contemptuous may discover her own anger.

This is one reason the field perspective can reduce simplistic blame. It reveals roles as relationally organised and potentially reversible. But reversibility does not mean every role is equally enacted or equally harmful. The dyadic form must still be compared with actual behaviour.

The unconscious dyad is also his bridge to alchemy. Sol and Luna, king and queen, brother and sister, double-headed serpent, hermaphrodite, and other paired images become symbolic representations of relational opposites. In the strongest form of his argument, these are not merely metaphors imposed by an interpreter but images of underlying psychic form. That stronger claim belongs to his Jungian-alchemical theory and should remain labelled as such.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 6, pp. 92-118; ch. 8, pp. 153-167; ch. 10, pp. 212-218]

## 15. Field Phenomenology versus Objective Behavioural Evidence

| Question | Field phenomenology | Objective behavioural evidence |
|---|---|---|
| What is observed? | Atmosphere, bodily resonance, image, felt polarity, shifts in identity or distance. | Words spoken, actions taken, timing, repeated behavioural pattern, records, consequences. |
| Epistemic status | First-person or intersubjective experience; meaningful but interpretive. | More directly observable and potentially corroborable, though still context-dependent. |
| Typical question | "What seems to be happening between us?" | "What did each person actually say or do?" |
| Strength | Captures subtle relational experience that may precede explicit formulation. | Protects against mind-reading, mystification, and rewriting harmful behaviour as symbol. |
| Risk | Treating feeling as fact about the other. | Missing symbolic, embodied, unconscious, or contextual meaning. |
| Best use | Generate hypotheses and deepen phenomenological awareness. | Establish the concrete interaction and test interpretations. |

[LABORATORY SYNTHESIS grounded in Schwartz-Salant: chs. 3-5, 8-10]

---

# Part IV - Embodiment, Image, and Mutual Unconscious Interaction

## 16. Mutual Unconscious Interaction

Schwartz-Salant's model is explicitly reciprocal. Both participants possess unconscious processes; both can project; both can be affected; both can defend; and both can contribute to an emergent dyad. In analysis, this means that the therapist's subjectivity is not noise to be eliminated. It is part of the data and part of the risk.

He uses several languages for reciprocal unconscious influence:

- projection and counter-projection;
- projective identification;
- transference-countertransference;
- unconscious dyad;
- field dynamics;
- somatic or subtle-body resonance;
- imaginal perception.

These should not be treated as one mechanism. Projection is an attributional or externalising process. Projective identification is an interpersonal process in which another may be recruited into a state. Countertransference includes the analyst's response, whether induced, personal, or mixed. Field language is Schwartz-Salant's broader model for the configuration that emerges.

His strongest clinical claim is therefore not "we know what the other unconsciously feels." It is closer to: **in some relational states, each person's unconscious organisation becomes relevant to the other's experience, and the resulting pattern may be easier to understand if the interaction itself is temporarily treated as an object.**

Where he speaks of acausal, subtle-body, or transpersonal effects, the text enters a more speculative register. Those passages belong to the history of Jungian field theory but should not be converted into empirical claims about mind-to-mind transmission.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 2, pp. 18-33; ch. 4, pp. 59-74; ch. 5, pp. 75-91]

## 17. Embodied Relational Experience

The body is not secondary in Schwartz-Salant's account. The analyst may notice tension, contraction, a desire to flee, deadness, heat, attraction, pressure, cardiac sensation, or a shift in the felt texture of space. These bodily events can be treated as clues to the relational process.

His distinction between a **psychic unconscious** and a **somatic unconscious** is important. The psychic mode is associated more with meaning, image, spirit, and mental organisation. The somatic mode is associated more with embodied relational energy, affect, passion, and the lived "space of relations." The alchemical subtle body is his imagined mediator between the two.

The practical implication is that interpretation can become defensive when it outruns the body. An analyst who quickly produces meaning may be escaping an intolerable state of blankness, fear, or non-connection. Schwartz-Salant's clinical method often requires allowing bodily and affective states to be felt before assigning them a conceptual meaning.

This does not make bodily experience infallible. A clenched chest may reflect the analyst's own anxiety, illness, memory, or current stress. A sensation is evidence that something is happening in the experiencing person. It becomes evidence about the relationship or the other person only through further inquiry and corroboration.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 4, especially pp. 67-74; ch. 5, pp. 75-91; ch. 9, pp. 177-187]

### Clinical phenomenology versus metaphysical claim

| Level | Responsible formulation | Overreach to avoid |
|---|---|---|
| Somatic | "I notice tension and an urge to withdraw while we speak." | "Your unconscious is pushing me away." |
| Affective | "The interaction feels unusually dead or angry to me." | "The field objectively contains hatred." |
| Imaginal | "An image of a locked room appeared to me." | "The image reveals what is literally inside you." |
| Relational hypothesis | "Perhaps we are both participating in a pattern of approach and retreat." | "We share one unconscious mind." |
| Alchemical interpretation | "Schwartz-Salant might imagine this as a union-death sequence." | "This proves the relationship is undergoing coniunctio." |

[LABORATORY SYNTHESIS]

## 18. Imagination and Image in the Field

Schwartz-Salant extends Jung's active imagination from the intrapsychic domain toward the relation itself. In classical active imagination, a person attends to spontaneous images or affects and enters a conscious dialogue with them. Schwartz-Salant asks whether a field can be approached similarly: can the relationship, or the dyad imagined within it, be allowed to show its form rather than being immediately explained?

This produces a distinctive clinical stance. An affect may be treated as an imaginal object. A therapist may notice a field of anger without first deciding who owns it. An unconscious pair may be imagined in the space between the participants. An alchemical image may function as a container that helps the analyst stay with an otherwise disorganising state.

The method depends on **as-if** thinking. The field is experienced *as if* it were an object; anger is placed *as if* between the participants; the dyad is treated *as if* it has a life. The usefulness of this method depends on preserving the as-if. Once metaphor is treated as literal fact, the imaginative method becomes suggestive and potentially coercive.

Schwartz-Salant's trauma case in chapter 9 demonstrates the danger. He believed the analysand could receive his imaginal formulation as symbolic material, but she heard it concretely. The failed intervention shows that image work requires assessment of the recipient's capacity for symbolic distance, reality testing, and differentiation.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 5, pp. 75-91; ch. 9, pp. 177-187]

## 19. Atmosphere, Texture, and "Seeing"

The book repeatedly uses perceptual language that exceeds ordinary vision. Schwartz-Salant speaks of "seeing" a form in the field through eyes, body, feeling, or image. He describes space as acquiring texture or spirit rather than remaining an empty Cartesian interval.

Within his model, this is **imaginal perception**. The word "seeing" should not be mistaken for visual hallucination or paranormal certainty. It refers to a cultivated capacity to notice patterned experience that has not yet been formulated in discursive thought. An analyst may "see" a sadistic pair in the sense of apprehending a relational form, not in the sense of observing two literal entities in the room.

This imaginal mode is one of the places where alchemy functions as a perceptual training tradition for Schwartz-Salant. Its images give the clinician a repertoire for recognising union, splitting, death, return, and transformation. But the repertoire also creates confirmation risk: once a clinician expects a coniunctio or nigredo, ambiguous material may be forced into that frame. A disciplined use therefore requires comparison with alternative formulations.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 4, pp. 59-74; ch. 8, pp. 153-167]

## 20. The Subtle Body

"Subtle body" is one of Schwartz-Salant's most historically loaded concepts. He draws on alchemical, Renaissance, and Jungian traditions in which an intermediate medium links mind and body, spirit and matter, or soul and sensory life. He uses this language to give conceptual form to embodied relational experience.

In contemporary terms, the least metaphysical reading is that the subtle body names **the experienced interface of body, affect, image, and relationship**. That reading captures much of his clinical use without asserting a literal intermediate substance.

His stronger alchemical reading treats the subtle body as a real intermediate realm in which psychic and physical qualities are joined and in which relational forms may be perceived. This stronger claim is integral to his worldview, especially in chapters 6-10. It is not established by the clinical material itself.

The subtle body becomes particularly important in his interpretation of the *Rosarium*. The Rebis standing on the moon represents, for him, a mind-body linking and a recoverable capacity to perceive the field. In later stages, passion and spiritual awareness must be integrated without either disembodied spirituality or engulfing fusion destroying the structure.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 4, pp. 67-74; ch. 8, pp. 150-167; ch. 9, pp. 187-208]

---
# Part V - Alchemy, Coniunctio, and Transformation

## 21. Why Alchemy Matters to Schwartz-Salant

Alchemy is not decorative in this book. It is the principal symbolic grammar through which Schwartz-Salant organises relational transformation. He believes modern scientific and developmental models are strongest when they explain causes, structures, and history, but weaker when they try to describe experiences that feel simultaneously psychological, bodily, symbolic, and relational. Alchemy, by contrast, was historically built around transformation of substances through combination, separation, heating, dissolution, blackening, whitening, reddening, death, and renewed form. Schwartz-Salant treats these operations as images for the changing forms of relationship.

Three features of alchemical thought are especially useful to him.

**First, alchemy gives priority to process over static identity.** A substance is not understood only by what it is but by what it becomes when combined with another. Applied psychologically, the question shifts from "What kind of person is this?" toward "What happens when these psychic states meet?"

**Second, alchemy assumes transformation through opposites rather than elimination of one side.** Sol and Luna, spirit and matter, masculine and feminine in historical symbolism, active and receptive, union and separation, life and death all become pairs whose tension is part of the work.

**Third, alchemy treats breakdown as potentially meaningful without automatically calling it success.** Blackening and putrefaction can be stages of transformation, but the work can also fail. Schwartz-Salant uses this to resist the modern impulse to interpret every loss of connection as something that must be immediately repaired.

The resulting model is not a fixed sequence that every couple traverses. Schwartz-Salant repeatedly works recursively. A relationship may form a union, lose it, return to it, discover a more dangerous form of fusion, differentiate, and then repeat the cycle at another level.

[Schwartz-Salant: Preface, pp. vii-ix; ch. 1, pp. 1-17; chs. 6-10, pp. 92-221]

## 22. The Vessel and Containment

The alchemical **vas**, vessel, is one of the most useful relational images in the book. Psychologically, a vessel is a structure able to contain affect and contradiction long enough for transformation rather than immediate discharge to occur.

In analytic work, the frame itself contributes to containment: regular meetings, role boundaries, the rule that difficult material can be spoken and reflected upon, and an analyst whose task includes maintaining a reflective function. In intimate relationships, there is no equivalent professional frame. Commitment, trust, boundaries, and a shared willingness to investigate the interaction have to perform some of the containing work.

For Schwartz-Salant, the field can become a vessel only when the participants are capable of both entering and observing it. A chaotic atmosphere alone is not a vessel. Nor is fusion. Containment requires enough structure that affects can be endured without being automatically acted out, disowned, or projected as facts.

This differs from an **attachment secure base**. A secure base in attachment theory concerns confidence in an available, responsive attachment figure that supports exploration and regulation. Schwartz-Salant's alchemical vessel concerns the capacity of a relation to hold psychic opposites, imaginal material, passion, breakdown, and transformation. The constructs can overlap phenomenologically but derive from different theories.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 4, pp. 59-74; ch. 9, pp. 177-187; ch. 10, pp. 216-220]

## 23. Prima Materia and Chaos

The **prima materia** is the difficult starting material of the opus. Schwartz-Salant refuses to reduce it to one psychological content. It can appear as confusion, contradiction, unbearable affect, bodily agitation, deadness, fused opposites, psychotic anxiety, or what he calls the "mad parts of sane people."

A central distinction is between mere disorder and a chaos that has become psychologically available for work. Chaos is not valuable because it is chaotic. It becomes prima materia when the participants can hold it in awareness, refrain from immediate evacuation or forced explanation, and allow its organisation to be discovered.

The double-headed serpent of the *Mercurial Fountain* becomes Schwartz-Salant's image of annihilating opposites: two states that cannot coexist and therefore flip into each other, such as absolute closeness versus absolute rejection. The lower waters represent Dionysian passion and somatic intensity. He treats the upper dyadic conflict and lower embodied waters as two forms of the same starting problem: mind and body, spirit and passion, or opposing states have not yet found a rhythmic relation.

The practical lesson is not "stay in chaos." It is "do not destroy the information in chaos by prematurely imposing the wrong order." This is especially important in his clinical discussions of dissociation and trauma, where an analyst's need to make sense can become intrusive.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 6, pp. 92-118; ch. 8, pp. 153-156]

## 24. Opposites

Opposites are the structural engine of Schwartz-Salant's alchemical psychology. Yet he does not mean that every difference is an archetypal polarity. The relevant opposites are states that become mutually exclusive, compelling, and dynamically linked.

Examples include:

- closeness versus separation;
- attraction versus repulsion;
- idealisation versus demonisation;
- spirit versus body;
- knowing versus unknowing;
- activity versus receptivity;
- love versus hate;
- domination versus submission;
- connection versus radical non-connection;
- fusion versus individuation;
- stability versus transformation.

In a psychotic or "mad" sector, the opposites can annihilate rather than complement each other. One state erases memory of the other. When a partner feels connected, disconnection seems impossible; when disconnected, previous love may feel unreal. The alchemical task is to create a structure in which both states can be remembered and eventually held without catastrophic switching.

Schwartz-Salant's treatment of historically gendered opposites requires care. Sol/Luna, king/queen, male/female, and related images are part of the source tradition. They should be retained as historical-symbolic language rather than silently translated into contemporary sex or gender identities. Schwartz-Salant himself sometimes uses gendered cultural generalisations that should not be treated as universal psychological law.

[Schwartz-Salant: chs. 4, 6-9]

## 25. Coniunctio

The **coniunctio**, the conjunction or union of opposites, is central but easily misunderstood.

[JUNG PRIMARY]

In Jung's alchemical psychology, coniunctio names a symbolic union of opposites and is elaborated through alchemical imagery, especially in *The Psychology of the Transference* and *Mysterium Coniunctionis*. It is not simply a description of romantic love. Jung's use ranges across intrapsychic integration, transference, symbolic conjunction, and the relation of psychic opposites.

[Primary Jung: CW 16, "The Psychology of the Transference"; CW 14, *Mysterium Coniunctionis*]

[SCHWARTZ-SALANT DEVELOPMENT]

Schwartz-Salant explicitly relationalises the coniunctio. A field may enter a union state in which two people feel unusually connected, alive, erotically charged, mutually permeable, or organised by a shared symbolic pattern. He also includes negative union: hatred, envy, sadomasochistic coupling, or persecutory engagement can bind people as strongly as affection.

The decisive point is that **union is not the goal by itself**. The union must die. If partners idealise the coniunctio and try to preserve it, it becomes fusion. The creative sequence is union followed by nigredo, then possible reorganisation. The capacity to remember the union during disconnection, and to remember difference during union, gradually creates a more stable structure.

Schwartz-Salant therefore rejects the common romantic equation:

```text
intensity = union = destiny = psychological truth
```

His own alchemical model is closer to:

```text
union
→ exposure of previously split material
→ loss / death / non-connection
→ differentiation and reorganisation
→ possible renewed union at another level
```

### Coniunctio is not romantic fusion

| Coniunctio in Schwartz-Salant's model | Romantic fusion |
|---|---|
| Symbolic and processual union of opposites. | Wish to erase difference in permanent togetherness. |
| Followed by death, separation, or nigredo. | Treats separation as failure or betrayal. |
| Can reveal shadow and conflict. | Often idealises the partner and relationship. |
| Requires differentiation and capacity to survive loss of union. | Depends on maintaining identification and sameness. |
| May be positive or negative in felt tone. | Usually imagined as positive emotional union. |
| Does not prescribe staying together. | Often interpreted as proof the relationship should continue. |

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 7, pp. 119-142; chs. 8-9, pp. 143-208]

### Coniunctio is not secure attachment

Secure attachment concerns availability, responsiveness, confidence in support, and the regulation of attachment threat. Coniunctio belongs to a symbolic-alchemical psychology of opposites and transformation. A secure relationship can contain symbolic conjunctions; a coniunctio experience can occur in an insecure or even destructive relationship. One does not validate the other.

[Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1-3, 7, 10; Schwartz-Salant: chs. 7-10]

## 26. Nigredo, Mortificatio, Putrefaction, and Death of Form

Schwartz-Salant gives the **nigredo**, or blackening, exceptional weight. It is the experience in which a union or established structure dies. In relationship this may appear as despair, deadness, non-connection, loss of meaning, withdrawal, anger, or a sense that two people live in parallel universes.

In chapter 9 he uses the sixth and seventh *Rosarium* woodcuts to describe two increasingly severe forms. The sixth depicts the conjoined king and queen dead in a coffin, a "putrefying conception": the union has died but the dead form remains together. The seventh depicts the soul leaving the body, corresponding in his clinical reading to severe dissociation, absence of linking, and states in which meaningful contact is almost impossible.

The associated alchemical operation **mortificatio** is therefore relevant as symbolic death, not as literal harm. **Putrefaction** describes the decomposition of an old organisation. **Separatio** is relevant more broadly as differentiation or division of fused elements, although Schwartz-Salant's book is more consistently organised around coniunctio, nigredo, and the *Rosarium* sequence than around a fixed taxonomy of operations.

The important guardrail is that **depression or suffering by itself is not a creative nigredo**. Schwartz-Salant says this explicitly in substance: the transformative nigredo follows a meaningful union and participates in a process of structural change. Ordinary despair, abuse, neglect, or chronic misery should not be elevated into spiritual transformation merely by naming it nigredo.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 8, pp. 160-167; ch. 9, pp. 168-187; ch. 10, pp. 219-221]

## 27. The Coniunctio-Nigredo Sequence

The coniunctio-nigredo sequence is the closest thing the book has to a governing transformation model.

```text
1. A relational union or new order forms.
2. The union constellates material previously split off or defended against.
3. The union dies or becomes unavailable.
4. Participants encounter grief, rage, blankness, shame, non-linking, or disorganisation.
5. Attempts to restore the old union can intensify the problem.
6. If the death can be endured without forced repair, new differentiation becomes possible.
7. A more stable field or self-structure may emerge.
8. The sequence can recur at another level.
```

This sequence is not a universal law of couples. It is Schwartz-Salant's alchemical model. It is best treated as a hypothesis-generating pattern: **does this relationship repeatedly move from intense connection to a catastrophic loss of connection, and does the attempt to prevent the loss make the cycle worse?**

Where the pattern fits, it can add something to a purely behavioural account by giving psychological meaning to the loss of a previously achieved form. Where it does not fit, applying it can mystify ordinary incompatibility or avoidable harm.

[Schwartz-Salant: chs. 7-10]

## 28. Fusion and Differentiation

Fusion is one of the book's recurrent dangers. In fusion, the distinction between subject and object becomes unstable in a way that reduces reflective freedom. The partner may be experienced as the source of one's worth, safety, persecution, or identity. Separation then feels like annihilation rather than ordinary difference.

Schwartz-Salant's alchemical model does not seek a permanently separated self. It seeks a rhythm in which intimacy can intensify without destroying differentiation and differentiation can occur without emotional abandonment.

This distinction can be put sharply:

- **fusion is not intimacy** because merger can eliminate the actual other;
- **differentiation is not distance** because a differentiated person can remain emotionally engaged;
- **separation is not rejection** because a relationship can survive periods in which partners are psychologically distinct;
- **union is not loss of self** because mature conjunction requires the capacity to return to one's own centre.

The Attis-Cybele material in chapter 7 represents a dark, destructive form of union in which separation and attachment become mutually catastrophic. Schwartz-Salant uses it to expose the danger in idealising all conjunction. He also warns against literalising the gendered myth into a theory in which a dangerous woman engulfs a man. The deeper pattern concerns fusion and individuation in either person.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 7, pp. 119-142]

## 29. The Axiom of Maria and the Axiom of Ostanes

Schwartz-Salant uses two alchemical axioms as structural maps.

The **Axiom of Maria** gives a qualitative sequence in which one becomes two, two becomes three, and from the third comes a transformed fourth or renewed unity. He interprets this relationally:

- initial undifferentiated chaos;
- differentiation of two positions or opposites;
- creation of a third field in which the opposites can be related;
- emergence of a new form that contains them differently.

The **Axiom of Ostanes** gives another sequence: one nature delights in another, one nature conquers another, one nature dominates another. Schwartz-Salant draws on historical commentary to read this as mixture, transformation, and stabilisation. In clinical work, he uses it to think about how two states first meet, then one disrupts the equilibrium, and finally a new structure becomes sufficiently stable to support further transformation.

Neither axiom should be treated as a validated process model. Their value in the book is symbolic and heuristic. They help Schwartz-Salant avoid a static concept of integration and describe the way a new relational order may need both encounter and stabilisation.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 4, pp. 59-74; ch. 8, pp. 143-150]

## 30. The Rosarium Philosophorum as a Relationship Sequence

Chapter 8 and especially chapter 9 give the *Rosarium Philosophorum* a central role. Schwartz-Salant reads its first ten woodcuts as particularly suitable for an interactive-field interpretation because the sequence repeatedly depicts a couple and culminates in the Rebis, a hermaphroditic figure.

His broad reading is:

- **Mercurial Fountain:** abstract representation of the starting field, divided yet flowing, with volatile opposites and embodied waters.
- **Left-hand Contact:** first contact, ambivalence, dangerous or illicit attraction, and the beginning of mutual engagement.
- **Naked Truth:** more direct exposure of desire and shadow, with less defensive covering.
- **Immersion in the Bath:** entry into the shared waters of emotion, body, and relational mystery.
- **Conjunction:** union of the pair under the water, an unconscious or partly unconscious coniunctio.
- **Coffin / Putrefying Conception:** death of the achieved union; old structure decomposes.
- **Extraction / Impregnation of the Soul:** severe dissociation or non-linking; the previous relation cannot be consciously accessed.
- **Falling Dew:** renewal begins; a new linking factor descends.
- **Return of the Soul:** connection and embodied relatedness become possible again.
- **Rebis:** a more stable connecting structure, which Schwartz-Salant describes in relational terms as kinship and a recoverable field.

He then reads the second set of ten images as a **rubedo** phase in which passion, spirit, embodiment, and durability are worked further. Fermentation brings renewed life and passion. Later images repeatedly sacrifice and reconstruct the relation between solar-spiritual and lunar-embodied modes. The final Resurrection represents a more individual and orienting self rather than only a shared linking structure.

This distinction between Rebis and Resurrection matters. The field may help create a connecting structure, but individuation cannot stop with a shared self. The final symbolic emphasis returns to a single resurrected figure: relationship contributes to the creation of a self that can stand as an individual centre while remaining related.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 8, pp. 150-167; ch. 9, pp. 168-208]

## 31. Albedo and Rubedo

Schwartz-Salant uses **albedo** and **rubedo** to distinguish two broad achievements.

The albedo or "White Stone" represents a relatively stable mind-body linkage and the ability to recover a sense of self and relation after abandonment fear or psychic death. Yet this structure is "watery": passion can still dissolve it.

The rubedo adds "redness," life, blood, passion, and the challenge of embodiment in the ordinary world. A person who can maintain spiritual meaning only by avoiding erotic conflict, aggression, dependence, or bodily life has not achieved the integration Schwartz-Salant associates with rubedo. Conversely, passion without a containing structure can regress into fusion or acting out.

The trajectory is therefore not from body to spirit but toward a more difficult integration of both. This is a major reason he considers intimate relationship important to individuation: the other person frustrates the fantasy of spiritual integration achieved in isolation.

[Schwartz-Salant: ch. 8, pp. 150-152; ch. 9, pp. 187-208]

## 32. Passion and Erotic Energy

Passion is not treated as a symptom to be eliminated. Schwartz-Salant believes modern psychological approaches often domesticate erotic intensity by translating it too quickly into developmental need, defence, or symptom. Alchemy, in his view, takes passion seriously as a transformative fire.

Yet he is equally clear that passion is dangerous. It can destroy judgement, intensify fusion, produce literalisation of fantasy, activate sadomasochistic dynamics, and destabilise hard-won self-structure. The task is therefore neither repression nor surrender but containment and transformation.

Erotic energy in the field can also change positions of power and activity. Schwartz-Salant notes role reversals in the rubedo imagery and uses them to imagine a relation in which each person can be active and receptive, penetrating and penetrated in symbolic terms. Contemporary readers should preserve the historical imagery without assuming fixed sex-linked psychological roles.

The decisive criterion is whether passion contributes to greater capacity for reality, differentiation, and relatedness or instead narrows the participants into compulsion, secrecy, domination, or loss of self.
