Curiosity is the active antidote to projection in the book. Projection says, "I already know what you are and why you did it." Curiosity says, "I have an interpretation, but I may not know enough." That does not require abandoning judgment. It requires delaying finality long enough to encounter data from the partner. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 4]

Curiosity is especially important after disillusionment because negative certainty can become self-sealing. Every new act is read as further evidence of the existing image. Questions interrupt that closure. A partner can discover that silence meant overload rather than contempt, that criticism concealed fear, or that apparent indifference reflected a different way of organising attention. Equally, curiosity may confirm that the partner really does hold a value or intention the questioner finds unacceptable. Dialogue is therefore a method for reducing premature certainty, not a method for making every interpretation benign.

### 30. Dialogue and Uncertainty

The "mindful gap" requires the capacity to remain with "I do not fully know." This uncertainty is not indecision about one's own experience. A person may know very clearly, "I am angry," "I do not consent," or "I cannot live with this arrangement" while remaining uncertain about the partner's motive or internal state.

Uncertainty protects otherness because the other is never fully reducible to one's model. Long familiarity does not abolish this. In Young-Eisendrath's account, mature intimacy combines familiarity and mystery. The beloved is known enough to be particular and trusted, but not known so completely that surprise disappears. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 4-5]

### 31. Listening

Listening in dialogue is not passive silence. It requires temporary suspension of rebuttal, active paraphrase, emotional regulation, and willingness to discover that one's first interpretation was incomplete. The listener tries to receive the other's account without immediately replacing it with a self-defensive account. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 4]

This listening is neither submission nor approval. The fourth step of responding makes that explicit. The listener returns as a subject. The capacity to hear another without disappearing is part of differentiation.

### 32. Voice

Dialogue without voice would reproduce hierarchy. Young-Eisendrath's first skill, speaking for oneself, gives each partner ownership of a perspective. The speaker names personal experience, desire, fear, grievance, or boundary without presenting an inference about the other as incontestable fact. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 4]

Voice also means that the partner who is more articulate, psychologically sophisticated, financially powerful, or confident should not automatically become the narrator of the relationship. Equality requires that both accounts be available. A technically polished dialogue in which one person cannot safely disagree would not satisfy the underlying model.

### 33. Difference and Irreducible Difference

Difference is not a failure of love. It is the condition that makes an equal relationship psychologically demanding. If two partners were effectively one mind, there would be no need for dialogue, negotiation, or witnessing. The mature relationship exists because separateness remains. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 4-6]

Some differences can be integrated through compromise or changed understanding. Some persist. The source supports the idea that the partner remains partly other and that mature relationship requires tolerating this. It does not support the fantasy of total transparency.

Irreducible difference also sets a boundary on the spiritualisation of relationship. A person may become less projective and more compassionate and still conclude that a core difference in values, sexuality, geography, children, money, religion, or life purpose makes the partnership unworkable. Better recognition can clarify incompatibility rather than dissolve it.

### 34. Feelings, Aggression, and the Conditions for Dialogue

Young-Eisendrath distinguishes feeling an emotion from expressing it immediately. Mindfulness creates a choice about how and when emotion is communicated. This is especially important for aggression. In chapter 3 she distinguishes anger as potentially informative boundary-setting from aggressive conduct that attacks, humiliates, dominates, or withdraws destructively. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 3]

Containment, one of the three Cs, means not treating every impulse as a mandate for action. It is the capacity to prevent intense internal states from automatically becoming interpersonal harm. At the same time, suppression is not the goal. If people hide their needs to preserve apparent harmony, the relationship becomes less truthful and differentiation is weakened.

Dialogue therefore presupposes a minimum capacity for regulation. When activation is too high, Young-Eisendrath recommends stepping away with the intention to return rather than forcing immediate resolution. A pause can protect dialogue; silent punishment is different. The distinction depends on whether distance is used to regulate and resume contact or to control and abandon contact.

### 35. Failed Dialogue

Dialogue can fail for several reasons supported by the source:

- one partner cannot or will not regulate aggression enough to listen;
- paraphrasing is used as performance rather than genuine contact;
- curiosity is replaced by interrogation;
- one partner assumes the expert or diagnostic role;
- the conversation is organised around contempt;
- the speaker disguises accusation as self-expression;
- the listener equates understanding with capitulation and therefore refuses to understand;
- the partners are too activated to keep the mindful gap;
- a serious betrayal or violence has damaged trust beyond what dialogue can repair;
- an actual incompatibility remains after the meanings are clarified. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3-5]

Failure is therefore not proof that the method was performed incorrectly. Sometimes the relational conditions are not present. Sometimes the discovery produced by dialogue is precisely that there is no mutually acceptable solution.

## Part VI - Gender, Power and Relational Equality

### 36. Power

Young-Eisendrath's account of power is dispersed rather than systematic. She discusses hierarchy, control, decision-making, money, gender roles, contempt, dependency, spiritual authority, and the temptation to occupy a superior position. Her central relational objection is that equality fails when one person's subjectivity is automatically privileged over the other's. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1, 5-7]

Power enters even ordinary intimacy because partners affect access to money, sex, housing, caregiving, social status, information, emotional reassurance, and shared decisions. Young-Eisendrath recognises that money is especially linked to freedom and power. She also argues that a spiritual teacher or other authority who receives devotion without reciprocal vulnerability does not exemplify the equal mutual love she is describing. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 5]

The book is less developed on coercive control and structural inequality than contemporary abuse and power frameworks. Its vocabulary of mutuality should therefore never be used to erase a severe asymmetry of threat, resources, or freedom. That is a Laboratory guardrail, not a claim that the source offers a full coercive-control model.

### 37. Equality and Power

Psychological equality requires more than declaring two people equal. At a minimum, the book implies that each person needs meaningful voice, recognised agency, the ability to disagree, and some real capacity to participate in decisions that shape common life. A relationship in which one partner is formally called an equal but is punished for dissent would not meet the model's substantive requirements.

Equality also requires limits on interpretive power. The person with more psychological vocabulary cannot legitimately diagnose the partner from above. Chapter 2 is explicit that equals are not in a position to diagnose each other. This is a significant form of relational equality because psychological explanations can otherwise become instruments of rank. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 2]

### 38. Gender

Gender is historically central to Young-Eisendrath's project because the modern possibility of love between equals depends, in her account, on the weakening of prescribed male-female hierarchy. She contrasts contemporary personal love with older arrangements in which marital roles were more clearly assigned through gender, law, custom, and economic dependence. The gain is freedom and recognition; the cost is that partners must negotiate what hierarchy once settled. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1, 6-7]

The book does not, however, leave historical gender concepts entirely behind. Chapter 3 includes claims about embodiment, sex, gender development, and contra-sexual psychological patterns that retain elements of traditional Jungian and psychoanalytic thinking. Chapter 7 also makes claims about the psychological importance of mother and father images and about children's internal representation of two parents that should be treated as historically and theoretically situated rather than as settled contemporary evidence.

The source does acknowledge same-sex couples and people who identify in other ways, and it discusses same-sex pair bonds. Yet it does not provide a comprehensive queer or gender-diverse relational theory. Its core equality principles can be analytically distinguished from some of its more historically specific gender assumptions, but this file does not attribute that later generalisation back to the author as if she had fully developed it.

### 39. Anima / Animus Revision

Young-Eisendrath is a post-Jungian writer with a long history of feminist revision of Jungian psychology, but *Love between Equals* is not a sustained technical critique of anima and animus. The book uses Jungian ideas of complexes, projection, and psychological otherness more centrally than it uses the classical binary anima/animus schema. Therefore a careful reconstruction must avoid inventing a comprehensive 2019 anima/animus revision that the source does not provide.

#### Primary Jung baseline

Jung's CW 17 §338 gives the classical historical formulation: the man carries an unconscious image of woman called anima, while the woman carries an image of men called animus, and these images are projected onto beloved persons and contribute to attraction and aversion. The paragraph also makes strongly gender-essentialist claims about anima as erotic-emotional and animus as rationalising. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 17, §338]

Young-Eisendrath's relational model departs from this baseline in practice. She gives both partners the same fundamental tasks: recognise projection, become mindful of subjective contribution, speak for oneself, listen, tolerate difference, and encounter the actual other. Equality is not organised through complementary male/female psychic functions. The result is a more symmetrical theory of **subjecthood** even though the book still retains some historically gendered formulations elsewhere. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1-7]

### 40. Equality and Heterosexual Tradition

The book's argument depends partly on a historical contrast between traditional heterosexual marriage and modern chosen partnership. Older arrangements could provide stable role definitions while embedding legal, economic, sexual, and gender hierarchy. Modern equality removes some of those constraints but requires repeated negotiation of tasks, money, caregiving, sex, work, and authority. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1, 6-7]

Young-Eisendrath sometimes speaks in a heterosexual marital idiom even while widening the model. This should remain visible. The source is not a neutral social history of marriage and should not be treated as one. Its historical argument serves a psychological thesis: equality is difficult partly because there is no longer an accepted superior role that resolves ordinary differences.

### 41. Same-Sex and Gender-Diverse Relationships

The source directly acknowledges same-sex relationships and discusses same-sex pair bonding. It also comments on same-sex relational dynamics from the author's clinical perspective. These passages establish that the model is not intended exclusively for heterosexual couples. They do **not** establish a comprehensive theory of same-sex or gender-diverse relationships. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3-4, 7]

Some of the author's generalisations, especially about lesbian relationships and merger, are based on clinical observation and should be treated cautiously. They are neither necessary to the core equality model nor sufficient as contemporary population-level evidence. The portable corpus should preserve them as source formulations without universalising them.

## Part VII - Relationship as Developmental / Spiritual Path

### 42. What Does "Spiritual Path" Mean?

The subtitle can easily be over-read. In Young-Eisendrath's central relational argument, a relationship becomes a spiritual path when it functions as a repeated discipline of confronting self-centred fantasy, recognising projection, tolerating imperfection, reducing harm, cultivating compassion, and learning to encounter a person who cannot be controlled into sameness. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 2, 5-6]

The developmental work is not primarily about achieving bliss. It involves accepting the ordinary frustration of depending on another imperfect person. The partner becomes a powerful occasion for seeing one's own desire, aggression, fear, idealisation, entitlement, shame, and defensive certainty. This can increase self-knowledge while also increasing other-knowledge.

Young-Eisendrath draws explicitly on Buddhism, especially interdependence, non-harming, mindfulness, equanimity, and the First Noble Truth of dukkha. She uses these to recast relationship difficulty as part of human imperfection rather than proof that the wrong partner has necessarily been chosen. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 2, 6]

### 43. Spiritual versus Metaphysical Claims

The relationship-as-practice thesis should be distinguished from metaphysical destiny. Young-Eisendrath does not support the inference that intense attraction proves two people are soulmates or that a relationship must be preserved because it has spiritual significance. She explicitly questions partner-choice destiny narratives and presents true love as something developed through practice rather than simply found in a predestined other. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 2]

The book also contains Buddhist doctrinal and cosmological material, including statements concerning karma and rebirth. Those are source-level spiritual claims, not empirical proof of the relational model. A reader can reconstruct the psychological discipline of mindfulness, compassion, and non-harming without treating the book as evidence for a metaphysical system.

Thus:

**spiritual path ≠ soulmate destiny ≠ supernatural proof ≠ obligation to remain together.**

### 44. Love as Practice

True love in this book is a practice because its central capacities are repeated rather than possessed once and for all. Partners repeatedly have to notice projection, return to curiosity, tell the truth without aggression, regulate impulses, inquire into the other, negotiate responsibilities, and make room for changing needs. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3-6]

The practice includes the three Cs of chapter 3:

- **Commitment:** valuing and protecting the relationship as something more than momentary preference or a series of trades.
- **Constraint:** accepting the limitations that come with embodiment, choice, circumstance, and a particular committed relationship rather than imagining limitless alternatives.
- **Containment:** holding intense impulses and emotional states without automatically converting them into harmful behaviour.

Young-Eisendrath sees these constraints as creating freedom rather than merely reducing it. By accepting that no partner can be everything, the couple can invest in knowing the actual person rather than perpetually comparing reality with fantasy.

### 45. Love versus Romantic Infatuation

Infatuation is organised by intensity, idealisation, projection, novelty, desire, and the fantasy of unusually complete fit. Mature true love retains desire but adds realistic knowledge, reciprocal witnessing, tolerance of limits, deliberate commitment, and the capacity to remain curious after familiarity has replaced novelty. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1-2, 5-6]

The contrast is developmental, not contemptuous. Young-Eisendrath does not argue that romance is worthless. Falling in love can open the possibility of relationship. The error is to treat its perceptual conditions as the permanent standard of love.

### 46. Love versus Fusion

Love requires separateness in this model because there must be another person to love. Fusion reduces the other to sameness or to a function of the self. Mature love therefore does not mean maximising distance; it means maintaining enough differentiation for genuine recognition to occur. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 4-6]

This makes mature dependence possible. The more clearly the partner is recognised as not under one's control, the more vulnerable dependence becomes. That vulnerability is not a defect in the model. It is one of the costs of loving a real person rather than an internal image.

### 47. Relationship Development versus Relationship Longevity

Young-Eisendrath strongly values commitment and long-term practice, but development should not be equated with duration. A long relationship can remain organised by domination, contempt, projection, or deadened coexistence. A relationship can also become more differentiated and truthful and then end because an incompatibility is recognised. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3, 5, 7; Laboratory guardrail]

Longevity may provide more opportunities for the work she describes, but time itself does not produce maturity. The relevant variables are the quality of recognition, responsibility, dialogue, differentiation, and mutuality.

## Part VIII - Young-Eisendrath in Relation to Jung and Other Post-Jungians

### 48. Young-Eisendrath versus Jung on Psychological Relationship

The closest primary Jung anchor is "Marriage as a Psychological Relationship" in CW 17. Jung begins from a demanding premise: a psychological relationship requires consciousness. At CW 17 §326 he states that relationship requires distinction between self and other; where unconscious identity prevails, there is no psychological relationship in the full sense. He then argues that young partners necessarily bring large unconscious areas into marriage, so mate choice and early union are strongly shaped by unconscious motivation. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 17, §§324-330]

Jung's model contains a developmental movement that clearly anticipates part of Young-Eisendrath's problem. In CW 17 §331, he argues that an individual psychological relationship becomes possible only as unconscious motivations are recognised and original identity is broken down. In §331b, he describes midlife disunity being projected onto the partner. In §§337-340 he describes the early harmony of marriage as substantially supported by projected archetypal images, particularly anima and animus. The crisis of those projections can therefore become a condition for consciousness. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 17, §§331-340]

Young-Eisendrath **inherits** from Jung:

- the idea that unconscious identity limits genuine relationship;
- the role of projection in attraction and conflict;
- the developmental importance of withdrawing projections;
- the claim that psychological relationship requires differentiation;
- the idea that intimate relationship can become a setting for individuation and self-knowledge.

She **revises and extends** Jung by centring equal adult subjecthood, reciprocal witnessing, dialogue, mindfulness, practical negotiation, and symmetrical responsibility for psychological self-examination. Her model is less concerned with the contained/container polarity and far less dependent on the classical anima/animus binary. She asks not only whether projection is withdrawn but whether two people can maintain a dialogical relationship with the actual other after projection becomes more visible. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1-7]

She also makes a historical correction to Jung's marital world. Jung's essay presupposes early twentieth-century heterosexual marriage and contains assumptions about men, women, instinct, development, and gender roles that are not equivalent to a contemporary theory of egalitarian partnership. Young-Eisendrath's key modern problem is precisely what happens when hierarchical gender scripts no longer legitimately decide the relationship.

### 49. Individuation in Relationship

Jung defines individuation in CW 7 §266 as becoming an individual and coming to selfhood. At §267 he distinguishes it from individualism: individuation develops the unique configuration of a person without implying egoistic rejection of collective obligation. This is important for reading Young-Eisendrath. Her differentiation should not be translated into a cult of independence. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 7, §§266-269]

For Young-Eisendrath, intimate relationship can contribute to individuation because the actual other frustrates the fantasy that reality should conform to the self. Projection becomes visible, disowned reactions return to the subject, and the person has to articulate an individual standpoint rather than merge with the couple. The partner is therefore both a source of attachment and a limit on narcissistic omnipotence. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1, 4-6]

The model is relational but not couple-centred in the sense that individuation depends on staying with one partner. The developmental value lies in what becomes conscious, not in preserving a particular outcome. A separation could, in principle, be more individuating than continued fusion or domination. Conversely, invoking individuation to justify emotional disengagement would confuse Jung's concept with individualism.

### 50. Individuation versus Independence

Jung's own distinction between individuation and individualism provides a useful control. Individuation is not the maximisation of private preference. It is development of the particular person in a way that can improve rather than abolish relationship to collective life. Young-Eisendrath's mature dependence fits this better than a self-sufficiency ideal. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 7, §§267-268]

Thus a person may be highly individuated and deeply dependent on loved others. The issue is whether dependence coexists with consciousness, agency, and responsibility. Neither Jung nor Young-Eisendrath gives a basis for equating psychological development with needing fewer people.

### 51. Actual Other versus Archetypal Other

Jung's projection model often asks what unconscious image has been constellated through the external person. In the marriage essay, anima and animus are major examples. Young-Eisendrath retains the question but shifts emphasis toward what happens after one discovers that the external person is not identical with the archetypal or personal image. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 17, §§337-340; Young-Eisendrath, chs. 1-2, 4]

The "actual other" is therefore not the opposite of archetypal depth. It is a guard against allowing archetypal interpretation to erase the person's independent reality. One can ask what an attraction symbolises and still ask what this particular person actually values, chooses, refuses, fears, and does. In a couple context, the second question prevents depth psychology from becoming a sophisticated form of mind-reading.

### 52. Projection Withdrawal and Love

Jung's classical language can sometimes be read as if relationship improves in proportion to the amount of projection withdrawn. Young-Eisendrath adds a necessary relational complication. Withdrawing projection creates the **possibility** of knowing the partner, but knowing the partner requires positive practices: listening, curiosity, truth-telling, tolerance of difference, responsibility, and care. Projection withdrawal alone is not love. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 4-6]

This is one of the file's central post-Jungian developments. A person could become intellectually aware that an image is projected and still remain contemptuous, avoidant, controlling, or uninterested in the partner. Mature relationship therefore requires more than intrapsychic correction.

### 53. Self-Knowledge and Other-Knowledge

Young-Eisendrath treats self-knowledge and other-knowledge as mutually corrective. Greater awareness of projection improves the chance of knowing the other. Greater exposure to the other's difference reveals one's own assumptions. Dialogue makes this reciprocal movement explicit. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 2, 4]

Jung supplies a parallel in CW 16's dialectical conception of psychotherapy. At CW 16 §1 he describes the therapeutic relation as reciprocal interaction between two psychic systems. At §§2 and 7-11 he rejects a superior stance in which the therapist assumes complete knowledge of the other and instead emphasises a dialectical encounter in which each participant is affected. The analytic relationship is not the same as couple equality, but it provides a primary Jung precedent for treating genuine psychological encounter as reciprocal rather than one-sided expertise. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 16, §§1-11]

### 54. Young-Eisendrath versus Schwartz-Salant

Nathan Schwartz-Salant's *The Mystery of Human Relationship* is also post-Jungian and relational, but it theorises the relationship through a different centre of gravity. Schwartz-Salant develops the **interactive field** or "third area" between people. He is interested in how chaotic or "mad" states emerge in and through the field and how alchemical symbolism can illuminate transformation. He explicitly argues that the mystery of relationship lies less in simply deciding who projects what onto whom than in entering and experiencing the in-between field that affects both participants. [Post-Jungian comparison: Schwartz-Salant, *The Mystery of Human Relationship*, Preface; chs. 2-5]

Young-Eisendrath's centre of gravity is more interpersonal and procedural. Her recurring questions are whether partners are equal, whether each is being treated as a subject, whether projection is obscuring the actual other, whether differentiation is possible, and whether dialogue can sustain curiosity under conflict. She occasionally speaks of a relational field, but *Love between Equals* does not build the same alchemical field theory.

The difference can be summarised:

- **Schwartz-Salant:** the emergent third, field states, chaos, alchemical transformation, reciprocal unconscious influence.
- **Young-Eisendrath:** equal subjecthood, projection and idealisation, mindfulness, witnessing, differentiation, dialogue, negotiated adult mutuality.

They overlap in refusing simple blame and in treating relationship as transformative. They should not be merged. An interactive field is not simply a dialogue, and a dialogue is not a synonym for the field.

### 55. Dialogue versus Relational Field

Dialogue is an intentional practice carried out by identifiable subjects. The interactive field is a theoretical account of emergent relational phenomena that may exceed the intentions and conscious knowledge of either person. [Post-Jungian comparison: Schwartz-Salant, Preface; Young-Eisendrath, ch. 4]

A dialogue can become one way of observing or altering a field, but the concepts operate at different explanatory levels. Young-Eisendrath asks partners to speak, listen, paraphrase, inquire, and respond. Schwartz-Salant asks the analyst or participants to attend to a third area with its own patterns and transformative potential. Conflating them would erase an important post-Jungian debate about whether the primary unit of explanation is the two differentiated subjects or the relational field that includes and exceeds them.

### 56. Young-Eisendrath versus Knox

Jean Knox's *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis* reconstructs analytical psychology through developmental psychology, attachment, cognitive science, internal working models, and reflective function. Its central claim is that meaning and many psychic patterns emerge developmentally in relationship rather than existing as fully formed symbolic contents awaiting activation. [Post-Jungian comparison: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, Introduction; chs. 3-7]

The closest overlap with Young-Eisendrath is Knox's account of **reflective function**: the capacity to experience oneself and others as independent psychological and emotional beings with beliefs, intentions, desires, judgments, and feelings. Knox treats this capacity as developmentally emerging and closely linked to attachment security and mentalisation. [Post-Jungian comparison: Knox, ch. 6]

Young-Eisendrath's "actual other," witnessing, mindful gap, and dialogue point toward a similar relational achievement, but her explanation is different. She emphasises projection, idealisation, mindfulness, equality, and adult dialogical practice. Knox explains the developmental architecture by which a person becomes capable of representing minds in the first place.

### 57. Recognition versus Reflective Function

Recognition in Young-Eisendrath is a relational practice of encountering the partner as a separate subject. Reflective function in Knox is a developmental capacity to represent mental and emotional states in self and other. The two can support each other but are not identical. [Post-Jungian comparison: Knox, ch. 6; Young-Eisendrath, ch. 4]

A person could have considerable reflective-function capacity and still behave contemptuously in a particular relationship. Conversely, a couple may practice a dialogue structure that improves recognition even when mentalisation collapses under stress. Knox gives a developmental model; Young-Eisendrath gives an adult relational discipline.

### 58. Young-Eisendrath versus Contemporary Jungian Couples Therapy

Elizabeth Éowyn Nelson and Anthony Delmedico's *The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy: An Introduction* is a later, 2026 contemporary Jungian couple text and should not be back-projected into Young-Eisendrath's 2019 book. It integrates Jungian concepts with contemporary couple-clinical material, explicitly including complexes, attachment, enactments, typology, family-of-origin patterns, and the soul of the relationship. [Post-Jungian comparison: Nelson and Delmedico, *The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy*, Introduction and relevant chapters]

Relative to Young-Eisendrath, Nelson and Delmedico provide a wider clinician-facing toolkit. They are more explicit about complex constellations, reciprocal enactments, and the therapist's work in the consulting room. Their model can treat a couple episode simultaneously through complexes, attachment patterns, typological difference, and relational process. Young-Eisendrath's source is more focused: equality, projection, mindful differentiation, witnessing, and dialogue.

Young-Eisendrath should therefore be read as one important relational strand within contemporary Jungian couple work, not as a comprehensive statement of that field.

## Part IX - Attachment, EFCT and Contemporary Couple Comparisons

### 59. Love Between Equals versus Adult Attachment Theory

Adult attachment theory and love between equals overlap around dependence, closeness, support, and relational security, but they ask different primary questions. Mikulincer and Shaver organise adult attachment around an evolved attachment behavioural system. An attachment figure provides proximity, a safe haven in distress, and a secure base for exploration. Attachment security concerns expectations about whether important others are available and responsive when needed and how threat is regulated. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1-3, 9-10]

Young-Eisendrath's model asks whether two adults can recognise each other as equal subjects after idealisation and disillusionment. Its distinctive variables are projection, otherness, dialogue, differentiation, mutuality, adult agency, and witnessing. Secure attachment may make these tasks easier, but it does not define them.

A secure attachment bond can exist in a relationship that remains unequal in other respects. Conversely, two adults may value equality and maintain strong differentiation while still struggling with attachment insecurity. The constructs intersect but neither subsumes the other.

### 60. Equality versus Secure Attachment

Secure attachment is not equivalent to equality. Attachment theory does not define all close bonds as relationships between equal subjects. Parent-child attachment is paradigmatically asymmetrical: the caregiver is expected to function as the stronger and wiser safe haven and secure base. Adult romantic attachment becomes more reciprocal, but the theory's core criterion remains access to protection, comfort, and security under threat. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 1]

Young-Eisendrath's equality instead concerns adult standing, voice, agency, mutual recognition, and negotiated power. A relationship could feel secure because one partner reliably provides comfort while the decision-making structure remains paternalistic. That would satisfy some attachment functions while failing Young-Eisendrath's egalitarian criterion.

### 61. Differentiation versus Attachment Security

Differentiation and attachment security are also distinct. Secure attachment can support exploration and autonomy; Mikulincer and Shaver explicitly argue that mature autonomy and reliance are compatible and that successful attachment makes closeness and autonomous functioning mutually sustainable. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 1]

This is highly compatible with Young-Eisendrath's rejection of self-sufficiency. Yet differentiation names a different task: maintaining self-definition and recognition of otherness in the face of emotional involvement and projection. Attachment security is about confidence in availability and support. A securely attached person can still make projective assumptions; a differentiated person can still become attachment-anxious under threat.

### 62. Dependency in Attachment and Young-Eisendrath

Here the frameworks converge against a common error. Attachment theory explicitly rejects the view that dependence on others is immature or pathological at all ages. Mature adults seek care when threatened, lonely, in pain, or demoralised. Young-Eisendrath likewise rejects emotional self-sufficiency and values mature dependence. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, ch. 1; Young-Eisendrath, chs. 1, 5]

The difference is explanatory. Attachment theory understands support-seeking through the attachment behavioural system and working models. Young-Eisendrath understands adult dependence through reciprocity, witnessing, equality, vulnerability, and responsibility for projection. Both can be true descriptions at different levels without being the same construct.

### 63. Young-Eisendrath versus EFCT

Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT), in Susan M. Johnson's attachment-centred lineage, organises couple distress around insecure attachment, emotion, and self-reinforcing interaction cycles. The therapist tracks the negative cycle, accesses primary attachment emotion, restructures interaction, and creates more accessible, responsive, and engaged bonding encounters. [EFCT comparison: Johnson, *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 2-9; Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 6-7]

Young-Eisendrath's model is not a negative-cycle attachment model. She does describe mutually reinforcing projective interactions, but her formulation foregrounds idealisation, projective identification, equality, differentiation, mindfulness, and dialogue. The two traditions can describe the same observable episode differently.

For example, a pursuer's accusation and a withdrawer's silence might be understood in EFCT as an attachment protest and protective deactivation within a negative cycle. Young-Eisendrath might ask what each person assumes about the other, what projected role is being enacted, whether the partners can reclaim their own feelings and interpretations, and whether dialogue can restore the mindful gap. Neither explanation should be relabelled as the other.

### 64. Dialogue versus EFCT Enactment

This distinction is mandatory. In EFCT, an **enactment** is deliberately shaped by the therapist after emotional experience has been accessed and deepened. The therapist may ask one partner to turn and express a newly formulated vulnerable message directly to the other, then help the receiving partner process and respond. Johnson describes such enactments as central mechanisms for creating new attachment interactions. [EFCT comparison: Johnson, *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 4, 8]

Young-Eisendrath's **dialogue** is a structured mutual conversation whose core skills are speaking for oneself, paraphrasing, curiosity, and responding. Its purpose is accurate encounter across difference and the reduction of projection. It does not require a therapist to heighten primary attachment emotion or choreograph a bonding event.

Thus:

- dialogue can occur outside therapy;
- EFCT enactment is a therapist-shaped clinical intervention;
- dialogue prioritises inquiry and differentiated recognition;
- enactment prioritises new emotional engagement and attachment responsiveness;
- either may include listening and emotional disclosure, but shared techniques do not make them theoretically identical.

### 65. Equality versus EFCT Mutual Accessibility

EFCT seeks relationships in which partners can be emotionally accessible, responsive, and engaged, especially under attachment threat. Young-Eisendrath's equality asks whether each partner is recognised as an autonomous adult subject with voice and agency. These often support one another but do not entail each other. [EFCT comparison: Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, ch. 6; Young-Eisendrath, chs. 4-6]

A highly responsive partner could still be controlling. A strongly egalitarian relationship could still struggle to provide reliable attachment comfort. Equality should therefore not be inferred from emotional accessibility alone.

### 66. Young-Eisendrath versus Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy

Leslie Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy is an emotion-process model rather than Johnson's attachment-centred EFCT. Greenberg distinguishes kinds of emotional response, works with emotion schemes, and uses experiential processes to access, symbolise, regulate, and transform maladaptive emotion with other emotion. [Emotion-Focused Therapy comparison: Greenberg, *Emotion-Focused Therapy: Coaching Clients to Work Through Their Feelings*, 2nd ed., chs. 1-9, 13]

Young-Eisendrath's dialogue is not primarily an emotion-transformation technology. It does ask participants to recognise and regulate emotional activation, but its distinctive aim is to restore subject-to-subject contact and epistemic humility. Greenberg asks more directly what emotion is present, whether it is primary adaptive, primary maladaptive, secondary, or instrumental, what need it contains, and how emotional change occurs.

The models can complement one another. A person may need emotion-focused work to transform entrenched shame or fear before sustained dialogue is possible. But "emotion processing" and "projection withdrawal" remain different theoretical descriptions.

### 67. Conflict

For Young-Eisendrath, conflict often exposes the exact material that idealisation had concealed: difference, dependency, limits, power, projection, and incompatible expectations. Conflict is therefore not evidence that love has failed. It becomes destructive when the partners lose the mindful gap and convert difference into enemy-making. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 1-4]

Her model directs attention to several questions: What role has each person assigned the other? What certainty about motive is being treated as fact? What personal expectation has been violated? What actual behaviour occurred? What part belongs to each person? Can each partner speak without diagnosing the other? Can the disagreement be made specific?

### 68. Disagreement

Disagreement is a normal expression of separateness. A relationship between equals should expect it. The psychological issue is whether difference can remain difference rather than becoming proof that one partner is defective, uncaring, irrational, or hostile. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 4]

Dialogue can improve the quality of disagreement by making each side more accurately known. It need not produce a common position. Mature disagreement may end in compromise, parallel preferences, a boundary, delayed decision, or recognition of incompatibility.

### 69. Criticism and Defensiveness

Young-Eisendrath draws selectively on John Gottman's work, especially the damaging role of contempt, and she repeatedly describes the defensive loops created when partners feel humiliated or misread. Criticism and defensiveness become especially toxic when they confirm projected images: "you are controlling," "you are selfish," "you do not care," "you are impossible." [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 5]

The source's remedy is not a blanket ban on criticism. Partners need to identify hurt, unfairness, broken commitments, and difference. The shift is from totalising character judgment toward specific observation, owned feeling, inquiry, and accountability.

### 70. Rupture

The source does not present a formal rupture-repair model comparable with some contemporary psychotherapy literature. A rupture can nevertheless be reconstructed as a loss of trust, witnessing, or mutual recognition produced by aggression, betrayal, contempt, chronic projection, or repeated failure to engage. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3-5]

Some ruptures are repairable; some are not. Young-Eisendrath explicitly notes that violence can make trust irreparable. This is a crucial limit on any spiritualised command to remain in dialogue.

### 71. Repair

Repair in Young-Eisendrath's framework involves restoration of truthful contact. It can include apology, accountability, a reduction in aggression, renewed curiosity, dialogue, clarification of projection, and renegotiation of commitment or boundaries. The source does not establish a single repair protocol. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 2-5]

Repair should therefore be treated as a family of processes rather than a guaranteed result of good communication. It also need not mean reconciliation. A relationship may be repaired enough for an honest separation.

### 72. Dialogue Does Not Guarantee Reconciliation

Dialogue can lead to deeper relationship, but it can also expose a difference that cannot be reconciled. The source contains a strong commitment ethic, yet it also recognises violence, betrayal, practical incompatibility, changing life aims, and the possibility that mutual true love is not available. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 4-7]

Therefore:

**successful dialogue = clearer encounter**, not **successful dialogue = staying together**.

This distinction is partly a Laboratory guardrail, but it is consistent with the source's own recognition that mutuality sometimes fails and that trust can be damaged beyond repair.

### 73. Equality versus Equal Responsibility

Equal personhood never means equal responsibility for every event. Young-Eisendrath's commitment to no-blame compassion is paired with explicit accountability. If one person lies, assaults, coerces, humiliates, or breaks an agreement, responsibility for that act does not become fifty-fifty merely because the relationship is mutual. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 2; Laboratory guardrail]

A relational analysis can examine how both partners respond without laundering conduct into symmetry. The distinction is especially important when one person has greater power or uses fear to constrain the other.

### 74. Mutual Influence versus Mutual Blame

Partners inevitably influence each other. One person's withdrawal may heighten the other's pursuit; pursuit may intensify withdrawal. But causal interaction and moral responsibility are not the same question. [Laboratory synthesis informed by Young-Eisendrath, EFCT, and attachment theory]

A sound formulation can say both:

- "these behaviours form a self-reinforcing interaction," and
- "one particular behaviour may still be substantially more harmful or blameworthy."

The first statement maps process. The second addresses responsibility. Collapsing them creates false equivalence.

### 75. Dialogue and Boundaries

Listening does not require accepting mistreatment, abandoning a boundary, remaining in the relationship, or treating all positions as equally valid. Dialogue is possible only when there is enough safety for each person to have a standpoint. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3-5; Laboratory ethical guardrail]

A boundary may be dialogical when it is stated clearly and owned: "I understand what you want, and I will not agree to that." There are also situations in which the appropriate response is not further dialogue but protection, distance, external help, or ending contact.

### 76. Equality under Power Imbalance

The source recognises differences in money, authority, gender tradition, need, illness, and relational dependence, but it does not provide a comprehensive contemporary framework for severe power imbalance. Therefore any extended analysis of coercive control, violence, financial abuse, immigration leverage, disability exploitation, or institutional discrimination must be labelled external or Laboratory synthesis.

[Laboratory guardrail] Equal-subject language is not sufficient where one person cannot safely refuse, leave, speak, or access resources. In such cases, the factual structure of power must be assessed before a mutual-dialogue frame is applied.

### 77. Staying, Leaving and Mature Love

Young-Eisendrath values commitment and repeatedly presents a long-term partnership as a privileged site for development. That normative preference must not be turned into the universal equation:

- staying = maturity;
- leaving = avoidance;
- reconciliation = successful dialogue;
- separation = failure of individuation.

A mature process can lead to deeper commitment, renegotiation, altered living arrangements, clearer boundaries, or separation. Conversely, staying can reflect fear, dependency, coercion, economic necessity, or unwillingness to confront incompatibility. Relationship outcome is not a direct measure of psychological development. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, chs. 3-7; Laboratory outcome-neutrality rule]

### 78. Equality and Commitment

Commitment is one of Young-Eisendrath's three Cs and is central to her model. She treats it as commitment to the relationship as an enduring third reality rather than as constant calculation of immediate advantage. Commitment creates a stable container in which disillusionment can be worked through rather than automatically interpreted as a signal to leave. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 3]

Yet equality changes the meaning of commitment. It is not obedience to one partner or to an inherited hierarchy. It is a chosen constraint among adults. If the relationship becomes coercive or fundamentally incompatible, the book does not justify treating commitment as an unlimited duty.

### 79. Love and Compatibility

Recognition of the actual other does not guarantee compatibility. Young-Eisendrath explicitly notes that partners can change politically, religiously, sexually, practically, or developmentally. She also acknowledges that some incompatibilities are visible before marriage and should be taken seriously. [Young-Eisendrath: *Love between Equals*, ch. 7]

Mature love can therefore coexist with the conclusion that two life projects cannot be combined. Better dialogue may reveal that incompatibility more clearly. Love is a relation to a person; compatibility is a relation between two sets of values, needs, constraints, and life choices. They overlap but are not the same construct.

## Part X - Self-Analysis and Couple-Dynamics Applications

The following sections are **Laboratory applications** built from source-supported distinctions. They are not Young-Eisendrath's published checklists and should not be attributed to her as exact protocols. They are designed for reflective inquiry rather than diagnosis.

### 80. Self-Analysis Relevance

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

A Young-Eisendrath-informed self-inquiry begins by shifting attention from "What is wrong with my partner?" to a more discriminating set of questions:

- What did I actually observe, and what did I infer?
- What do I assume my partner should be for me?
- Which expectation am I treating as self-evident rather than negotiated?
- What role have I assigned them: rescuer, rejecter, parent, child, critic, incompetent one, selfish one, perfect witness?
- What parts of the partner do I routinely fail to recognise because they do not fit that role?
- Where do I demand sameness in the name of equality?
- Where do I erase my own standpoint in the name of harmony?
- What difference is hardest for me to tolerate?
- What am I certain I know about the partner's motives that I have not actually asked about?
- What would genuine curiosity require here?
- Which part of my reaction belongs to a known history, wound, fear, ideal, or habitual self-image?
- Which part of the problem is clearly external and behavioural rather than projective?
- Am I using "projection" to excuse something that actually violates a boundary?
- Am I using "differentiation" to justify emotional withdrawal?
- Am I using "dependency" as an insult when ordinary adult need is involved?
- Am I using "mutuality" to obscure asymmetrical responsibility?
- Do I want understanding, agreement, reassurance, apology, behavioural change, or a decision? These are different requests.

The value of this inquiry is not that it guarantees the "correct" interpretation. It reduces premature certainty and separates several questions that are often fused under emotional pressure.

### 81. Actual-Other Inquiry Protocol

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

1. **Describe the observable act.** Use the narrowest behavioural description available.
2. **Separate observation from interpretation.** Write the meaning you assigned to the act as a second statement.
3. **State the motive-story.** Identify what you believe the other intended or felt.
4. **Identify the expectation.** What did you assume a loving, loyal, respectful, or competent partner would do instead?
5. **Name the emotional reaction.** Include anger, fear, shame, grief, desire, envy, humiliation, or relief without assuming the emotion proves the interpretation.
6. **Ask whether projection may be involved.** Does the image of the partner resemble an older relational pattern or a disowned part of self?
7. **Seek disconfirming evidence.** What facts do not fit the current image of the partner?
8. **Name what you do not know.** Especially distinguish unknown motive from known behaviour.
9. **Ask the partner's account if safe and appropriate.** Do not convert the question into a prosecution.
10. **Preserve your own standpoint.** Understanding the partner need not revise a boundary or value.
11. **Distinguish understanding from agreement.** Record both separately.
12. **Identify actual value or life-choice differences.** Some problems are not projection problems.
13. **Identify power or boundary issues.** Ask whether the other person can safely disagree or refuse.
14. **State the remaining uncertainty.** A good inquiry can end with unresolved ambiguity.

The protocol operationalises Young-Eisendrath's movement from certainty toward curiosity without promising objective access to the partner psyche.

### 82. Equality Inquiry Protocol

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Use this protocol to examine the structure of a relationship rather than score the virtue of either person:

1. Does each person have meaningful voice in decisions that affect both?
2. Can either person disagree without retaliation, ridicule, withdrawal of necessities, or threats?
3. Is each person recognised as an adult subject rather than managed as a child or diagnosed as a patient?
4. Are differences in need, desire, ambition, sexuality, sociability, and pace permitted to exist?
5. Is one person's interpretation automatically treated as more rational, moral, spiritual, or psychologically sophisticated?
6. Are practical asymmetries openly negotiated rather than converted into permanent superiority?
7. Is dependency used to control the dependent person?
8. Is self-sufficiency idealised in a way that shames legitimate need?
9. Is "fairness" being confused with identical contribution in every period?
10. Is "mutual influence" being used to claim equal responsibility for a specific harmful act?
11. Can each person maintain friendships, purposes, and areas of competence outside the couple?
12. Are material resources and exit options distributed in ways that make formal equality misleading?

Questions 11 and 12 extend beyond the book's strongest treatment and are therefore explicit Laboratory guardrails.

### 83. Dialogue Inquiry Protocol

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS BASED ON SOURCE-SUPPORTED PRINCIPLES]

1. Identify the specific disagreement or hurt.
2. Partner A speaks from personal experience without claiming direct access to Partner B's mind.
3. Partner B paraphrases the meaning heard and checks accuracy.
4. Partner B asks open questions where meaning remains unclear.
5. Partner A corrects or expands the account.
6. Partner B responds from a distinct standpoint rather than simply accommodating.
7. Reverse roles if needed.
8. Identify assumptions or projections that changed during the exchange.
9. State the disagreement that remains after improved understanding.
10. Identify any concrete request.
11. Identify any boundary or non-negotiable constraint.
12. Ask what changed in understanding, not merely who "won."
13. Do not force synthesis. A clarified difference is a legitimate outcome.
14. Stop the dialogue if safety, coercion, extreme activation, or incapacity makes genuine participation impossible.

Steps 1-7 are closely grounded in chapter 4's four-skill model. Steps 8-14 are Laboratory elaborations designed to protect equality, boundaries, and outcome neutrality.

### 84. Projection-to-Dialogue Map

[HEURISTIC RECONSTRUCTION]

```text
projected image of partner
        ↓
certainty about motive / character
        ↓
contradiction by behaviour or difference
        ↓
disappointment, anger, fear, devaluation
        ↓
possible recognition of subjective expectation
        ↓
"I may not fully know what this means to you"
        ↓
curiosity + first-person speech + listening
        ↓
partner's account enters the picture
        ↓
clearer distinction between projection and external behaviour
        ↓
more actual encounter, with agreement or disagreement still possible
```

### 85. Equality-Differentiation Map

[HEURISTIC RECONSTRUCTION]

```text
SELF AS SUBJECT  ↔  OTHER AS SUBJECT
       |                    |
   own voice            other voice
   own need             other need
   own agency           other agency
       \                  /
        tolerance of difference
                 ↓
              dialogue
                 ↓
       reciprocal recognition
                 ↓
       intimacy without fusion
```

The map is conceptual, not mathematical. It shows why equality and differentiation support one another: equality protects both subjects; differentiation keeps them distinct; dialogue makes the distinction relational rather than isolating.

### 86. Couple Episode Map

[LABORATORY COMPARATIVE SYNTHESIS]

**Observable episode:** Partner A experiences Partner B's silence after conflict as rejection. Partner A presses for immediate discussion. Partner B experiences the pressing as overwhelming pressure and becomes quieter or leaves the room.

#### Young-Eisendrath formulation

Questions would include: What does A assume B's silence means? What role does B occupy in A's internal theatre? What does B assume A's pursuit means? Are either or both partners treating an interpretation as direct knowledge? What is each person's actual need and boundary? Can they restore the mindful gap and dialogue without requiring identical timing? [Young-Eisendrath: chs. 2-4]

#### Attachment formulation

A's pursuit may be understood as proximity-seeking under perceived relational threat; B's withdrawal may function as a deactivating or protective strategy under distress. The central question is whether the attachment system can regain felt security through available and responsive connection. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, chs. 1-3, 9-10]

#### EFCT formulation

The sequence may be mapped as a negative interaction cycle, often pursuer-withdrawer. Therapy would access primary attachment emotions beneath the positions and shape new interactions in which vulnerability can be expressed and responded to. [EFCT comparison: Johnson, *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 5-9]

#### Jungian formulation

# Contemporary Jungian Couples Therapy

## Source and Edition Note

The principal source is Elizabeth Éowyn Nelson and Anthony Delmedico, *The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy: An Introduction*. The Project EPUB verifies the title, subtitle, and joint authorship on its title page. The copyright page states that the book was first published in 2026 by Routledge, an imprint of the Taylor & Francis Group, and is copyright © 2026 Elizabeth Éowyn Nelson and Anthony Delmedico. The print and ebook identifiers in that source are ISBN 978-1-032-68799-5 (hardback), 978-1-032-68798-8 (paperback), 978-1-032-68800-8 (ebook), and DOI 10.4324/9781032688008. The EPUB package metadata contains a production date of 2025-05-19 and an additional ebook identifier, but this is packaging metadata rather than the publication year; the bibliographic publication date used in this file is therefore 2026. No separate edition statement appears beyond “First published 2026.”

The verified source structure is: Prologue; Introduction; 1. “The Self, Individuation, and Jungian Couples Therapy”; 2. “Couples and Complexes”; 3. “The Archetypal Basis of Coupled Life”; 4. “Typology in Couples Therapy”; 5. “The Shadow and the Couple”; 6. “The Alchemy of Relationship”; 7. “Diversity and Contemporary Coupling”; 8. “Jung and the Soul of the Therapist”; Index. The chapters are not separately attributed to different contributors. Nelson and Delmedico are the book’s authors throughout. The volume includes two conceptual figures before the alchemy chapter, nine alchemical images in Chapter 6, five typology tables in Chapter 4, chapter-end notes and references, short “excursions,” and relatively little conventional case-vignette material.

The source is a clean born-digital EPUB with machine-readable XHTML and encoded print-page markers. Extraction quality is high. It is possible to reconstruct chapter sequence, headings, figures, tables, notes, references, and print pagination with good confidence. The main limitation is not technical but clinical: the authors explicitly decline to present a manualized or systematized treatment protocol. Their book is a clinical-philosophical introduction to an attitude, conceptual vocabulary, and way of working. Clinical vignettes are deliberately sparse. Dreams, extended dream interpretation, imaginal dialogue, the transcendent function as a method, and sandplay are acknowledged as useful Jungian practices but are explicitly left outside the book’s main scope. Any protocol in this file that goes beyond the authors’ stated procedures is therefore marked as Laboratory synthesis rather than attributed to Nelson and Delmedico.

[Nelson & Delmedico: *The Art of Jungian Couples Therapy*, title page, copyright page, contents, Introduction, pp. 1–10]

## What Makes the Approach Jungian and Dyadic

Nelson and Delmedico do not define Jungian couple therapy as “Jungian analysis with two patients.” Their central move is to bring a recognizably Jungian model of psyche into a clinical situation in which at least two subjectivities, their actual relationship, and the therapist are simultaneously active. In the Introduction they speak of “four souls”: each partner, the therapist, and the relationship itself. The formulation is deliberately imaginal. It means that the therapist must hold more than two individual psychologies. The relationship is treated as living and developmental, not merely as the arithmetic sum of the two partners.

The approach is Jungian because it assumes that unconscious life is active, autonomous, symbolic, prospective as well as historical, and oriented in part toward wholeness. It uses the Self, individuation, complexes, archetypal patterns, projection, shadow, typology, alchemical imagery, myth, soul, and transference as working concepts. It expects relationship to expose material that neither partner can encounter alone. It regards suffering as potentially meaningful without claiming that suffering is inherently good. It asks not only what caused a difficulty, but what the difficulty may be asking of the persons and the relationship. It privileges curiosity, symbolic imagination, and sustained attention over rapid technique.

It is specifically dyadic because these concepts are followed in real-time exchanges between partners. A complex is not only something inside Partner A. It may be constellated by what Partner B actually says or does; A’s complex-driven reaction then becomes an event B must respond to; B’s response may constellate another complex; and a recursively reinforced relational pattern can emerge. Projection is not treated only as a patient’s image of an analyst. Each partner may project onto the other, while the therapist is simultaneously drawn into transferential and countertransferential processes. Typology becomes clinically useful because different modes of perceiving, judging, social engagement, and processing can collide in ordinary domestic life. Individuation becomes a couple issue because one person’s development changes the relational arrangements to which both have adapted.

The authors nevertheless resist turning these concepts into labels. The recurring instruction is to notice, wonder, slow down, differentiate, and remain close to immediate experience. A Jungian term should enlarge perception, not close inquiry. The therapist is asked to move between two levels: the personal-historical level and the archetypal-symbolic level. Neither should erase the other.

[Nelson & Delmedico: Introduction, pp. 1–8; ch. 1, pp. 11–25; ch. 2, pp. 29–41]

## Coverage of This File

This reconstruction gives priority to Nelson and Delmedico’s actual model. Primary Jung is used to clarify inheritance rather than to pretend that Jung himself developed conjoint couple therapy. Later Jungian authors are used only where they help distinguish specific developments: Schwartz-Salant for the interactive field; Young-Eisendrath for equality, projection, and dialogue; Knox for developmental and attachment-informed revision; Beebe for later typological elaboration; Fordham for developmental Self theory; and Samuels for the differentiation of post-Jungian schools. Adult attachment, Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT), and Greenberg’s Emotion-Focused Therapy are treated as comparison frameworks rather than as hidden translations of Jungian ideas.

The file also imposes an explicit reality check that the source itself sometimes leaves implicit. Three levels must remain available at once:

1. **Individual psychology:** each partner’s complexes, projections, shadow, typology, history, conscious attitudes, and individuation pressures.
2. **Dyadic process:** observable triggers, reactions, counter-reactions, defensive moves, escalating or withdrawing sequences, mutual expectations, and recursive reinforcement.
3. **Actual relational reality:** what people actually do, what they have agreed, whether they lie or tell the truth, whether values and goals match, whether there is betrayal, coercion, intimidation, violence, material dependency, sexual non-consent, or other asymmetries of power and responsibility.

Levels 1 and 2 never cancel Level 3. A symbolic interpretation can be meaningful and still fail as an account of responsibility. Mutual influence does not prove equal causation. Projection can coexist with accurate perception. A partner can carry another person’s projection and simultaneously be behaving badly.

[LABORATORY METHODOLOGICAL GUARDRAIL]

## How to Use This File

Use the file in three passes. First, identify what Nelson and Delmedico actually claim and do. Second, distinguish their use of Jung from Jung’s own primary formulations and from later post-Jungian innovations. Third, if applying concepts to a real relationship, route every interpretation through observable behaviour and alternative hypotheses. A “complex,” “shadow,” “projection,” “inferior function,” “alchemical stage,” or “field” should be treated as a hypothesis that organizes attention, not as a diagnosis or a privileged explanation.

The key anti-flattening distinctions are carried throughout:

- complex activation is not attachment activation and is not an emotion scheme;
- projection is not misperception in general and is not an attachment expectation;
- shadow is not the partner’s disliked behaviour;
- anima/animus is not partner gender;
- type difference is not incompatibility;
- inferior function is not emotional dysregulation;
- individuation is not independence and not relationship satisfaction;
- differentiation is not distance;
- mutual influence is not equal responsibility;
- unconscious complementarity is not compatibility;
- symbolic meaning is not factual truth;
- archetypal intensity is not relationship destiny;
- coniunctio is not synonymous with a successful marriage;
- dialogue is not an EFCT enactment;
- secure attachment is not individuation;
- repair is not projection withdrawal.

---

## Part I - From Individual Analytical Psychology to Couple Therapy

### 1. The conceptual shift: one psyche to multiple subjectivities

Classical Jungian analysis is most easily pictured as one analysand in relationship with one analyst. Nelson and Delmedico move the centre of gravity to Partner A + Partner B + therapist, while also granting the relationship a phenomenological status of its own. This produces four simultaneous lines of attention: what is happening within A, within B, between A and B, and within the therapist as participant-observer. Their “four souls” language is not a literal ontology that can be empirically verified. It is a clinical device for preventing reduction of the relationship to one identified patient or one explanatory variable.

This shift changes interpretation. If A becomes enraged after B arrives home late, a purely intrapsychic account might ask what childhood complex has been triggered in A. A dyadic account also asks what B did, what B expected would happen, how B responded to A’s anger, and what sequence the two create together. A reality-based account asks whether lateness breached an agreement, whether there is chronic unreliability, deception, fear, or any material consequence. A symbolic account may ask what “waiting,” “absence,” “home,” or “being left” means in A’s psychic life. Nelson and Delmedico’s method is strongest when these modes coexist rather than compete.

[CONTEMPORARY JUNGIAN COUPLES]
[Nelson & Delmedico: Introduction, pp. 1–8; ch. 1, pp. 23–25]

### 2. What is the unit of treatment?

The source does not settle on one technical unit of treatment. At different points it treats:

- each partner as an individuating psyche;
- the couple’s recurring interaction and shared story;
- the relationship as a living “third” or alchemical vessel;
- the transferential field including the therapist;
- the cultural field in which coupling occurs.

This plurality is deliberate. In Chapter 6 the authors note that couples therapists are often taught that “the relationship is the patient,” then enlarge this by insisting that a relationship still consists of two individuals with separate needs and fates. An alchemical state of mind is therefore asked to hold both partners and the relationship. In Chapter 8 the therapist is added explicitly as a psyche that will be altered by the work.

The best source-faithful answer is therefore: **the unit of treatment is multiple and shifting**. There is no single privileged object called “the couple system” that subsumes the individuals, and the authors do not reduce the work to two parallel individual analyses. The therapist follows whichever level is psychologically alive while preserving the others.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 101–102, 114–117; ch. 8, pp. 140–150]

### 3. Treatment goals

The source does not provide a formal goals hierarchy. Its recurring goals can nevertheless be reconstructed with reasonable confidence:

- increasing consciousness of unconscious motives and patterns;
- helping each partner distinguish self from partner, especially through projection withdrawal and separatio;
- making complexes more recognizable and less blindly autonomous;
- increasing the capacity to hold affect without immediate evacuation or enactment;
- respecting psychological type and developing more flexible access to neglected functions;
- widening each partner’s sense of self through shadow work and individuation;
- allowing relationship narratives, myths, and roles to be revised;
- increasing the couple’s ability to remain in contact with difference;
- cultivating a relationship that can change rather than demanding restoration of an earlier idealized state;
- allowing the therapist and couple to remain with uncertainty, grief, endings, and non-linear change.

Improved communication and deeper intimacy are present, but they are downstream of greater consciousness rather than presented as the entire treatment. Reconciliation is not the universal goal. The source explicitly recognizes that some relationships are already dissolving, that separatio can include breakup or divorce, and that individuation may reveal rather than resolve difference.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 20–25; ch. 5, pp. 89–97; ch. 6, pp. 105–118]

### 4. Assessment

There is no standardized intake instrument. Assessment is embedded in conversation and observation. Source-supported assessment domains include:

- the presenting complaint and the moment-to-moment interaction in the room;
- individual developmental and family histories;
- recurrent conflict and the emotional charge surrounding it;
- family-of-origin roles and complexes;
- what initially attracted each partner and what now irritates or repels them;
- typological preferences and mismatches in processing;
- the couple’s explicit vows and implicit “shadow vows”;
- sexual and romantic history, especially in affirmative work with gender-, sexual-, and relationship-diverse (GSRD) partners;
- cultural, religious, political, class, gender, and relational norms shaping the couple;
- projections and the degree to which partners can consider alternative accounts of one another;
- the therapist’s somatic, affective, imaginal, and countertransferential responses.

Chapter 2 recommends gathering detailed histories and discusses Young-Eisendrath’s preference, where tolerable, for hearing one partner’s individual history in the other partner’s presence. This can serve two functions: it generates developmental context and gives the therapist information about the listening partner’s capacity to tolerate the other’s subjectivity. Chapter 6’s separatio questions are another assessment process: reconstruct a conflict in granular sequence, separating what was done, felt, wanted, withheld, remembered, bodily experienced, and associated to prior conflicts.

The source does not establish a comprehensive contemporary screening protocol for intimate partner violence, coercive control, suicidality, child safety, or contraindications to conjoint work. That is a substantive scope limitation, not evidence that such screening is unnecessary.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 2, pp. 34–39; ch. 6, pp. 116–117; ch. 7, pp. 124–136]

### 5. Jung primary comparison: relationship and consciousness

Jung’s 1925 essay “Marriage as a Psychological Relationship” provides an important historical base but not a modern couple-treatment model. Jung begins by saying marriage is psychologically complex and contains subjective and objective factors; he then restricts his essay primarily to the psychological side. He defines a psychological relationship as requiring consciousness and differentiation. Where large areas remain unconscious, one person tends to presuppose that the other is structured like the self. Jung describes unconscious parental influences on partner choice, the initial feeling of oneness, and the crisis through which a merely collective or conventional bond may become more individual and conscious. He also connects later relationship crises to second-half-of-life development and projection.

The contemporary book inherits the consciousness/differentiation problem but alters the frame in several ways. It works with actual conjoint interaction, treats therapist participation as an explicit clinical variable, gives more room to culture and diversity, and directly criticizes Jung’s gender-essentialist formulations. It also refuses to assume that the conscious development of a relationship necessarily means preserving it.

[JUNG PRIMARY]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 17, “Marriage as a Psychological Relationship,” §§324–331c]
[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 20–23; ch. 7, pp. 122–136]

---

## Part II - Attraction, Projection and Partner Choice

### 6. The couple story

Nelson and Delmedico do not prescribe a formal “couple story interview,” but narrative is central. Therapists listen for the story of meeting, attraction, family reactions, vows, turning points, repeated disappointments, and the metaphors through which partners already imagine the relationship. Chapter 3 explicitly treats story as medicine. A narrative may have hardened into an indictment in which each new event is admitted only as evidence for a settled conclusion. The therapist can slow the telling, invite detail, and allow each partner’s partial truth without immediately adjudicating a single final story.

The partner’s presence as witness can be reparative when the listener is capable of hearing without weaponizing the disclosure. The source is alert to the opposite possibility: not every disclosure is safe to make in front of every partner. The story therefore has timing, context, and relational conditions. Narrative work is not merely “communication training”; it is an attempt to loosen literalized identity and allow a new relation to the same facts.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 3, pp. 50–57]

### 7. Attraction

Attraction is multiply determined. The source supports at least four Jungian contributors.

First, **complexes** can shape what feels compelling. The authors recommend asking what most attracted partners initially and what now drives them crazy, because the same constellation may underlie both. A partner who initially felt wonderfully protective can later feel controlling; spontaneity may become irresponsibility; emotional expressiveness may become “too much.” The formulation is not that attraction was false. Rather, the unconscious significance of the quality may exceed what the partners consciously understood.

Second, **typological complementarity** can create fascination. A highly differentiated function in one partner may carry the other’s neglected or inferior function. Introverts may be drawn to extraverts; thinkers to feelers; intuitive partners to sensing partners. Early in a relationship this can feel like completion. Later, dependency on the partner to carry an undeveloped function can become a source of irritation or contempt.

Third, **archetypal projection** can produce numinous intensity. Falling in love may carry a sense of fate, recognition, or extraordinary significance. The source treats this as psychologically important without treating it as proof that the relationship is destined or objectively “meant to be.”

Fourth, **actual partner qualities** remain real. Jungian interpretation does not require reducing desire to projection. A person may genuinely admire another’s humour, intelligence, kindness, sexuality, courage, beauty, reliability, creativity, or values. The correct formula is therefore: attraction may be amplified and organized by unconscious material, but **attraction ≠ projection alone**.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 20–22; ch. 2, pp. 33–35; ch. 4, pp. 74–79; ch. 6, pp. 105–113]

### 8. Unconscious partner choice

The source preserves Jung’s claim that unconscious factors participate in partner choice but does not turn this into a deterministic “we choose exactly the partner who will heal our childhood” doctrine. In Chapter 1, Jung’s historical account of parental bonds and instinctive choice is presented as part of the background. In Chapter 2, complexes are described as shaping the relational situations in which a person can continue familiar automatic modes of being. This can make some partners unusually evocative of long-standing expectations.

Primary Jung is stronger and more deterministic in places. In CW 17 §328 he directly links unconscious parental bonds to mate selection and in §330 describes marriage choice as normally following unconscious instinctive motivations unless distorted by other forces. A contemporary reconstruction should not turn those historical claims into an established empirical law of partner selection.

Young-Eisendrath provides a useful contrast. In *Love Between Equals* she explicitly resists the strong version of unconscious mate-selection theory. Her view is that intimate dependence can activate old emotional patterns with many close partners; it is not necessary to posit that one unconsciously selected a uniquely “right” person for a predetermined developmental task. Nelson and Delmedico are closer to classical Jungian language than Young-Eisendrath, but their own clinical stance remains exploratory rather than deterministic.

[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 17, §§327–330]
[Post-Jungian comparison: Young-Eisendrath, *Love Between Equals*, chs. 1–2]
[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 20–22; ch. 2, pp. 33–35]

### 9. Idealisation and disillusionment
