The book repeatedly describes an early state of fusion, especially through the alchemical language of **solutio**. Partners may feel that boundaries dissolve and differences disappear. The fantasy of “you complete me” can also be typological, archetypal, and cultural. Over time, separatio begins. Difference reappears. Positive projections weaken. The partner becomes more recognizably separate and ordinary.

This trajectory can be described heuristically:

**idealized image → lived contact → contradiction → disappointment → possible recognition of projection → greater differentiation**.

But the sequence is not automatic and it does not explain every disappointment. A partner may discover a real incompatibility, a broken agreement, a pattern of deception, or conduct that was not visible early on. Projection withdrawal is therefore one task among others, not a universal explanation for disillusionment.

Young-Eisendrath makes idealization/disillusionment even more central than Nelson and Delmedico. Her “personal love” model treats falling in love as strongly organized by idealizing projection and regards the move through disillusionment toward dialogue with an equal other as a major developmental task. Nelson and Delmedico share the projection-withdrawal theme but embed it in a broader matrix of complexes, type, archetype, shadow, alchemy, and individuation.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, pp. 74–79; ch. 5, pp. 87–94; ch. 6, pp. 105–114]
[Post-Jungian comparison: Young-Eisendrath, *Love Between Equals*, chs. 1–4]

### 10. Projection in couple therapy

Projection is central but distributed across the book rather than isolated in a single chapter. It appears in Jung’s marriage theory, complex dynamics, archetypal pairings, shadow, typology, anima/animus, alchemy, and transference. Clinically, projection is most evident when a person experiences an image or motive as unquestionably belonging to the partner while the source of some of that certainty lies in the projector’s own unconscious organization.

Projection can be positive or negative. In idealization, the partner may carry undeveloped capacities, desired qualities, an archetypal image, or a powerful hope. In demonization, the partner may carry aggression, dependency, selfishness, incompetence, emotionality, coldness, or other qualities the projector cannot tolerate as self-relevant. Complexes intensify projection because they produce emotionally charged, autonomous interpretations. Typological inferior functions are also especially available for projection because the person has limited conscious ownership of them.

The therapist’s task is not to announce “that is your projection.” The source repeatedly favors psychological discrimination: slow down, notice the emotional field, distinguish what belongs to whom, ask what is actually happening, and help the person reclaim what can be recognized as their own psychic material. Chapter 6 names this process through separatio. Chapter 5’s quaternity offers a diagrammatic way to picture conscious and unconscious relations crossing between partners.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 20–25; ch. 2, pp. 33–39; ch. 5, pp. 83–97; ch. 6, pp. 110–114]

### 11. Projection versus actual partner behaviour

This is a decisive clinical distinction. A person can project onto a partner who is also doing the thing complained of. If B repeatedly lies, A may additionally project motives or symbolic meanings onto B, but the lying remains behaviour. If A fears abandonment because of earlier loss, B’s unexplained disappearance for days may constellate the complex, but the current absence remains a relevant event. Psychological interpretation should add dimensionality, not subtract reality.

A useful three-column distinction is:

| Level | Question | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Observation | What happened that an external observer could in principle describe? | B left during the argument and returned three hours later. |
| Interpretation | What meaning did A give it? | “You left because you do not care about me.” |
| Projection hypothesis | What in A’s history or psyche may amplify or organize that interpretation? | Earlier abandonment, a neglected dependency need, an activated parental complex. |

The hypothesis does not settle motive. B may have left to punish A, to regulate overwhelming affect, to avoid escalation, to meet someone else, or for another reason. The actual reason remains an empirical and relational question.

[LABORATORY METHODOLOGICAL GUARDRAIL]

### 12. Withdrawal of projection

Projection withdrawal means increasing discrimination between the partner and the psychic image carried by the partner. In the source, **separatio** is the clearest process metaphor: sort commingled material, distinguish person from problem, identify previous wounding that has been placed onto the partner, and help each person own what can be owned. The authors emphasize that projections have “hooks”; the presence of a projection does not imply that nothing in the partner elicited it.

Projection withdrawal can increase intimacy because the actual partner becomes more visible. It can also reveal incompatibility. If an idealized image falls away and the partners discover conflicting values or goals, greater consciousness may increase clarity without increasing closeness.

Projection withdrawal is not equivalent to apology, behavioural repair, restored trust, or secure attachment. A partner can become more conscious of a projection while the other still needs to change harmful behaviour. Conversely, a couple can repair a specific rupture without any extensive Jungian projection work.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 110–113]

---

## Part III - Complexes, Shadow and Mutual Activation

### 13. Complexes

Chapter 2 is the source’s most operational clinical chapter. It returns to Jung’s early discovery that emotionally charged clusters of ideas, memories, images, bodily reactions, and tendencies can behave with relative autonomy. The crucial point for couple therapy is that a complex is not simply a belief a person can correct by deciding to think differently. When constellated, it can narrow consciousness, accelerate affect, and produce actions that later feel alien or disproportionate.

The authors describe complexes in several complementary registers. Figuratively, a complex can feel like another personality. Somatically, it appears in heart rate, breathing, colour, tension, sexual arousal, sweating, or other bodily shifts. Energetically, it gathers related material and produces attraction, repulsion, compulsion, and charged situations. Clinically, the therapist detects it through sudden changes in language, posture, tone, temperature of the interaction, and the repetitive “here we go again” quality of conflict.

Complexes may be organized around trauma, shame, parental relationships, authority, inadequacy, abandonment, dependency, sexuality, money, status, care, or other recurrent themes. They are not necessarily negative. A complex can organize creativity, devotion, or idealization as well as distress. The problem is relative autonomy and unconscious possession, not the mere existence of a complex.

Primary Jung’s *A Review of the Complex Theory* in CW 8 remains the relevant classical anchor: complexes are affectively charged and can disturb the ego’s intentions. Nelson and Delmedico’s innovation is to place multiple complex constellations into an immediate couple exchange and make the therapist’s own complexes part of the clinical field.

[JUNG PRIMARY]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 8, “A Review of the Complex Theory”]
[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 2, pp. 29–41]

### 14. Complex constellation between partners

[LABORATORY RECONSTRUCTION BASED ON SOURCE]

A source-consistent heuristic is:

**trigger → A’s complex constellates → A reacts from narrowed consciousness → B experiences A’s behaviour → B’s complex constellates → B counter-reacts → A experiences B’s response as confirmation of the original expectation → recursive reinforcement**.

For example, criticism might constellate an inadequacy complex in A. A becomes defensive or contemptuous. B experiences the defence as refusal to care and an abandonment complex constellates. B pursues more intensely. A experiences pursuit as further proof of being judged and withdraws. The cycle can then look self-confirming to both.

Nelson and Delmedico do not formalize this as a named “complex cycle” equivalent to a systemic or EFCT cycle. Their actual language is more organic: interlocked complexes, storm tracking, bipolar complexes, projective evacuation, and mutual activation. The diagram above is therefore a Laboratory heuristic, not an authorial algorithm.

### 15. Working with activated complexes

The source-supported clinical sequence is practical despite the book’s anti-manual stance:

- notice the increase in affective charge;
- slow the interaction rather than immediately adjudicating content;
- name the emotional climate without assigning a definitive interpretation;
- ask each person to notice bodily changes;
- reconstruct what happened immediately before the shift;
- inquire into memories or situations with a similar emotional signature;
- distinguish “I” from the autonomous “it” that seems to take over;
- make room for the complex without treating it as the whole personality;
- help partners signal when the “weather” is changing;
- allow enough time for ordinary ego functioning to return before demanding sophisticated reflection.

The source also invites a prospective question: not only “what caused this?” but “what may this disturbance be asking for?” That teleological question can uncover neglected development, but it must not be used to excuse conduct. The fact that rage has psychological meaning does not make intimidation acceptable.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 2, pp. 34–39]

### 16. Family-of-origin complexes

Developmental history matters because complexes accrue around repeated emotional situations and early relational figures. The source attends to maternal and paternal complexes, family roles, earlier conflict patterns, and implicit expectations inherited from the family. It recommends detailed histories rather than premature attachment labels. The point is to discover the particular affective constellation: what happened, what meaning formed, what bodily/emotional response recurs, and how the partner now becomes entangled in it.

This differs from an attachment formulation. A mother or father complex is not simply an insecure attachment style. The Jungian construct can include personal memory, fantasy, cultural imagery, symbolic and archetypal elements, and autonomous affective organization. Attachment theory asks different questions about availability, proximity, safe haven, secure base, internal working models, and regulation. The same history can be described through both frameworks without making the constructs identical.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 2, pp. 30–39]
[Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1–2, 5–7, 10]

### 17. Mutual activation does not mean equal responsibility

The source asks both partners to become curious about what they contributed to an interaction. It may ask not only what the person who acted did, but what was occurring internally in the other partner and what “button” was pressed. This can be clinically useful when the aim is to understand recurrence. It does not logically establish symmetrical responsibility.

One person may contribute a sharp tone while the other responds with violence. One may be anxious while the other lies repeatedly. One may withdraw while the other controls access to money. These are not morally or practically equivalent contributions. A complex formulation can explain why a person reacted intensely; it cannot allocate responsibility by itself.

[LABORATORY METHODOLOGICAL GUARDRAIL]

### 18. Complex versus actual conflict

Not all conflict is primarily complex-driven. Some conflicts concern real choices that remain difficult after excellent psychological insight: whether to have children, where to live, monogamy or consensual nonmonogamy, money, caregiving, religion, work, sexuality, family boundaries, sobriety, truthfulness, or incompatible futures. A useful distinction is:

| Complex-dominant indicators | Actual-conflict indicators |
|---|---|
| Affect is disproportionate, familiar, rapidly autonomous. | Disagreement remains coherent even when both are regulated. |
| The same emotional meaning appears across different situations. | The issue concerns a concrete decision, agreement, value, or resource. |
| Partner is experienced through a fixed charged image. | Each can describe the other accurately and still disagree. |
| Present event strongly evokes earlier family/relational material. | Resolution requires negotiation, choice, boundary, restitution, or separation. |

Many episodes contain both. The task is discrimination rather than choosing one explanation in advance.

### 19. Shadow

Chapter 5 treats the shadow as what the conscious personality does not recognize, accept, or want to be. It includes morally difficult material but is not synonymous with “bad traits.” Positive vitality, aggression, dependency, erotic desire, play, ambition, tenderness, selfishness, or solitude can all become shadowed when incompatible with the persona.

Couple life is fertile ground for shadow because the partner sees what the persona cannot fully maintain. The relationship also develops a culture of its own, with approved and prohibited roles. One partner may become “the emotional one,” the other “the rational one”; one “responsible,” the other “spontaneous”; one “good,” the other “selfish.” The more rigidly the pair distributes qualities, the easier it is for each person to disown what the other carries.

The therapist should not make the crude inference “whatever annoys you is your shadow.” Sometimes what annoys a person is simply annoying, harmful, or incompatible. A shadow hypothesis becomes stronger when the disliked quality has unusual emotional charge, appears repeatedly across relationships, contradicts the person’s self-image, or represents a capacity they forbid in themselves.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 5, pp. 83–97]

### 20. Persona, cultural shadow, and relationship roles

The persona organizes adaptation to social expectations. In couple life, personae include “good spouse,” “provider,” “caregiver,” “rational one,” “sexual one,” “easygoing one,” “perfect couple,” or culturally specific gender and family roles. These roles can stabilize a relationship while also generating shadow. What the couple cannot admit becomes more likely to appear indirectly.

Chapter 5 explicitly extends shadow work into the culture of the relationship and Chapter 7 broadens the cultural critique. A couple’s assumptions about monogamy, masculinity, femininity, parenting, money, sex, privacy, and social presentation may be partly inherited rather than consciously chosen. Contemporary Jungian work therefore asks not only “whose shadow is this?” but “what does this couple or culture make difficult to think, feel, or say?”

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 5, pp. 84–94; ch. 7, pp. 122–136]

### 21. Vows and shadow vows

One of Nelson and Delmedico’s clearest specific contributions is the idea of **shadow vows**, developed from their earlier work. Explicit vows state what the couple consciously promises. Shadow vows name the tacit assumptions, dependencies, bargains, prohibitions, and expectations that accompany the conscious bond. Examples are not fixed formulas, but the structure may resemble: “I will be the strong one if you remain the dependent one,” “we will never be like my parents,” “you will carry emotion so I do not have to,” or “our relationship will protect me from loneliness.”

Shadow-vow work is not an accusation that the original commitment was fraudulent. It recognizes that every conscious commitment has unconscious conditions. Bringing them into language can create what the authors sometimes describe as a “second relationship” between the same people, one more able to tolerate contradiction. It can also expose conditions that one or both no longer accept.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 5, pp. 89–94]

### 22. Jung’s quaternity and the couple

Figure 5.1 adapts the quaternity from Jung’s *Psychology of the Transference* (CW 16 §422) to couple work. In the source’s reconstruction:

- the upper horizontal relation represents the conscious relation between partners, including explicit vows;
- the vertical relations represent each individual’s conscious-unconscious axis;
- the lower horizontal relation represents unconscious-to-unconscious relation, including shadow vows;
- the diagonals represent crossings of conscious and unconscious material, including projections.

The value of the figure is that it prevents the conscious conversation from being mistaken for the whole relationship. Its danger would be to reify an invisible unconscious relation as if it could be directly observed. It is best used as a map of possible levels, not proof of a specific unconscious transaction.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 5, pp. 94–95, Figure 5.1]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 16, “The Psychology of the Transference,” §422]

### 23. Hatred, abandonment, selfishness, and play

The source deliberately makes room for shadowed affects and motives that couples prefer not to admit. Hatred can coexist with love. Naming its presence may reduce the pressure to convert it into moral superiority, symptom, or projective attack. “Abandonment” includes overt betrayal but also ordinary experiences of being left for work, children, family, friends, or solitude. “Selfishness” may be a genuine moral problem, but accusations of selfishness can also appear when one partner’s individuation requires solitude, boundaries, or an activity that the couple has not learned to accommodate.

The source’s treatment of betrayal is sometimes deliberately teleological, asking what an affair or other rupture reveals about the relationship. That symbolic question must not become a causal or moral exoneration. Betrayal remains an act with consequences and responsibility. The partner who was betrayed is not required to discover their “share” before the betrayer is accountable.

The chapter ends with playfulness. Drawing on Winnicott, the authors treat the capacity to play, imagine, and move out of fixed roles as a sign that psychic life has room to breathe. Play is not a universal intervention and should not be demanded in unsafe or acutely injured relationships.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 5, pp. 95–97]
[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

---

## Part IV - Psychological Type and Couple Difference

### 24. Type in couple therapy

Chapter 4 gives psychological type an unusually practical role. The authors retain Jung’s two attitudes, introversion and extraversion, and the four functions, sensation, thinking, feeling, and intuition. They repeatedly warn against turning typology into a personality box. Type is a description of habitual orientation and differentiated consciousness, not a total description of a person.

The attitudes matter because they organize where psychic energy tends to move. The introverted partner characteristically needs greater access to the inner world, time, solitude, and internal processing. The extraverted partner more readily engages the external situation, social contact, activity, and thinking aloud. In relationship, the same difference can be moralized: one is accused of withdrawal, secrecy, or antisocial behaviour; the other of intrusion, noise, superficiality, or dependency on external stimulation. A type formulation can reduce this moralization by asking whether at least part of the conflict concerns different habitual orientations.

The functions matter because they organize how information is taken in and evaluated. Nelson and Delmedico follow the classical distinction:

- **sensation** attends to what is concretely present and given;
- **intuition** attends to possibilities, implications, and what may emerge;
- **thinking** evaluates through conceptual connection, logic, and impersonal criteria;
- **feeling** evaluates by worth and value. Feeling here is a rational judging function in Jung’s sense, not a synonym for emotional intensity.

This final distinction is clinically important. A thinking-dominant partner can have intense emotion; a feeling-dominant partner can reason. The type terms describe preferred modes of conscious orientation, not capacities a person either possesses or lacks.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, pp. 63–74; Tables 4.1–4.5]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 6, ch. X “General Description of the Types”]

### 25. Type assessment without typing the partner

The authors suggest listening to the verbs through which partners naturally describe experience and sometimes explicitly asking the same issue through several functions: What do you think? What do you feel or value? What do you notice concretely? What possibilities do you imagine? A strongly differentiated thinker may answer a feeling question with another thought; a strong feeler may answer a thinking question by describing value or relationship impact. This is information, not proof of a fixed type.

The therapist should also notice pace. Introverted processing may require time before speech; extraverted processing may occur through speech. Intuitive partners may move rapidly to possibilities while sensing partners insist on details. Thinking partners may believe they are “just being rational,” while feeling partners may believe they are “just caring about people.” Each can mistake preference for objective superiority.

The clinical use of type is therefore **translation and differentiation**. It asks: what mode is this person using; what is the partner expecting; which function is being demanded; and is the demand realistic in a charged moment? It should not be used to tell partners what they “really are,” particularly when one partner has read typology and recruits the therapist into classifying the other.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, pp. 68–79]

### 26. Type difference and misattunement

Type differences can shape recurrent misattunement in at least five ways.

1. **Processing pace.** One partner wants to talk now; the other needs time to formulate an inner response.
2. **Evidence.** One emphasizes concrete events and sequence; the other emphasizes meanings and possibilities.
3. **Decision criteria.** One emphasizes logical consistency or efficiency; the other emphasizes human value and relational consequences.
4. **Social regulation.** One restores energy through solitude, the other through activity and contact.
5. **Conflict language.** Each may repeatedly ask the other to communicate through the asker’s preferred function.

The source’s illustrative Partner T/Partner F encounter makes the point. Rather than deciding that the thinker is emotionally deficient or the feeler irrational, the therapist validates that both are speaking from differentiated but partial modes. The therapist slows the exchange and may invite auxiliary functions as a bridge when direct access to the inferior function would be too difficult.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, pp. 75–79]

### 27. Type versus compatibility

Type is not a compatibility metric. The source gives no ranking of pairings and no rule that opposite types are better or worse. Complementarity may produce attraction and practical division of labour. It may also create dependency and later resentment. Similar types may understand each other readily in one domain while jointly neglecting another. Actual compatibility concerns lived realities such as values, future goals, sexuality, money, lifestyle, reliability, boundaries, caregiving expectations, and willingness to negotiate.

**Type difference ≠ incompatibility. Similar type ≠ compatibility.**

A type explanation should be tested against simpler alternatives. If one partner repeatedly refuses agreed responsibilities, “different type” may be an evasion. If one partner needs an hour to process before returning to a conflict and reliably returns, introverted processing may be highly relevant. The concept is clinically useful precisely when it sharpens observation rather than dissolves accountability.

### 28. The inferior function

The inferior function is the least differentiated and most unconscious function in Jung’s classical model. Nelson and Delmedico emphasize its vulnerability, fascination, and tendency to be projected. The partner may carry with relative ease what the self has not developed. Early in relationship that can feel liberating. Later, the partner’s strength may become irritating because it continually confronts the person with their own underdevelopment.

This produces a specific developmental tension. A thinker who relies on a feeling partner to carry valuation and emotional relationship may eventually resent the partner for “making everything personal.” A strong intuitor may admire a sensing partner’s practicality and later experience it as dullness. The problem is not that the opposite function is bad. The problem is dependence on the other to carry psychic work that increasingly presses for ownership.

Inferior-function activation should not be confused with emotional dysregulation. A person can become dysregulated for attachment, trauma, neurobiological, contextual, or other reasons independent of typology. Conversely, inferior-function material can appear as awkwardness, fascination, overstatement, or childish certainty without severe dysregulation.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, pp. 74–79]
[Post-Jungian comparison: von Franz, *Lectures on Jung’s Typology*, Part I]

### 29. Beebe’s model: acknowledged, not operationalized

Nelson and Delmedico cite John Beebe’s later development of an eight-function model and his linking of function-attitudes to archetypal positions. They do not, however, turn Beebe’s full eight-position architecture into their couple-treatment model. Their interventions remain grounded in Jung’s basic attitudes, four functions, superior/inferior differentiation, and auxiliary bridges.

Accordingly, File 21 should not retroactively place every partner response into Beebe’s hero, parent, child, anima/animus, opposing personality, senex/witch, trickster, or demon/daimon positions. Beebe is best used here as later typological context. The source-controlled claim is modest: Nelson and Delmedico know the elaboration and use some of its developmental sensibility, but their book does not require a full Beebe map for couple formulation.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, notes and discussion of later typology]
[Post-Jungian comparison: Beebe, *Energies and Patterns in Psychological Type*, chs. 1–3, 7–9, 14]

### 30. Figure 4.1 and the hierarchy of functions

Figure 4.1 depicts a hierarchy in which the superior function stands closest to conscious command, the inferior function reaches most deeply into the unconscious, and two auxiliary functions mediate between them. The clinical value is developmental rather than classificatory. Couples often organize division of labour around each partner’s differentiated functions. Over time the inferior function’s demand for development can destabilize that arrangement.

The figure should therefore be read dynamically: differentiation creates one-sidedness; the unconscious compensates; relationship exposes what has been left behind; development may require the person to reclaim capacities previously carried by the partner.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 4, Figure 4.1, pp. 73–75]

---

## Part V - Individuation, Differentiation and Relationship Development

### 31. The Self and individuation

Chapter 1 begins from the Self as an archetype of wholeness and from Jung’s conviction that the unconscious seeks manifestation. In this framework, the conscious reason a couple gives for seeking therapy may not exhaust the psychological significance of the crisis. A relationship can become one arena in which previously excluded parts of personality demand recognition.

The Self is not the everyday ego and not a synonym for an “authentic self.” In Jungian usage it names the psyche as a larger totality and an ordering tendency that exceeds conscious intention. Individuation is the process of becoming a more differentiated and integrated individual in relation to that totality. Nelson and Delmedico translate this into couple work by asking how relationship both supports and frustrates the development of each person.

The partner is not an instrument supplied by the Self for one’s development. The other remains a separate subject with their own fate. That point is clinically necessary because otherwise individuation language can become grandiose: “my growth requires you to accept whatever I do.” A Jungian couple frame should instead ask what development is occurring in each person, what the relationship is demanding, and what actual agreements and consequences follow.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 11–23]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 7, Part Two “Individuation”; CW 9ii, early chapters on ego, shadow, syzygy, and Self]

### 32. The residue of the past and the pull of the future

The source refuses a purely retrospective psychology. Childhood, parental bonds, attachment patterns, cultural roles, and earlier injuries matter. Yet it also asks what is emerging and what the future is “pulling” into form. A crisis may not simply repeat the past; it may expose an old adaptation that can no longer contain present development.

For couple therapy this produces a dual inquiry:

- **causal:** What happened before that made this response intelligible?
- **prospective:** What new capacity, identity, limit, or relation may be trying to emerge?

The prospective question must remain a hypothesis. It should not romanticize distress as destiny or presume that every conflict carries a hidden higher purpose. Sometimes a conflict is a conflict. Sometimes a broken agreement requires direct repair. The Jungian addition is that the clinician remains open to the possibility that symptoms and relational crises also have developmental meaning.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 15–20]

### 33. Individuation in the couple context

The source links relationship and individuation in several ways. Commitment moves a person beyond the familiar world of the family of origin and into sustained contact with someone psychologically different. Intimacy exposes projections, shadow, type one-sidedness, and complexes. A partner can therefore become a persistent challenge to the fantasy that one’s conscious viewpoint is the whole truth.

At the same time, individuation may destabilize established couple roles. If the formerly dependent partner becomes more autonomous, the formerly indispensable caretaker loses a role. If the extravert begins to need solitude or the “rational” partner develops feeling, the old division of labour changes. If one partner questions inherited religious, sexual, career, or family norms, the relationship must either reorganize or face its limits.

Nelson and Delmedico do not guarantee that development brings harmony. In Chapter 6, relationships repeatedly dissolve and coagulate into new forms; in Chapter 5, shadow-vow work may reveal conditions that can no longer be sustained; in Chapter 7, changing identities can require renegotiation of the relationship itself.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 20–25; ch. 5, pp. 89–94; ch. 6, pp. 105–118; ch. 7, pp. 122–136]

### 34. Individuation versus relationship preservation

The distinction is explicit and non-negotiable:

**greater consciousness can deepen a relationship, change it, destabilize it, or reveal incompatibility.**

Staying together is not proof of individuation. Separating is not proof of individuation. A person can use “individuation” to rationalize avoidance or self-centered behaviour; a person can also remain in a relationship in a highly differentiated way. The outcome must be assessed in the concrete circumstances of the people involved.

The source’s alchemical language reinforces outcome neutrality. **Solutio** may describe the dissolving of a relationship already underway. **Separatio** can include breakup, divorce, and the many material and psychological separations that follow. **Coniunctio** is one process among others and, in Chapter 6, the authors explicitly reject a return to fusion as a sustainable goal for mature couples.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 105–118]

### 35. Differentiation

Although “differentiation” is not developed as a standalone systems-theory construct, the process is everywhere in the book. Jung’s primary marriage essay says psychological relationship requires distinction between self and other. Nelson and Delmedico translate this into projection withdrawal, typological development, shadow ownership, separation from families of origin, and the alchemical separatio through which partners sort what belongs to whom.

Differentiation therefore means clearer self-other distinction while remaining capable of relation. It is not emotional distance. The source’s preferred mature image is **separate connectedness**: two persons become three, not one. Each retains individuality and the relationship exists as a third reality to which both contribute.

[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 17, §§325–331]
[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 110–114]

### 36. Individuality and relatedness

The central tension is not autonomy versus dependence in a simple zero-sum sense. The source assumes that relationship can be necessary for psychic life and that individuation requires becoming more distinctly oneself. Intimacy without differentiation risks fusion and role dependency. Differentiation without relatedness can become defensive isolation.

A mature Jungian formulation therefore asks two simultaneous questions:

- What does this person need to own, develop, choose, or refuse as an individual?
- What form of relatedness remains possible when the partner is no longer required to complete, rescue, contain, or mirror the self?

The answer may be a renewed couple bond, a changed structure of partnership, or separation. There is no single prescribed endpoint.

### 37. Alchemy as a developmental meta-framework

Chapter 6 gives the book its most comprehensive model of change. The relationship and each psyche are imagined as alchemical vessels or **retorts** containing **prima materia**, the mixed and often unpleasant material of lived experience. The therapist is a co-tender rather than an engineer who can impose linear progress.

The principal processes are:

- **calcinatio:** heat, conflict, burning away confusion, and emergence of sharper truth;
- **solutio:** dissolving rigid attitudes, ego boundaries, or sometimes the relationship itself;
- **coagulatio:** formation, embodiment, stabilization, or stuckness;
- **mortificatio:** deadness, decay, loss of old forms, despair, and composting;
- **separatio:** discrimination, sorting, individuation, withdrawal of projection, and literal separation where relevant;
- **coniunctio:** union or renewed connection, ideally without abolishing individuality.

The authors also use the colored stages **nigredo**, **albedo**, and **rubedo** and the circular process of **circulatio**. The clinical implication is non-linearity. Insight is not final. A relationship may move from confusion to clarity and then be tested again in daily life.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 100–118]

### 38. Alchemical images as clinical imagination

Chapter 6 contains nine conceptually meaningful images:

1. **Figure 6.1, mining operation:** inner work as extracting and processing difficult material.
2. **Figure 6.2, alchemical bath:** therapist and couple both immersed in the process; containment and heat.
3. **Figure 6.3, calcinatio scene:** purification through heat and the expulsion of folly.
4. **Figure 6.4, king swimming in the sea:** the distressed relationship immersed in solutio.
5. **Figure 6.5, eagle chained to a toad:** spirit or upward movement bound to earthy heaviness, an image for coagulatio and stuckness.
6. **Figure 6.6, crowned couple in a coffin:** mortificatio and the death of an old relational form.
7. **Figure 6.7, egg struck by a fiery sword:** separatio as forceful discrimination and breaking apart what has been fused.
8. **Figure 6.8, king and queen separated with a third image between:** coniunctio as differentiated relatedness rather than fusion.
9. **Figure 6.9, ouroboros in the vessel:** continuing cycles of destruction, renewal, and reconstitution.

These images are not diagnostic tests. Their purpose is to expand the therapist’s capacity to imagine what kind of process is occurring and to tolerate phases that do not resemble linear improvement.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, Figures 6.1–6.9]

---

## Part VI - Relational Field, Therapist Participation and Countertransference

### 39. The transferential field

The source uses **transferential field** and related field language, but its field model is not identical to Schwartz-Salant’s interactive field. In Chapter 1, transference and countertransference describe the mutual projection of unconscious contents and the bond this creates among partners and therapist. The therapist is drawn into the couple’s drama because the partners’ material evokes the therapist’s own memories, wounds, fantasies, moods, bodily reactions, and complexes.

The field is experienced phenomenologically: the room can feel hot, cold, foggy, dead, frantic, seductive, or heavy. A therapist may become unusually sleepy, urgent, angry, frightened, rescuing, judgmental, or mute. Such responses are potential information. They are not objective readings of the couple. The same response may arise from the therapist’s history, current life, type, fatigue, bias, or activated complex.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 24–25; ch. 8, pp. 140–150]

### 40. Relationship as third, field as third, symbolic third

Several forms of “thirdness” need to be kept distinct.

1. **The relationship as third.** Chapter 6 states that when two people come together something new is created: the relationship becomes another retort. This is the clearest Nelson/Delmedico use of “third.”
2. **The transferential field.** This includes the therapist and the mutual unconscious influences generated in treatment.
3. **Schwartz-Salant’s interactive field.** This is a more elaborated post-Jungian theory of an “in-between” area with its own phenomenology, often involving projective identification and alchemical imagery.
4. **The transcendent function.** In Jung, this is the emergence of a new attitude from sustained tension between conscious and unconscious positions. The book mentions the transcendent function but does not develop a conjoint technique around it.
5. **A symbolic third.** Any image, story, or metaphor that reorganizes an opposition may function clinically as a mediating symbol, but the source does not equate every useful symbol with a formal “third.”

Using “third” without specifying which meaning is intended would flatten distinct theories.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 101–102, 112–114]
[Post-Jungian comparison: Schwartz-Salant, *The Mystery of Human Relationship*, chs. 1–5]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 8, “The Transcendent Function”]

### 41. Nelson/Delmedico versus Schwartz-Salant

Schwartz-Salant’s *The Mystery of Human Relationship* explicitly develops the **interactive field** as a “third area” between persons and treats the field as capable of affecting both participants. His focus is often the transformation of chaotic or “mad” states through entering and experiencing the field rather than merely asking who projects what onto whom. Nelson and Delmedico cite him, particularly in Chapter 6, and share an alchemical and field-sensitive stance.

But their book is broader and more specifically oriented to couple therapy. It integrates complexes, type, shadow vows, diversity, individual histories, and the therapist’s soul. It does not make projective identification or the interactive field the master concept. Therefore:

**couple relationship ≠ interactive field; transferential field ≠ Schwartz-Salant’s full field theory.**

[Post-Jungian comparison: Schwartz-Salant, *The Mystery of Human Relationship*, preface and chs. 1–5]
[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 100–118]

### 42. Therapist as participant

The therapist cannot remain untouched. Nelson and Delmedico invoke Jung’s dialectical view that the therapist is affected by the patient and that the whole person of the clinician participates. Chapter 8 makes this a major theme. The therapist is asked to be psychologically porous enough to be influenced while maintaining professional responsibility and boundaries.

Participation includes:

- observing the couple’s interaction;
- noticing bodily and affective responses in oneself;
- tracking spontaneous images, fantasies, and reveries;
- allowing uncertainty rather than forcing technique;
- speaking from genuine human contact when clinically appropriate;
- recognizing one’s own complex activation;
- using supervision, consultation, therapy, and reflective work to metabolize what the couple stirs.

Participation does not mean unrestricted self-disclosure, spontaneous opinion, or abandoning role. The source quotes later Jungian cautions against turning therapy into the therapist’s platform.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 8, pp. 140–150]
[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 16, “Fundamental Questions of Psychotherapy” and “The Psychology of the Transference”]

### 43. Neutrality versus participation

A useful formulation is **disciplined participation**. Classical ideas of neutral observation are insufficient because therapist and couple influence one another. But abandoning neutrality altogether would invite enactment. Nelson and Delmedico want the therapist to acknowledge susceptibility to influence, remain curious about it, and subject it to reflection.

Participation is therefore not taking sides. It is not making the therapist’s feeling the truth of the room. It is not joining one partner’s projection against the other. The more intense the therapist’s certainty, the more reason there may be to ask whether a complex, archetypal role, rescue fantasy, or personal history has been constellated.

### 44. Countertransference and therapist complexes

Chapter 2 gives a concrete developmental account of therapist complexes. A therapist who survived childhood by mediating parental conflict may rush to create peace. One who coped by hiding may become overwhelmed by high conflict. One who froze may become psychologically immobilized. These reactions can look like technique but actually arise from the therapist’s own adaptation.

Chapter 7 extends the same reasoning to bias and social identity. Work with GSRD couples may expose limits in the therapist’s assumptions about gender, sexuality, monogamy, kink, family, religion, or normality. The task is not to pretend to be bias-free but to cultivate self-reflexivity, educate oneself, consult, and refer when one cannot provide sufficiently affirmative care without causing harm.

**Therapist feeling ≠ objective proof about the couple.** Countertransference is data about an interaction between this therapist and this couple at this moment, requiring discrimination.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 2, pp. 39–41; ch. 7, pp. 134–136; ch. 8, pp. 140–150]

### 45. Therapist triangulation and recruitment

The book does not develop a formal systems concept of triangulation, but the clinical danger is present throughout. High-conflict partners may recruit the therapist as judge, rescuer, parent, teacher, moral authority, or witness for the prosecution. Archetypal pairings can also pull the therapist into a complementary role. A hero/helpless couple may invite a therapist to become an additional hero; a parent/child pairing may invite the clinician to scold or rescue.

A source-faithful response is to notice the pull and return attention to the pattern. The therapist can wonder what role is being offered rather than accepting it. This is one reason self-observation is essential.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 3, pp. 49–52; ch. 8, pp. 140–150]

### 46. The therapist as interpreter

The authors are suspicious of expert certainty. Mythic, symbolic, and archetypal interpretations should enlarge imagination, not become declarations about what partners “really” mean. A metaphor may be clinically powerful because it allows a couple to experience a familiar problem differently. Its value is pragmatic and psychological, not proof that the therapist has decoded an objective hidden truth.

This is especially important in conjoint work because an authoritative interpretation can be weaponized. If the therapist tells A that B is “carrying A’s shadow,” B may later use the phrase to invalidate A. If the therapist labels a role as “anima projection,” the terminology may replace dialogue. The book’s repeated stance of curiosity, not-knowing, and vulnerable observation is an antidote to such misuse.

---

## Part VII - Dreams, Symbols, Intimacy and Conflict

### 47. Dreams

Dreams are unmistakably Jungian, but this book does **not** provide a developed method of dream work with couples. The Introduction explicitly lists dreams among areas that could not be fully treated. Chapter 1 notes that dream images can be meaningful; Chapter 7 suggests that strongly gendered or non-gendered dream figures may inform exploration of masculine, feminine, and multiple inner figures. Chapter 8 includes dreams as part of the therapist’s own soul work.

A conservative use in couple therapy would therefore be: receive a dream as the dreamer’s symbolic material; explore its subjective meanings; consider relationship relevance without treating the dream as evidence of the partner’s motives; and avoid assuming that a shared or parallel image proves a “couple unconscious.”

[Nelson & Delmedico: Introduction, pp. 7–8; ch. 1, pp. 13–15; ch. 7, pp. 130–132; ch. 8, pp. 140–150]

### 48. Active imagination

Active imagination is named in the book’s framing as an important Jungian practice but is not developed into a conjoint intervention. No standardized couple active-imagination exercise is presented. It would therefore be inaccurate to attribute a dyadic active-imagination protocol to Nelson and Delmedico.

For routing, active imagination belongs primarily to Jung’s CW 8 and specialist Jungian sources. In File 21 it remains contextual: the authors value imaginal life and spontaneous inner figures, but their couple method works mainly through conversation, embodied awareness, metaphor, narrative, typology, and therapist attunement.

### 49. Metaphor and archetypal imagery

Metaphor is a practical bridge between ordinary couple language and symbolic understanding. Chapter 3 treats spontaneous images such as “iceberg” or “leech” as psychic facts in the Jungian sense: they reveal how an experience is organized imaginally. The therapist can stay with the image, ask what it evokes, and explore its consequences rather than translating it immediately into a technical term.

Archetypal language becomes clinically useful when it de-literalizes conflict. A partner experienced only as “the problem” may also be carrying a recurring figure such as teacher/student, hero/helpless one, wounded/healer, good parent/bad parent, angel/devil, optimist/pessimist, sexual/frigid, fixed/broken. The therapist should introduce such patterns tentatively. The aim is to make the drama visible enough that partners can regain choice.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 3, pp. 45–54]

### 50. Shared symbolic material

A couple may develop recurring stories, images, jokes, rituals, places, songs, objects, or myths that function as shared symbolic material. The source’s alchemical images and shadow-vow idea demonstrate how a relationship can be thought about symbolically. But the presence of shared symbols does not prove a metaphysically independent couple psyche. Symbolic usefulness and factual truth are different claims.

**symbolic meaning ≠ factual truth.**

### 51. Conflict

The source does not treat conflict as a single phenomenon. It may arise from:

- complex constellation;
- projection and misattributed motive;
- typological difference;
- split archetypal roles;
- shadow material;
- developmental pressure toward individuation;
- inherited family or cultural roles;
- actual disagreement, betrayal, boundary violation, or incompatibility.

The alchemical chapter is unusually tolerant of conflict. Calcinatio can describe the heat through which confusion burns away. Separatio can reconstruct an argument in detail. The therapist may allow productive disagreement rather than quickly suppress it. But the source itself puts conditions around “fighting fairly”: no domination, no violence, and absence of the Gottmans’ four destructive patterns in the idealized version of productive fighting.

Conflict is therefore not inherently individuating. Heat can clarify, but it can also traumatize, intimidate, or entrench contempt. Whether conflict is psychologically useful depends on what occurs in it and whether the persons remain sufficiently safe and agentic.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 104–118]

### 52. Recurring conflict patterns

The authors conceptualize repetition through several overlapping Jungian lenses rather than one master “cycle”:

- interlocked complexes;
- projection and projective evacuation;
- archetypal pairings;
- typological oppositions;
- shadow vows and unconscious relational arrangements;
- alchemical phases that the couple resists or overidentifies with.

This is less parsimonious than EFCT’s negative-cycle formulation but more plural. The advantage is depth and multiple symbolic entry points. The risk is overinterpretation. A therapist can generate many plausible Jungian meanings for one argument; the discipline is to remain close to what is observable and useful.

### 53. Communication

Nelson and Delmedico do not offer a generic communication-skills curriculum. Communication improves through better differentiation of internal states, type-aware translation, slower reconstruction of conflicts, narrative listening, shadow ownership, and decreased projection. The source includes concrete communication moves, but they are embedded in deeper formulation.

Examples include repeating a partner’s position accurately, naming the atmosphere, asking what was wanted but not said, inviting a partner to articulate feeling rather than outsourcing it, and identifying when absolutist language can be temporally qualified. These are not presented as a fixed sequence.

### 54. Mind-reading and motive attribution

Projection makes inferred motive feel like observation. A partner may say “you wanted to humiliate me” when the observable fact is “you corrected me in front of our friends.” The first statement may be true, false, partly true, or impossible to establish. Jungian work is useful when it creates distance between the event and the psychic image of the event.

A disciplined formulation asks:

**observation → interpretation → emotional certainty → projection/complex hypothesis → evidence and counterevidence → partner’s account → residual uncertainty.**

This is not scepticism about all intimate knowledge. Partners often know each other well. It is a protection against treating psychic conviction as self-validating fact.

### 55. Listening and dialogue

The source values dialogue but does not formalize Young-Eisendrath’s mindfulness-based dialogue model. Nelson and Delmedico emphasize listening to the partner’s story, becoming a witness to difference, and resisting the urge to convert the other’s experience into one’s own framework. Type work can facilitate dialogue by showing that different modes of consciousness organize experience differently.

Young-Eisendrath goes further by making structured dialogue and mindful witnessing central skills in love between equals. Her model has clearer procedural emphasis on stepping out of enemy-making projection and relating to the other as an equal subject. Nelson and Delmedico’s dialogue is more embedded in symbolic, typological, complex, and archetypal exploration.

[Post-Jungian comparison: Young-Eisendrath, *Love Between Equals*, ch. 4 “Dialogue: Becoming a Mindful Witness”]

### 56. Rupture and repair

“Repair” is not a central technical construct in the Nelson/Delmedico source in the way it is in contemporary attachment therapies. The book describes processes that can contribute to repair: recognition of one’s part, withdrawal of projection, more accurate listening, emotional ownership, changed behaviour, greater tolerance of difference, and reconstitution of the relationship through new vows or a new coniunctio.

But **projection withdrawal ≠ repair**. Repair may require apology, restitution, changed conduct, truth-telling, renewed reliability, explicit renegotiation, or time. A symbolic insight without behavioural change may leave a rupture untouched.

### 57. Intimacy

Intimacy is not fusion. The alchemical argument is explicit: early solutio can produce a blissful loss of boundaries, but mature connection cannot be sustained by continually trying to return to that state. Separatio and differentiation are prerequisites for a more robust coniunctio.

The source’s mature image is being “alone together”: distinct enough to have an interior life and connected enough to remain in relationship. Intimacy therefore includes encounter with otherness. It can be deepened when projections recede and the actual partner becomes more visible.

### 58. Sexuality and desire

Sexuality appears throughout but Chapter 7 is the most explicit. The authors encourage therapists to invite a fuller account of sexual life, especially with GSRD couples, rather than assuming heterosexual, cisgender, monogamous, or conventional sexual scripts. Kink, bondage and discipline, domination and submission, sadism and masochism (BDSM), consensual nonmonogamy, asexuality, queer identities, and changing desires are all treated as legitimate areas for exploration rather than automatic pathology.

Chapter 6 notes that sexual frustration and unspoken desire can generate strong calcinatio. Chapter 7 treats erotic fantasies as potentially meaningful expressions of soul that may or may not fit the existing relationship. The therapist’s task is to make space for exploration and negotiation where possible.

Sexual or romantic desire may contain projection, but it is not reducible to projection. The source does not offer a modern sex-therapy model of desire discrepancy, arousal, pain, or sexual dysfunction. Loss of desire should therefore not be explained automatically as withdrawal of projection, blocked individuation, or archetypal change.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 104–105; ch. 7, pp. 122–136]

---

## Part VIII - Power, Accountability, Safety and Relationship Outcome

### 59. Power

Power appears in several forms but is not treated with the systematic depth found in specialist domestic-violence, coercive-control, feminist family-therapy, or structural approaches. The book addresses power indirectly through role rigidity, hero/helpless pairings, gender norms, cultural privilege, therapist authority, domination in conflict, and the historical limitations of Jung’s gender theory. Chapter 7 makes social and political context especially visible for GSRD couples.

The source is clear that productive conflict excludes domination and violence. It also recognizes the therapist’s power and warns against hiding behind expertise or imposing normative sexual and relationship models. Still, the book does not provide a full power-assessment framework.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 6, pp. 116–118; ch. 7, pp. 122–136; ch. 8, pp. 140–150]

### 60. Mutuality versus equal responsibility

Both partners can participate in a pattern without bearing equal responsibility for what happens. This needs stronger emphasis than the source itself consistently provides.

[LABORATORY METHODOLOGICAL GUARDRAIL]

Mutual influence does not establish equal responsibility for:

- lying or deliberate concealment;
- coercive control;
- intimidation or threats;
- physical or sexual violence;
- non-consensual conduct;
- stalking;
- chronic betrayal;
- control of money, housing, migration status, healthcare, or other material necessities;
- severe power asymmetries that constrain one partner’s agency.

A “mutual complex,” “shared shadow,” “field,” or “alchemical process” formulation must never be used to distribute responsibility symmetrically by default.

### 61. When dyadic interpretation becomes unsafe

Dyadic interpretation becomes unsafe when it converts unilateral harm into a mutual symbolic drama. For example, describing coercive control as “the couple’s power complex,” violence as “calcinatio,” or chronic deception as “shadow material shared by both” can obscure action, agency, and risk.

A minimum reality check is:

1. What exactly happened?
2. Was there consent?
3. Could either partner freely disagree or leave?
4. Was there fear, threat, intimidation, violence, stalking, or material control?
5. Is one person’s account being suppressed by a demand for symmetry?
6. Does conjoint exploration itself increase risk?

These questions are Laboratory guardrails. Nelson and Delmedico do not provide a complete protocol for answering them.

### 62. Safety and suitability for conjoint work

The source does not establish a comprehensive contemporary safety-screening protocol or formal contraindication criteria. It assumes professional clinical competence and occasionally notes that some disclosures or interactions may be unsafe. This is a real limitation for a book positioned as an introduction to couple work.

A reader should therefore not infer from the book that all couples are suitable for conjoint depth work, that open conflict should always be facilitated, or that “staying with the heat” is appropriate regardless of intimidation or violence. The alchemical metaphor only functions clinically inside a sufficiently safe container.

[INTERPRETIVE CAUTION]

### 63. Is saving the relationship the goal?

No. The source is notably clear that preservation cannot be assumed. It challenges the fantasy that the therapist’s job is to rescue the couple and repeatedly criticizes a heroic fixing stance. Relationships may deepen, re-form, or end. The alchemical language of solutio, mortificatio, separatio, and coniunctio explicitly includes dissolution, death of old forms, and literal separation.

This outcome neutrality is one of the most important differences between the book and popular expectations of couple therapy. The therapist serves greater consciousness and care of soul, not a predetermined marital outcome.

[Nelson & Delmedico: ch. 1, pp. 22–25; ch. 6, pp. 105–118]

### 64. Individuation and separation

Separation can be part of an individuation process, but it is not individuation by definition. A person can leave impulsively, repeat an unconscious pattern, or use psychological language to avoid intimacy. Conversely, staying does not mean failure to individuate. The question is whether the person becomes more conscious, differentiated, responsible, and able to relate to reality.

### 65. Compatibility

Compatibility is a practical and relational construct that should remain distinct from attraction, projection, archetypal intensity, type complementarity, and individuation. It includes whether partners can live with each other’s values, goals, boundaries, behaviour, lifestyle, sexual agreements, parenting expectations, financial practices, geography, health realities, and degree of desired commitment.

A relationship can be psychologically fascinating and practically unworkable. It can be archetypally intense and unsafe. It can promote individuation and still end. Conversely, a stable compatible relationship does not need to be symbolically dramatic to be psychologically meaningful.

---

## Part IX - Comparison with Post-Jungian, Attachment, EFCT and EFT Models

### 66. Contemporary Jungian couples therapy versus Jung

Jung supplies much of the conceptual vocabulary but not the contemporary treatment format. Nelson and Delmedico’s project is an adaptation, extension, and critique.

| Dimension | Jung primary | Nelson / Delmedico contemporary couple therapy |
|---|---|---|
| Unit of treatment | Primarily individual psyche in analysis; marriage discussed psychologically in CW 17; transference dyad in CW 16. | Two partners, the relationship, and the therapist are simultaneously held in mind. |
| Projection | Fundamental mechanism through which unconscious contents appear in the object; projection withdrawal is central to differentiation. | Followed between partners and into the therapist; always considered alongside actual interaction and relationship history. |
| Complexes | Affectively charged, relatively autonomous psychic constellations. | Used to track real-time couple activation, reciprocal triggering, bodily shifts, and therapist complexes. |
| Type | Attitudes and four functions describe forms of conscious orientation and compensation. | Used clinically to de-moralize difference, translate communication, and identify inferior-function vulnerability. |
| Shadow | Unrecognized aspects of personality, especially those incompatible with conscious identity. | Extended to relationship roles, cultural expectations, “shadow vows,” and couple-level disavowal. |
| Anima / animus | Historically sex-binary contrasexual archetypes, particularly in CW 7 and CW 9ii. | Retained selectively, explicitly criticized for essentialism, and reimagined through multiplicity and gender fluidity. |
| Individuation | Development of a differentiated person in relation to the Self, especially in the second half of life. | Relationship is one major arena in which individuation may be supported, resisted, or revealed; preservation is not guaranteed. |
| Therapist stance | Dialectical participation, mutual influence, transference/countertransference, dreams, symbolic amplification. | Strong emphasis on therapist subjectivity, somatic and imaginal response, self-reflexivity, and care of the therapist’s soul. |
| Actual interaction | Less developed as a recursive conjoint process. | Immediate couple exchanges are observed and slowed; individual and dyadic processes are integrated. |
| Diversity and contemporary coupling | Historically limited, heteronormative, and gender-essentialist. | Explicitly affirmative toward lesbian, gay, bisexual, transgender, queer, nonbinary, consensually nonmonogamous, kinky, BDSM, and other GSRD relationships. |
| Outcome | Marriage essay emphasizes development from unconscious identity toward psychological relationship. | Relationship can renew, reorganize, or end; greater consciousness does not prescribe one outcome. |

[Primary Jung comparison: Jung, CW 6; CW 7; CW 8; CW 9i; CW 9ii; CW 16; CW 17]
[Nelson & Delmedico: entire source, especially Introduction and chs. 1–8]

### 67. Nelson / Delmedico versus Young-Eisendrath

Polly Young-Eisendrath’s *Love Between Equals* is a closer contemporary relative than either attachment therapy or classical individual analysis, but the models remain distinct.

| Dimension | Nelson / Delmedico | Young-Eisendrath |
|---|---|---|
| Central problem | Unconscious complexes, projection, type one-sidedness, shadow, archetypal roles, alchemical change, and individuation in relationship. | The demands of “personal love” between equals, idealizing projection, disillusionment, enemy-making, and the need for mindful dialogue. |
| Equality | Important but embedded in broader diversity and anti-normative stance. | Foundational social and psychological condition of contemporary intimate love. |
| Projection | One among several major Jungian processes. | Especially central to falling in love, disillusionment, intimate enemy-making, and the path toward seeing the real other. |
| Dialogue | Important but not formalized as the master intervention. | A core developed practice: becoming a mindful witness and maintaining respect across difference. |
| Differentiation | Developed through individuation, separatio, shadow ownership, and type. | Developed through loosening projection, preserving self-respect, and seeing the partner as an equal separate subject. |
| Partner choice | Allows unconscious complexes and typological/archetypal attraction to matter. | More sceptical that people choose a uniquely predetermined partner to complete a psychological task; old emotional dynamics can be activated with many close others. |
| Spiritual path | Relationship can be a sacred site of soul-making and individuation. | “True love” is explicitly framed as a spiritual practice informed by psychoanalysis and Buddhism. |
| Technique | Plural and imaginal, deliberately non-manualized. | More structured around dialogue, witnessing, projection awareness, equality, reciprocity, and “streets of love.” |

The frameworks overlap on projection withdrawal, otherness, development, and respect for the partner as more than an internal object. They differ in emphasis. Young-Eisendrath makes equality and dialogue the organizing centre; Nelson and Delmedico distribute attention across a larger Jungian symbolic ecology.

[Post-Jungian comparison: Young-Eisendrath, *Love Between Equals*, chs. 1–7]
[Nelson & Delmedico: chs. 1–8]

### 68. Nelson / Delmedico versus Schwartz-Salant

| Dimension | Nelson / Delmedico | Schwartz-Salant |
|---|---|---|
| Clinical setting | Explicit conjoint couple therapy. | Human relationship and analytic work more broadly, illustrated through clinical and personal relational material. |
| Field | Transferential field and relationship as alchemical retort/third. | Interactive field is a major theoretical object, a “third area” that affects both participants. |
| Projection | Central but integrated with complex, type, shadow, culture, and behaviour. | Moves beyond a purely “who projects what?” model toward experiencing field states and their transformation. |
| Projective identification | Not a master construct in Nelson/Delmedico. | More important to field dynamics and interpersonal transformation. |
| Alchemy | Chapter 6 is a major clinical meta-framework for couple change. | Alchemy structures the entire account of transformation in human relationship. |
| Therapist participation | Strongly emphasized, especially therapist complexes and soul. | Field participation and tolerance of destabilizing unconscious states are central. |
| Diversity | Explicit 2020s affirmative framework. | Not a central organizing theme of the 1998 text. |

Schwartz-Salant is therefore an important ancestor and amplifier, not a synonym for Nelson and Delmedico’s model.

[Post-Jungian comparison: Schwartz-Salant, *The Mystery of Human Relationship*, chs. 1–10]

### 69. Nelson / Delmedico versus Knox

Jean Knox’s *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis* operates at a different level. Knox revises Jungian archetype theory through developmental psychology, cognitive science, attachment theory, internal working models, trauma, reflective function, and the analytic relationship. Nelson and Delmedico are less concerned with reconstructing the developmental origin of symbolic structures. They retain a more classically archetypal and imaginal clinical language.

| Dimension | Nelson / Delmedico | Knox |
|---|---|---|
| Primary task | Jungian couple practice. | Revision of archetype and analytic process through developmental/attachment models. |
| Archetype | Symbolic, imaginal, bipolar pattern active in couple life. | Reinterpreted developmentally, with emphasis on emergent meaning and image schemas. |
| Attachment | Acknowledged as clinically relevant but not the master explanatory model. | Explicitly integrated with Jungian theory and internal working models. |
| Reflective function | Not a central technical term. | Central to the capacity to represent mental states and analytic change. |
| Couple interaction | Directly observed and worked with. | Not a conjoint couple-treatment manual. |
| Projection | Classical/post-Jungian relational use. | Understood within developmental models of representation and relationship. |

A Knox-informed clinician could add developmental precision to Nelson/Delmedico, but doing so creates an integration that neither source should be made to “own.”

[Post-Jungian comparison: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, chs. 3–7]

### 70. Contemporary Jungian couples therapy versus adult attachment theory

Mikulincer and Shaver organize adult attachment around an evolved behavioural system concerned with protection, proximity, safe haven, secure base, internal working models, emotion regulation, and individual differences in anxiety and avoidance. This is a different theoretical language from Jungian complexes, projection, archetypes, and individuation.

| Dimension | Jungian couple therapy | Adult attachment theory |
|---|---|---|
| Core problem | Unconscious one-sidedness, complexes, projection, shadow, type, symbolic and developmental demands of relationship. | Threat to attachment security, accessibility of attachment figures, working models, and regulatory strategies. |
| Developmental history | Family complexes, inherited roles, personal and collective unconscious, symbolic meaning. | Repeated attachment experiences shaping expectations of self/other and regulation. |
| Projection | Psychic content experienced as belonging to the other; may be archetypal or complex-mediated. | Not a core attachment construct. Biased expectations and working models can influence perception but are not “projection” in Jung’s technical sense. |
| Complex | Affectively charged autonomous psychic constellation. | No direct equivalent. An attachment-related working model or activated attachment system is not a Jungian complex. |
| Regulation | Can be described through containment, complex activation, body awareness, type, and relationship. | Central: hyperactivating and deactivating strategies, proximity seeking, safe haven, secure base. |
| Partner availability | Important as actual relational reality but not a single master variable. | Core to attachment-system functioning. |
| Conflict | May express complexes, projection, type, shadow, individuation, archetypal polarization, or actual disagreement. | Often understood partly as attachment-system activation and regulatory attempts. |
| Change | Greater consciousness, differentiation, integration, symbolic transformation, behavioural and relational reorganization. | Increased security, flexible regulation, revised working models, effective support seeking/caregiving. |
| Individuation | Core Jungian developmental concept. | Not an attachment construct. Personal growth may be facilitated by security, but secure attachment ≠ individuation. |

The same episode can receive both formulations. A partner’s panic when the other withdraws may involve attachment anxiety and also an abandonment complex. The constructs overlap phenomenologically but answer different questions.

[Attachment comparison: Mikulincer & Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1–2, 5–10]

### 71. Contemporary Jungian couples therapy versus EFCT

Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT), in Susan Johnson’s attachment-centred lineage, has a clearer change model and a more structured map of couple distress. It foregrounds attachment threat, negative interaction cycles, underlying vulnerable emotion, accessibility/responsiveness/engagement, corrective emotional interaction, enactment, and bonding.

Nelson and Delmedico foreground a broader depth-psychological field: complexes, projection, shadow, typology, archetypal pattern, individuation, alchemy, soul, symbolic meaning, and therapist participation. They are less protocol-driven and more tolerant of ambiguity and non-linear movement.

| Dimension | Nelson / Delmedico | EFCT |
|---|---|---|
| Organizing theory | Analytical psychology and post-Jungian relational/depth thinking. | Adult attachment theory plus experiential/systemic process. |
| Primary unit | Individuals, relationship, therapist, and field held plurally. | Attachment bond and recursive couple cycle, with each partner’s experience in the cycle. |
| Core distress map | Multiple: complex, projection, shadow, type, archetypal polarization, developmental pressure, actual conflict. | Negative cycle organized around attachment threat and protective responses. |
| Emotion | Important as complex affect, embodied process, shadow, and soul. | Tracked systematically from secondary/protective responses to primary vulnerable attachment emotion. |
| Intervention style | Curiosity, slowing, symbolic amplification, history, body awareness, type translation, alchemical tending, therapist self-reflexivity. | Structured tracking, validation, evocative responding, reframing in attachment terms, enactments, restructuring interaction. |
| Goal | Greater consciousness, differentiation, authentic relation, capacity to undergo transformation; outcome-neutral regarding preservation. | De-escalation and creation/consolidation of a more secure attachment bond when conjoint work is appropriate. |
| Therapist stance | Participating, affected, imaginally and somatically self-aware. | Active process consultant who tracks cycle and shapes corrective interactions while maintaining alliance with both. |
| Symbolic material | Central. | May be used experientially but is not the theoretical centre. |

Overlap exists. Both attend to recursive interaction, emotion, vulnerability, therapist attunement, and corrective relational experience. But the theoretical objects differ. Translating a Jungian complex into an “attachment wound” by default loses information; translating an EFCT cycle into “mutual projection” loses the attachment-regulatory logic.

[EFCT comparison: Johnson, *The Practice of Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy*, chs. 1–9; Johnson, *Attachment Theory in Practice*, chs. 1–3, 6–7]

### 72. Complex cycle versus EFCT negative cycle

This distinction is mandatory because the two formulations can look similar on the surface.

**Jungian question:** Which complexes are constellated, what projections organize the experience, what disowned qualities are carried by the other, and what developmental meaning may be present?

**EFCT question:** What attachment threat is occurring, what primary vulnerable emotions and needs are being protected, and how do each partner’s protective responses cue the other’s response to create a self-reinforcing cycle?

A criticism-withdrawal sequence could therefore be mapped differently:

- Jungian complex formulation: criticism constellates a shame/inadequacy complex; withdrawal is a complex-driven defence; the pursuer may project indifference or abandonment; typological processing differences may intensify the pattern.
- EFCT formulation: the pursuer protests disconnection to restore responsiveness; withdrawal protects against failure, rejection, or overwhelm; each response confirms the other’s attachment alarm.

These are competing or complementary hypotheses, not translations.

### 73. Relational field versus EFCT negative cycle

A **relational or interactive field** refers to an emergent in-between psychological reality, especially in post-Jungian field formulations. A **negative cycle** is a patterned sequence of observable and experiential attachment-protective responses. The field may be experienced as an atmosphere; the cycle can be tracked behaviourally and emotionally in sequence.

One can say “the room feels dead and hopeless” as a field observation while also mapping “A criticizes → B withdraws → A intensifies → B shuts down” as a cycle. Neither statement makes the other redundant.

### 74. Contemporary Jungian couples therapy versus Greenberg’s Emotion-Focused Therapy

Leslie Greenberg’s Emotion-Focused Therapy shares with Nelson and Delmedico an experiential respect for felt emotion and therapist attunement, but its technical language is different. Greenberg distinguishes types of emotion, emotion schemes, markers, tasks, and transformation of maladaptive primary emotion through access to adaptive emotion. His 2015 text also includes a chapter on couples, but the lineage should remain distinct from Johnson’s attachment-centred EFCT.

| Dimension | Jungian couple therapy | Greenberg Emotion-Focused Therapy |
|---|---|---|
| Basic unit | Psyche in relationship, symbolic and archetypal dynamics, dyadic interaction. | Emotion processing and emotion schemes within an experiential-humanistic framework. |
| Complex | Autonomous affective-symbolic constellation. | No direct equivalent; emotion schemes organize emotion, memory, appraisal, bodily experience, and action tendency differently. |
| Primary/secondary emotion | Not the central Jungian taxonomy. | Important technical distinction. |
| Change | Consciousness, differentiation, integration, projection withdrawal, symbolic transformation, alchemical reorganization. | Access, acceptance, symbolization, regulation, transformation by new adaptive emotion, new narrative. |
| Meaning | Often symbolic, mythic, prospective, and archetypal. | Emerges from embodied emotion and experiential processing. |
| Couple application | Explicitly dyadic throughout Nelson/Delmedico. | One application area within a broader Emotion-Focused Therapy model. |

**complex activation ≠ emotion-scheme activation.** They may organize some of the same experience but arise from different metapsychologies.

[EMOTION-FOCUSED THERAPY comparison: Greenberg, *Emotion-Focused Therapy: Coaching Clients to Work Through Their Feelings*, chs. 2–5, 6–9, 13]

### 75. Type formulation versus attachment formulation

Consider a generic observation: after criticism, Partner B becomes quiet, leaves the room, and says they need time.

A typological hypothesis might ask whether B requires introverted processing and becomes less articulate under extraverted pressure. An inferior-function hypothesis might ask whether criticism has exposed an undifferentiated feeling or thinking position. A complex hypothesis might ask whether shame, authority, or inadequacy has been constellated. An attachment hypothesis might ask whether B is deactivating to manage attachment threat and anticipated failure. An EFCT formulation might place withdrawal within a pursue-withdraw cycle. A behavioural formulation might ask whether B reliably returns to the conversation or simply refuses engagement.

No single formulation automatically supersedes the others. The best explanation is the one that fits evidence, predicts the next observable moves, and helps the people understand and alter what is happening without erasing responsibility.

---

# Jacobi: Classical Map of Analytical Psychology

## Source and Edition Note

### 1. Source and Edition Note

The primary source for this file is the Project copy of Jolande Jacobi's *The Psychology of C. G. Jung: An Introduction with Illustrations*. The actual file identifies the author as Dr. Jolande Jacobi and the translator as K. W. Bash, S.M. (Chic.). It was published in London by Routledge and Kegan Paul Ltd. The copy is the fifth English edition, dated 1951. The title-page verso records first English publication in 1942, followed by editions in 1943, 1944, 1946 and 1951. The work was translated from the German *Die Psychologie von C. G. Jung*.

The book is organised more simply than many later textbooks. After prefaces and a short orientation headed "The Psychology of Jung," the substantive exposition has three numbered chapters in the actual source:

1. **The Nature and Structure of the Psyche**, source pp. 19-67.
