2. **Laws of the Psychic Processes and Operations**, source pp. 68-76.
3. **The Practical Application of Jung's Theory**, source pp. 77-173.

These are followed by a biographical sketch, a bibliography of Jung's writings and an index. The 1951 edition also contains nineteen plates and a number of schematic diagrams. Jacobi explicitly warns that her diagrams are aids to thought rather than literal anatomical maps of the psyche. That warning is methodologically central to this Laboratory file.

The Project PDF is text-searchable and unusually usable for a historical scan. Most prose is recoverable. Limitations remain: there are OCR errors in punctuation, ligatures and some words; hyphenation and line breaks occasionally distort sentences; mathematical or diagrammatic labels can be displaced; and illustrations require visual rather than text-only reading. Printed pagination is generally recoverable. This reconstruction therefore treats page-level provenance as reliable when clear, but does not invent a page or wording when the extraction is uncertain.

**Jacobi's role in the Laboratory:** this is the principal **classical secondary map**. It is valuable because it assembles many of Jung's dispersed concepts into a single teachable architecture. It is not the evidentiary authority for what Jung said at every period. For author-specific historical claims, the relevant Jung primary text controls.

[Jacobi: *The Psychology of C. G. Jung*, title pages; contents; Prefaces; source pp. 7-18]

## Jacobi's Role and Epistemic Status

### 2. Who Is Jacobi in Relation to Jung?

Jacobi belongs to the early, close circle of interpreters who helped present analytical psychology as a coherent body of thought. In the first English-edition preface she describes the book as a concise introduction intended to facilitate access to Jung's much larger corpus, and she thanks Jung and Toni Wolff for their support of the project. This proximity makes the book historically important. It also creates a predictable epistemic risk: a clear expositor can make a developing body of thought look more finished and architectonic than it actually was across decades of Jung's writing.

Jacobi should therefore be used in two ways at once. First, she is a high-value guide to the **classical configuration** of analytical psychology as it had been organised by the 1940s and early 1950s. Second, every concise Jacobi formulation should remain tagged mentally as a secondary formulation unless it is checked against Jung's primary text. Her book often quotes Jung and was designed as an exposition of his work, but quotation and systematisation are not the same as authorship.

Her own prefaces make this tension explicit. She repeatedly insists that Jung's psychology is empirical, revisable and "in flux," while at the same time trying to provide a concise and comprehensive picture of its central structure. The book is therefore best understood as an intentionally stabilising teaching map of a theory that Jacobi herself says should not be treated as a closed system.

[Jacobi's systematisation: Prefaces to the first and fourth English editions, source pp. 7-11]

### 3. What Kind of Book Is This?

The book functions simultaneously as an **introduction, teaching text, conceptual synthesis and systematisation**. It is not simply a commentary on one Jung volume, nor a historical account of how Jung's ideas changed. It draws definitions and formulations from multiple works and places them into a coherent order: first structure, then dynamics, then practical application and individuation.

That architecture has a strong pedagogical logic. Chapter I tells the reader what the psyche contains and how consciousness is organised. Chapter II tells the reader how the system moves. Chapter III tells the reader what happens when these principles are encountered in dreams, psychotherapy and individuation. This sequence makes analytical psychology look like an integrated system of structure, dynamics and development.

Epistemically, that is both the book's strength and its principal limitation. A student can see the relations among ego, functions, attitudes, personal and collective unconscious, complexes, archetypes, persona, shadow, anima/animus and Self much more quickly than by reading Jung chronologically. The cost is that distinctions developed at different dates and under different problems are presented as parts of one contemporaneous architecture. File 01 should therefore be activated whenever the research question concerns **development through time**, rather than the classical conceptual map.

[Jacobi: contents; "The Psychology of Jung"; chs. I-III]

## Coverage of This File

This file reconstructs Jacobi's map, preserves its internal logic, and then compares that map with primary Jung and selected later developments. It does not reproduce Jacobi's text, and it does not attempt to convert the 1951 framework into contemporary developmental psychology, attachment theory or couple therapy.

## How to Use This File

Use File 13 first when the question is, "Where does this concept sit in the classical Jungian architecture?" Then route to the relevant primary or later file. Do not use File 13 as the last word on a disputed Jung concept, as a chronology, or as a diagnostic checklist for a person.

---

# Part I - Jacobi's Architecture of the Psyche

### 4. Jacobi's Overall Architecture of the Psyche

Jacobi begins with the widest unit, the **psyche**, which she presents as the totality of conscious and unconscious psychological processes. The conscious and unconscious are not independent substances but opposed and complementary aspects of one dynamic whole. The ego participates in this totality but does not equal it.

Her first diagrams make the system visually memorable. Consciousness is a relatively small zone within a much larger psyche. The ego is the centre or subject of the field of consciousness. Immediately around or below conscious contents lies the personal unconscious, containing forgotten, repressed and subliminal material. Beyond that lies the collective unconscious, grounded in inherited possibilities of psychic functioning and expressed through typical forms and archetypal patterns. Jacobi repeatedly cautions that the circles and layers are heuristic representations, not literal spatial compartments.

The architecture then gains two other dimensions. One is **orientation**: consciousness differentiates through attitudes and functions, with a dominant or superior function and less differentiated functions, including an inferior function more closely linked to the unconscious. The other is **development**: persona, shadow, anima/animus and Self become important as consciousness encounters the unconscious more deliberately. These are not all introduced as equivalent "parts." Persona is a function-complex of social adaptation; complexes are affectively charged organisations; archetypes are collective structural patterns; shadow and anima/animus are characteristic figures or organisations of unconscious material; Self is the archetypal representation and ordering principle of totality.

[JACOBI'S CLASSICAL SYSTEM MAP]

```text
PSYCHE = totality of conscious and unconscious processes
|
+-- CONSCIOUSNESS
|   |
|   +-- EGO: centre/subject of the conscious field
|   +-- conscious attitude: extraverted or introverted orientation
|   +-- functions: thinking, feeling, sensation, intuition
|   +-- PERSONA: function-complex of outer/social adaptation
|
+-- PERSONAL UNCONSCIOUS
|   |
|   +-- forgotten, repressed, subliminal material
|   +-- personally acquired affective constellations
|   +-- complexes and personally conditioned shadow material
|
+-- COLLECTIVE UNCONSCIOUS
    |
    +-- inherited possibilities/forms of psychic functioning
    +-- archetypal patterns and typical modes of reaction
    +-- archetypal images when these patterns take representational form
    +-- figures increasingly important in individuation:
        shadow -> anima/animus -> further archetypal figures -> Self

DYNAMIC PRINCIPLES ACROSS THE WHOLE:
opposites <-> psychic energy/libido <-> compensation <-> symbol formation
<-> conscious-unconscious dialogue <-> individuation
```

This map is a Laboratory reconstruction of **Jacobi's systematisation**. It is not a diagram Jung himself published in this form.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 19-67; ch. II, pp. 68-76; ch. III, especially pp. 109-164]

---

# Part II - Consciousness, Ego and Personal Unconscious

### 5. Consciousness

Jacobi defines consciousness relationally. A content is conscious insofar as it stands in a relation to the ego. Consciousness is therefore not identical with thinking or intelligence. One can be conscious of a feeling, perception, wish, image or anxiety. Nor is consciousness coextensive with the psyche. In Jacobi's diagrammatic language it is a small island relative to a much larger unconscious field.

The important developmental point is that consciousness is differentiated rather than simply "present." It acquires structure through the formation of the ego, the differentiation of an attitudinal orientation and the development of the psychological functions. Jacobi regards this differentiation as a major achievement of the first half of life. It allows effective adaptation to external reality. The problem arises when a necessary differentiation hardens into one-sidedness and its neglected opposites accumulate energy in the unconscious.

Consciousness is thus both indispensable and limited. Individuation does not require its dissolution. On the contrary, Jacobi repeatedly stresses the need for a sufficiently firm conscious standpoint to withstand and assimilate unconscious contents. A weak or poorly differentiated ego is not made "whole" by flooding it with archetypal material.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 19-24, 25-43; ch. III, pp. 124-147]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 6, "Definitions"; CW 7, *The Relations between the Ego and the Unconscious*]

### 6. Ego

The ego is the centre and subject of consciousness, not the centre of the total psyche. Jacobi follows Jung's classical definition of ego as a relatively continuous complex of representations constituting the centre of the conscious field. It gives conscious experience continuity, identity and a standpoint from which the individual relates to inner and outer objects.

This position explains several distinctions that the Laboratory must preserve:

- the ego is a **complex**, but it is not simply one complex among others because it organises conscious identity;
- the ego can participate in unconscious processes but does not own or exhaust them;
- a strengthened ego is necessary for development, yet ego expansion is not the same as greater wholeness;
- the ego can become inflated by identifying with archetypal contents;
- the Self is not a larger ego. The Self denotes or symbolises a totality that includes the ego but exceeds it.

Jacobi's developmental model therefore has a double movement: the ego first requires differentiation and stability; later it must learn that it is not sovereign over the psyche. Individuation is not ego defeat but a relativisation of ego-centredness within a wider psychic order.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 20-24; ch. III, pp. 124-160]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 9ii, ch. I "The Ego" and ch. IV "The Self"]

### 7. The Personal Unconscious

Jacobi distinguishes the personal unconscious by origin. It contains material that belongs to the individual's acquired life: forgotten experiences, repressed contents, subliminal perceptions, thoughts and feelings, and other material that is not currently related to the ego. This layer is biographical even when its contents are not voluntarily accessible.

Complexes are the most important organised structures in this domain. They are not merely stored memories. They are affectively charged constellations that can influence perception, behaviour and association with partial autonomy. In practical analysis, Jacobi says the personal unconscious ordinarily needs to be worked through before deeper archetypal material can be approached safely and meaningfully.

The personal unconscious is therefore not a synonym for "bad material" and not merely a repository of repression. It includes forgotten and subliminal contents that may be neutral or useful. Nor is its boundary with the collective unconscious absolute. Jacobi's own presentation recognises transitions in which personal imagery deepens and takes on archetypal form.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 20-24, 51-57; ch. III, pp. 97-113]

### 8. The Collective Unconscious

Jacobi presents the collective unconscious as the inherited foundation of psychic functioning, shared at the level of species rather than acquired through an individual's biography. Its contents are not personal memories. They are typical possibilities, structural forms and modes of reaction that become visible through recurring symbolic patterns.

Her account is recognisably classical and strongly constitutional. She links the collective unconscious to inherited brain structure and to universal or very widespread human situations: fear, danger, conflict, parent-child relations, sexuality, birth, death and encounters with light and darkness. The collective unconscious supplies typical response-patterns that compensate the narrower, individually adapted standpoint of consciousness.

A crucial correction is needed for modern readers: Jacobi does **not** use "collective" to mean cultural consensus, social norms or shared discourse. Culture may clothe archetypal patterns in specific images, but the collective unconscious in her map is deeper than culture and is supposed to make cross-cultural recurrence possible.

Likewise, the collective unconscious is not best summarised as a warehouse of inherited pictures. Jacobi sometimes writes in image-heavy language characteristic of her period, but she also distinguishes underlying archetypal form from its representational manifestations. That distinction must be retained and tested against primary Jung rather than flattened into "universal images."

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 21-24, 57-67]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 9i, "The Concept of the Collective Unconscious"; CW 8, "On the Nature of the Psyche"]

---

# Part III - Collective Unconscious, Complex and Archetype

### 9. Complexes

Jacobi's treatment of complexes connects Jung's early experimental psychology to the later architecture of analytical psychology. A complex is an affectively charged group of ideas and associations organised around a meaningful nucleus. Complexes reveal themselves indirectly through disturbances of association, slips, disproportionate reactions, repeated themes, symptoms and dream material. Their defining feature is not simply content but **autonomy**: the complex can behave as if it were a partial personality with its own tendency and affective force.

The ego itself is a complex, but a complex need not be conscious or ego-syntonic. A complex can temporarily seize the field, narrow consciousness and alter the person's behaviour. Jacobi therefore treats complex theory as evidence that the psyche is not a unitary conscious will.

She also moves from personal to archetypal depth. A complex may be personally formed and biographically conditioned while having an archetypal nucleus or connection. That does not make "complex" and "archetype" synonymous. The complex is a concrete affective organisation in an individual psyche; the archetype is a collective structural factor that may organise or energise many possible personal formations.

Compared with Jung's early association studies, Jacobi gives the complex a clear place in a larger system. What is gained is orientation. What can be lost is the experimental and clinical history through which Jung first discovered the autonomy of complexes. File 03 restores that primary emphasis.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 51-57; ch. III, pp. 86-101]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory"; early association studies in CW 2]

### 10. Archetypes

Jacobi treats archetypes as central structural factors of the collective unconscious. They are inherited possibilities or formal dispositions through which recurrent human situations are apprehended and represented. They organise experience, behaviour and symbolic production, but they are not inherited biographies, characters or ready-made mythological pictures.

Her account is strongest when she differentiates the invisible organising factor from its concrete manifestation. The archetype itself is not exhausted by any image. When an archetypal factor becomes active it can clothe itself in historically and individually variable images: mother, king, dragon, wise old person, tree, cave, sun, child, circle and countless other forms. The image is conditioned by culture and individual life; the underlying patterned possibility is what Jacobi calls archetypal.

Jacobi also emphasises numinosity. When constellated, archetypal contents may carry unusual intensity and a sense of significance greater than ordinary ego concerns. That intensity helps explain why archetypal images can reorganise attention and behaviour, but it also creates danger of inflation if the ego identifies with them.

The source draws analogies between archetypes and instinctual patterns, and between typical psychic form and biological organisation. This is a historically important classical formulation. Later writers, especially Knox, challenge any reading that makes archetypes hard-wired inherited representational content. The Laboratory therefore preserves Jacobi's classical account without treating it as the only later Jungian model.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 57-67]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 9i; CW 8, "Instinct and the Unconscious" and "On the Nature of the Psyche"]

### 11. Archetype and Image

Jacobi's text supports a distinction between **archetypal structure** and **archetypal representation**, even though her terminology is not always as consistently formalised as later theoretical discussions. She describes the archetype as a latent or structural factor and the archetypal image as the pictorial, mythic or symbolic form through which it becomes experienceable.

This distinction can be represented as follows:

| Level | In Jacobi's classical map | Typical manifestation |
|---|---|---|
| Archetype | Inherited formal possibility or pattern of psychic reaction; not fully representable in itself | Inferred from recurring forms and effects |
| Archetypal image | Historically and personally shaped representation of an activated archetypal factor | Dream figure, mythic image, symbolic motif, religious or artistic figure |
| Personal image | Biographically specific representation | Actual mother, father, friend, event or memory in a dream |

Jacobi describes how an image may deepen in analysis: a personally recognisable figure can acquire broader symbolic meaning and eventually reveal mythological or collective dimensions. This does not mean the personal image was "really" only an archetype. It means that one representation can participate in more than one level of meaning.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 57-67; ch. III, pp. 111-116]

### 12. Persona

Jacobi presents the persona as a **function-complex of adaptation to the outer world**. It is the organised social face through which a person occupies roles, meets collective expectations and functions in society. It is necessary. A person without an adequately differentiated outer attitude cannot simply dispense with persona and remain psychologically mature.

The danger is **identification**. If the individual takes the role, title, profession, reputation or social attitude as the whole personality, the rest of the psyche is excluded from conscious life. Jacobi links an over-rigid persona to compensatory unconscious pressure. The more one-sided the external mask becomes, the more archaic and undifferentiated the inner counter-position may become.

Persona is therefore not equivalent to falseness. A role can be genuine and necessary without being the whole person. The problem is not having a persona; it is failing to distinguish persona from individuality.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 40-47; ch. III, pp. 136-145]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 7, Part One, ch. III "The Persona as a Segment of the Collective Psyche"]

---

# Part IV - Persona, Shadow, Anima/Animus and Self

### 13. Shadow

Jacobi gives the shadow a prominent place in her explicit individuation sequence. It represents the aspects of the personality that have remained unrecognised, rejected, undeveloped or incompatible with the conscious attitude. Because the conscious personality is necessarily selective, some capacities and tendencies remain "in shadow."

The shadow is not simply evil. Jacobi explicitly allows positive as well as negative qualities to be shadowed. A socially approved person can disown aggression, but a timid or constricted person may equally disown vitality, courage or creativity. The shadow can appear inwardly as a dream figure or outwardly through projection onto another person. It can also take more collective, mythic forms when deeper archetypal material is activated.

Jacobi connects shadow work with typology. The inferior or neglected functions often carry shadow qualities because conscious identity has been organised around the superior function and preferred attitude. But shadow and inferior function are not identical: the shadow is a broader organisation of unowned qualities, while the inferior function is a specific typological concept.

In individuation, the first major task is to distinguish oneself from the shadow while accepting that its contents belong to one's own psyche. "Integration" here does not mean acting out every impulse. Jacobi contrasts conscious recognition and responsibility with both repression and identification.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 126-131]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 9ii, ch. II "The Shadow"]

### 14. Anima and Animus

Jacobi reproduces the classical gendered formulation: anima is the contrasexual "soul-image" in a man and animus the corresponding masculine figure in a woman. These figures mediate between ego-consciousness and deeper unconscious life and are strongly involved in projection, attraction, fascination and conflict.

This account must be retained historically rather than silently modernised. Jacobi understands anima and animus through binary assumptions about male and female psychology characteristic of classical analytical psychology. She also draws on Emma Jung and on cultural claims that no contemporary Laboratory file should treat as empirical fact merely because they occur in a classical source.

Within Jacobi's own system, however, several distinctions are important. The anima/animus figure is not simply the actual partner. The actual partner becomes a carrier for projection when sufficiently resonant with the unconscious image. The soul-image can also appear in dreams, fantasies and visions. Its forms vary enormously and can be positive or negative, guiding or obstructive. Jacobi also places anima/animus in compensatory relation to persona: the more rigidly one-sided the external attitude, the more primitive and powerful the inner contrasexual figure may remain.

The developmental task is differentiation. One must learn to recognise the projected factor as psychic content rather than confusing it with the actual other person. In Jacobi's classical account this withdrawal of projection creates a more conscious relation to the inner figure and a more realistic relation to the outer partner.

**Historical-context note:** later Jungian files address major revisions of gender, sexuality, subjectivity and relational theory. File 13 preserves Jacobi's formulation because changing it would destroy the historical map.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 131-144]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 7, Part Two, ch. II; CW 9ii, ch. III "The Syzygy: Anima and Animus"]

### 15. The Self

The Self is the culminating organising concept in Jacobi's classical map. It denotes psychic totality and an ordering centre that includes conscious and unconscious processes. The ego is related to this totality but does not coincide with it. In individuation the ego's previous centrality is relativised as the person experiences a wider order in which conscious and unconscious opposites can be held together.

Jacobi gives the Self archetypal status and describes its manifestations through symbols of totality: mandalas, circles, quaternities, centred arrangements and other images that organise multiplicity around a midpoint. Such images are not the Self itself. They are symbolic manifestations through which the totality becomes psychologically experienceable.

Her account also gives the Self a developmental place. After confrontation with shadow, anima/animus and related archetypal figures, a unifying symbol can emerge that represents a more inclusive order. Jacobi describes this as the "last station" in the way of individuation, but this phrase must not be mistaken for a once-and-for-all final achievement. Her wider discussion retains conflict, tension, continued life tasks and the limits of human completion.

The Self therefore does not mean "the true self" in the contemporary everyday sense. Nor is it merely a set of authentic preferences. It is a theoretical and symbolic construct for the psyche as a whole and for the ordering tendency through which opposites may be related.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 145-160]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 9ii, ch. IV "The Self"; CW 9i, mandala and individuation essays]

### 16. Psyche as a System

Jacobi's special value lies in showing relations rather than isolated definitions. The classical system can be summarised as a set of nested and interacting distinctions:

- **ego** organises the conscious field;
- **persona** mediates adaptation between ego and social world;
- **personal unconscious** contains biographical material and complexes outside current awareness;
- **collective unconscious** supplies deeper, inherited structural possibilities;
- **archetypes** organise recurring forms of experience and symbolic representation;
- **shadow** gathers what conscious identity excludes or leaves undeveloped;
- **anima/animus** in the classical model mediates a deeper relation to unconscious life and is especially active in projection;
- **Self** represents psychic totality and ordering beyond the ego;
- **psychic energy** describes the dynamic distribution of value and tension through the system;
- **compensation** links conscious one-sidedness with unconscious counter-tendency;
- **symbol formation** provides a form in which opposites can be related rather than simply cancelled;
- **individuation** describes the developmental process of differentiation, confrontation, assimilation and movement toward greater totality.

This is precisely why Jacobi remains useful. She makes the parts legible as a system. The corresponding caution is equally important: **a clear map is a secondary systematisation, not necessarily Jung's own final diagram.**

[Jacobi: chs. I-III]


---

# Part V - Psychological Type, Energy and Compensation

### 17. Psychic Energy / Libido

Chapter II gives Jacobi's concise statement of Jung's energic model. She uses **libido** in Jung's broadened sense of psychic energy, not as a synonym for sexual drive. Psychic energy is an abstract way of describing the intensity and value of psychic processes as inferred from their effects. When actualised it appears through wishes, affects, will, drives, performances and directed activity; when potential it appears as readiness, disposition, possibility or attitude.

The crucial point is relational rather than substantive. Psychic energy is not a special psychic fluid. It describes changes in the distribution of value among contents and systems. A conflict becomes dynamically important when opposed tendencies carry significant energetic charge. If one side monopolises conscious investment, the opposite may gain energy unconsciously. If the energy becomes obstructed, symptoms, complexes, regression or other disturbances may result.

Jacobi's system therefore combines a structural map with an energic one. The map tells us *where and in what form* a process appears; the energy model tells us *how strongly values are invested, displaced, blocked and transformed*. Symbol formation matters because symbols can mediate transformation of energy from one mode or level into another.

She distinguishes progression and regression. Progression is movement through successful conscious adaptation and differentiation. Regression follows obstruction or failure of adaptation and activates older or unconscious material. Regression is not simply pathology. It can be restorative if it reconnects consciousness with excluded sources and supplies symbols from which a new progression becomes possible.

[Jacobi: ch. II, source pp. 68-76]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 8, "On Psychic Energy"]

### 18. Compensation

Compensation is a basic regulatory principle in Jacobi's map. Consciousness necessarily takes a partial standpoint; the unconscious tends to produce material that adds what the conscious position excludes, neglects or overvalues. Compensation is therefore not a simple mechanical reversal and not a promise that the psyche will automatically become balanced.

A compensatory dream may correct conscious overconfidence, but it may equally support a consciously undervalued position. Jacobi's examples show that the sign of a dream depends on the total conscious situation. The same symbol can have different compensatory significance for different people, and the same person can receive different compensations as the situation changes.

This is why Jacobi's dream theory resists fixed dictionaries. One first establishes the conscious position and personal context, then asks what the unconscious material contributes to the whole psychic situation. Compensation is functional and relational. It is not "the unconscious always says the opposite."

At the level of type, the undifferentiated attitude and inferior function compensate the preferred conscious attitude and superior function. At the level of development, unconscious material can challenge a persona that has become too rigid. At the level of individuation, compensation contributes to the dialogue of opposites from which a new symbolic position may emerge.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 23-24, 38-41; ch. II, pp. 70-76; ch. III, pp. 88-96]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 8, dream essays; CW 7]

### 19. Opposites

Jacobi treats opposition as a structural condition of psychic life: consciousness/unconscious, extraversion/introversion, thinking/feeling, sensation/intuition, superior/inferior, persona/soul-image, progression/regression, spirit/instinct and other polarities. Energy presupposes difference. A living psyche cannot be reduced to one pole without generating a compensatory problem.

The aim is not to erase polarity. Jacobi emphasises preservation of former values together with recognition of their contrary. Development therefore asks the ego to tolerate tension long enough for a broader form of organisation to become possible. This logic anticipates the transcending function discussed later in the book.

[JACOBI'S OPPOSITES MODEL]

```text
one-sided conscious position
        |
        v
unconscious counter-position becomes energised
        |
        v
conflict / obstruction / regression / symbolic production
        |
        v
conscious engagement with both sides
        |
        v
possible new standpoint that contains more of the polarity
```

The model is dynamic rather than moral. Neither pole is automatically good or bad. A consciously "spiritual" position can be compensated by bodily or instinctual material; an impulsive attitude can be compensated by ordering or ethical demands.

[Jacobi: ch. II, source pp. 68-76; ch. III, pp. 77-85, 145-164]

### 20. Enantiodromia

Jacobi explicitly includes **enantiodromia**, borrowing the Heraclitean term used by Jung for the tendency of a one-sided position to turn into its opposite. She embeds it within self-regulation and the law of opposites. The point is not that every attitude inevitably flips in a predictable cycle. It is that extreme one-sidedness builds the conditions under which the excluded opposite can constellate with unusual force.

Enantiodromia is therefore a warning against taking successful adaptation as permanent psychic equilibrium. A value that has carried life for one period can become one-sided when conditions change. Development may require recognising the relative value of its contrary rather than abolishing the original value.

[Jacobi: ch. II, source p. 70]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 7 and CW 8; CW 6 "Definitions"]

---

# Part VI - Symbol, Dreams and Transcendent Function

### 21. Symbol

The symbol is one of Jacobi's central linking concepts because it joins structure, dynamics and development. She treats symbols as spontaneous products of the psyche capable of expressing something not yet adequately captured by a known concept. A genuine symbol has an excess of meaning. It cannot be fully translated into discursive language without remainder.

Jacobi distinguishes symbol from sign and allegory. A sign refers to a known thing by convention or substitution. An allegory restates a known content in another form. A symbol points toward a meaning not yet exhausted by the available concept and participates in both conscious and unconscious dimensions. This is why a symbol can transform rather than merely label experience.

She also gives symbols an energic function. A symbol can act as a "transformer" because it gives form to previously blocked or undifferentiated psychic energy. A dream image can both express a conflict and change the individual's relation to it. The image is thus not decorative. It is part of the process by which the psyche reorganises itself.

| Symbol | Sign |
|---|---|
| Expresses more than can presently be stated conceptually | Refers to a known, defined content |
| Arises from the interaction of conscious and unconscious processes | Can be conventional and consciously assigned |
| Retains ambiguity and multiple possible meanings | Ideally has a stable referent |
| May transform psychic energy by creating a new relation to a problem | Primarily indicates or denotes |
| Requires context and cannot be exhausted by one interpretation | Can often be decoded once the convention is known |

Jacobi's own treatment is especially retrieval-friendly because it links symbol to archetype without equating them. The archetype is the structural core or organising possibility; the symbol is a concrete, living expression that can carry that archetypal factor in a given psyche and situation.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 111-120, 153-164]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 6, "Definitions"; CW 8, "The Transcendent Function" and "On Psychic Energy"]

### 22. Dreams

Dreams occupy a central practical place in Jacobi's Chapter III. They are autonomous products of the psyche and a primary route to unconscious material. Their value rests on the assumption that the unconscious is functionally related to consciousness. Dream interpretation therefore begins with the conscious situation rather than with a universal code.

Jacobi presents dreams as potentially compensatory, reductive or prospective. A reductive dream may bring an inflated conscious attitude down to scale. A prospective dream can sketch a possible future development, not as prophecy but as an anticipatory configuration of tendencies already active in the psyche. The dominant rule remains compensation: the dream contributes what is needed to understand the whole situation more adequately.

She also presents a formal method for reading many dreams as small dramas: setting or dramatis personae, exposition of the problem, peripety or turn, and lysis or outcome. This is a heuristic, not a claim that all dreams have the same literary structure. More importantly, Jacobi insists that a single dream rarely supports broad conclusions. Series of dreams provide a more reliable picture because successive material corrects and develops earlier interpretations.

Interpretation can occur on **object level** or **subject level**. Object-level interpretation concerns the dreamer's relation to actual people or events. Subject-level interpretation treats dream figures as personifications of tendencies or factors in the dreamer's own psyche. Jacobi does not abolish the object level in favour of the subject level. Where a real relationship is important, both may need consideration.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 88-111]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 8, "General Aspects of Dream Psychology" and "On the Nature of Dreams"]

### 23. Amplification

Jacobi contrasts Jung's **amplification** with unrestricted free association. Amplification stays close to the dream image and enriches it through personal associations, analogous imagery, myths, religious motifs, art, cultural parallels and other relevant material. The aim is to clarify the range of meaning carried by the image without abandoning it for an indefinitely extending chain of associations.

The method is especially important when dream material is archaic or archetypal. The analyst may contribute parallels that the dreamer could not know, but the parallel is not a verdict imposed from outside. It is a way of placing the image in a wider field of human symbolic production. The personal situation remains decisive for what, if anything, the parallel means in that particular analysis.

Jacobi's diagrams of amplification are didactic: they depict the analyst repeatedly circling back to the central dream elements and connecting their possible meanings. This is a good example of her systematising style. It gives a clear picture of a method that in Jung's own clinical writings is more dispersed and context-dependent.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 100-107]

### 24. Psychological Types

Jacobi's presentation of type remains close to Jung's classical framework. She distinguishes two attitudes, **extraversion** and **introversion**, and four functions, **thinking, feeling, sensation and intuition**. Thinking and feeling are rational or judging functions because they evaluate. Sensation and intuition are irrational or perceiving functions because they apprehend without the same kind of rational judgement.

The superior function is the best differentiated and most readily available to conscious will. An auxiliary function can support it. The inferior function is least differentiated and most closely connected to the unconscious. Jacobi also emphasises that the unconscious attitude compensates the conscious one: an extraverted conscious standpoint tends to have an introverted unconscious counter-position and vice versa.

Type in this classical account is an orientation model, not a four-letter identity. It describes habitual direction and mode of psychic functioning. It does not imply that a person possesses only one function, that functions are skills ranked by performance, or that type is fixed by a modern psychometric inventory.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 24-43]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 6, ch. X "General Description of the Types" and ch. XI "Definitions"]

### 25. Inferior Function

For Jacobi the inferior function is the least differentiated function in the conscious hierarchy and remains comparatively autonomous, archaic and entangled with the unconscious. It is therefore not merely the function at which a person performs worst. A highly trained skill can coexist with typological inferiority, and a poorly practised skill does not by itself establish the inferior function.

Its importance is developmental. The first half of life requires differentiation of the principal function and effective adaptation. Later, the neglected side of the personality presses for recognition. Jacobi links this especially to midlife disturbances in otherwise capable people. What once secured adaptation may no longer be sufficient, and the inferior function can appear through symptoms, affect, projection and difficult relationships.

Jacobi's treatment is substantial but less elaborated than Marie-Louise von Franz's later account of the inferior function. File 14 should be used for that deeper later development.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 27-43]
[Later development: File 14, von Franz]

### 26. Typology and Individuation

Jacobi uses typology dynamically rather than descriptively. Type differentiation explains how the ego establishes a viable conscious standpoint; individuation then requires a more conscious relation to what that standpoint excluded. The inferior function is therefore one route through which the unconscious challenges one-sided adaptation.

Her developmental claim is not that all four functions become equally strong or consciously interchangeable. Rather, further differentiation of neglected functions broadens the personality while preserving the fact of individual type. The goal is not typological neutrality but less compulsive one-sidedness and greater capacity to relate to the unconscious opposite.

This is also relationally important. Jacobi notes that opposite types often project neglected qualities onto each other. Conflict can then be misread as purely objective fault when part of its intensity comes from one's own undeveloped counter-position.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 38-43; ch. III, pp. 126-131]

### 27. Projection

Jacobi defines projection as an unconscious displacement or appearance of subjective psychic content in an object. Her most useful formulation is process-based: projection is not ordinarily a deliberate act. A person experiences the projected quality as belonging to the other until differentiation and self-recognition make its subjective contribution visible.

Projection is therefore not synonymous with factual error. The carrier of a projection can really possess some of the perceived quality. The analytic question is whether the emotional charge, symbolic meaning or total attribution exceeds what the object alone explains, and what subjective content has become attached to it.

Jacobi gives projection a central role in shadow, anima/animus and relationship. Withdrawal of projection means recognising a previously unconscious factor as belonging to one's own psyche. It does not mean concluding that the other person is innocent, unreal or merely a screen. Mature interpretation must preserve both subject and object.

| Projection | Conscious attribution |
|---|---|
| Occurs without deliberate intention | Deliberate judgement or hypothesis |
| Subjective content is experienced as if located in the object | Observer knows the claim is their own assessment |
| Often carries disproportionate affect or fascination | May be emotionally neutral or proportionate |
| Withdrawal requires self-recognition and differentiation | Revision requires ordinary evidence and reasoning |
| Can coexist with real qualities in the object | Can also be correct or incorrect, but is not unconscious by definition |

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 107-111, 126-144]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 6, "Definitions"; CW 7; CW 9ii]

### 28. Transference

Jacobi treats transference briefly within her account of psychotherapy. She recognises it as projection within the analytic relationship but does not make it the sole or universally necessary motor of treatment. Her classical presentation stresses the **dialectical encounter** between two real psychic systems. Both analyst and patient can be changed by the encounter, and the analyst's personality and level of development matter.

She also presents a historically specific contrast with Freudian technique, arguing that Jung did not regard intense transference to the analyst as indispensable in every case. The important process is the actual working-through of unconscious relations and projections, whether they are constellated centrally in the analytic dyad or elsewhere in life.

File 11 should be used for Jung's primary and much more complex treatment of transference and coniunctio. Jacobi's few pages are orientation, not a sufficient transference theory.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 84-87, 138-140]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 16, "The Psychology of the Transference"]

---

# Part VII - Individuation and Wholeness

### 29. Individuation: Core Definition

Jacobi defines individuation as becoming the individual one is, in the sense of realising one's distinctive psychological totality rather than merely conforming to collective identity. She explicitly distinguishes it from narrow individualism. Individuation does not remove social responsibility; it aims at a person who can occupy a genuine place in community because he or she is less governed by unconscious collective identification and projection.

The process requires a sufficiently differentiated ego, confrontation with personal unconscious material, withdrawal of projections and progressive engagement with archetypal factors. It aims toward a more conscious relation between ego and Self. Jacobi describes this as movement toward totality, but not toward a painless or flawless state. Conflict and suffering remain part of life.

Her language can sound teleological because the Self appears as an ordering centre and the process is described as having directional form. Yet she repeatedly insists on individual variation. The method gives direction, not an identical route or outcome for every person.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 123-164]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 7, Part Two; CW 9i, individuation essays; CW 9ii]

### 30. Is Individuation a Stage Sequence?

In this particular 1951 edition, **yes, Jacobi does present a recognisable staged heuristic**, more explicitly than Jung does in any single universal formula. She says the course of individuation can be "roughly plotted" and then identifies characteristic archetypal figures associated with principal stages. The sequence she develops is approximately:

```text
established conscious ego / persona and adaptation
        |
        v
confrontation with personal unconscious and projections
        |
        v
SHADOW
        |
        v
ANIMA / ANIMUS (classical soul-image)
        |
        v
further archetypal figures, including mana-personality motifs
and Wise Old Man / Great Mother configurations
        |
        v
SELF / unifying symbol / mandala imagery
```

This is one of the most important findings for File 13. A common "Jungian stages" model is not simply fabricated from nothing: Jacobi does systematise the process in stage-like form. But its epistemic status must be stated correctly.

1. It is **Jacobi's classical systematisation**, supported by selected Jung material.
2. Jacobi herself calls the plotting rough and stresses individual variation.
3. Jung's primary corpus is historically broader, less linear and less consistently stage-like.
4. The sequence should not be turned into a diagnostic ladder on which every person's development can be located.
5. Later developmental Jungians substantially revise the assumption that individuation is primarily an adult sequence beginning after ego adaptation.

Therefore the Laboratory should neither deny that Jacobi presents a sequence nor attribute that sequence to Jung as an invariant law.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 126-160]

### 31. Integration

Jacobi uses integration to describe the incorporation of previously unconscious meaning into the functioning personality. It has several steps: the unconscious content becomes available; consciousness notices and holds it; its meaning is investigated; it is related to the whole situation; the individual accepts responsibility for it; and eventually the new understanding becomes more organically assimilated.

Integration is not the same as identification. Identification collapses the distinction between ego and a psychic content. A person can identify with persona, shadow, an archetypal figure or a numinous image and thereby become inflated or possessed. Integration preserves differentiation while allowing the content to contribute to the whole.

| Integration | Identification |
|---|---|
| "This belongs to my psyche and I must relate to it." | "This is what I am, without remainder." |
| Preserves ego-content differentiation | Collapses differentiation |
| Increases responsibility and flexibility | Can produce inflation, possession or rigidity |
| Allows symbolic meaning to be assimilated | Tends to literalise the content |

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 97-99, 124-160]

### 32. Wholeness

Wholeness in Jacobi is a regulative idea of psychic totality, not psychological perfection. The whole includes opposites, unconscious material, limitation and continuing tension. A more individuated person is not one who has eliminated conflict but one who can hold a more differentiated relation to the conflicting factors without compulsive projection or one-sided identification.

Jacobi's mandala material is particularly important here. Centred symbols represent order in multiplicity. Their symmetry should not be confused with a perfectly harmonious life. The symbolic centre organises difference; it does not abolish difference.

Wholeness also remains asymptotic. Jacobi's rhetoric sometimes sounds final when she speaks of the Self as the last station, but her broader account of suffering, ongoing development, mortality and the limits of consciousness contradicts any interpretation of individuation as complete psychic mastery.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 145-160, 169-173]

### 33. Transcendent Function

Jacobi uses the older translation "transcending function" for the capacity by which opposed positions can be brought into a new symbolic relation. She explicitly clarifies that "transcending" does not mean metaphysical transcendence. It means a transition from one psychic attitude to another through a complex function involving symbolic formation.

The sequence is:

```text
conscious position + unconscious counter-position
             |
             v
        sustained tension
             |
             v
symbol capable of expressing something of both
             |
             v
new standpoint not reducible to either original pole
```

Jacobi links the transcendent function closely to the unifying symbol and to mandala imagery in advanced individuation. Primary treatment belongs in File 08 because Jung's 1916 essay is more nuanced and not restricted to a late-stage mandala process.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 153-164]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 8, "The Transcendent Function"]

### 34. Active Imagination

Active imagination appears in two distinct but related places. In Chapter I Jacobi distinguishes it from artistic fantasy and intuition: active imagination deliberately raises, gives life to and holds images from the unconscious. In the later individuation discussion she compares it with imaginal practices in alchemy and yoga, while insisting that it is not ordinary fantasy or aimless daydreaming.

Its function is to give emerging unconscious material sufficient form and sustained attention for a dialogue with consciousness to occur. The person does not simply invent an entertaining story. The task is to observe, engage and represent inner facts without either passive surrender or premature rationalisation.

Jacobi does not provide a full technical manual. File 06 and File 09 should therefore be used for primary Jung's treatment of active imagination and symbolic method.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 42-44; ch. III, source pp. 162-165]

---

# Part VIII - Psychotherapy and Development

### 35. Psychotherapy

Jacobi presents Jungian psychotherapy as both treatment and a possible path of personality development. Her German framing of *Heilsweg* deliberately carries the double sense of healing and a wider movement toward psychological wholeness. The second meaning does not imply that every patient requires intensive individuation work. Jacobi repeatedly distinguishes ordinary treatment aims from the deeper path appropriate to some people when adaptation alone no longer resolves the problem.

The analytic relationship is dialectical. Analyst and patient are two psychic systems in reciprocal interaction. The analyst cannot remain a purely anonymous technician because his or her own personality affects the field. This creates a high ethical demand: the analyst should not lead another further than the analyst's own development permits.

Jacobi summarises four methods associated with Jung: the association method for identifying complexes; historical symptom analysis; anamnestic reconstruction of the development of the problem; and analysis of the unconscious through dreams and related material. These should not be mistaken for a rigid treatment protocol. Her purpose is to show a widening of depth and scope.

Dreams, fantasies and visions are central once work with the unconscious begins. The analyst offers interpretations and amplification, but an interpretation is not considered adequate merely because it is theoretically elegant. It must fit the patient's experience and survive correction by subsequent unconscious material.

[Jacobi: ch. III, source pp. 77-123]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 16, Part One; CW 8 dream essays]

### 36. Development across Life

Jacobi organises development around a broad first-half/second-half distinction. The first half of life requires separation from childhood dependence, strengthening of ego-consciousness, differentiation of the dominant function and attitude, establishment of a persona, work, sexuality, family and social adaptation. This is not "mere conformity" in her map. It is a developmental necessity.

In the second half, the one-sidedness that made adaptation possible can itself become limiting. Neglected functions, the unconscious counter-attitude, shadow material and larger questions of meaning become more urgent. The developmental task shifts from expansion in the external world toward a more conscious relation to inner life and mortality.

Jacobi does not present this as a rigid age-based programme. She notes that many people do not complete the tasks of the first half and therefore cannot simply leap to advanced individuation. The developmental distinction is functional and symbolic, not a calendar schedule.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 38-43; ch. III, pp. 123-173]
[Jung primary comparison: CW 8, "The Stages of Life"; CW 17, "The Development of Personality"]

### 37. Relationship and Marriage

Relationship is not a separate major chapter in Jacobi, but it appears repeatedly where projection, type and anima/animus are discussed. Type differences can become containers for projection of neglected functions and attitudes. An intimate partner can become the carrier of shadow or soul-image material. Attraction can therefore be partly a meeting with an actual other and partly an encounter with one's own unrecognised psychic possibilities.

Jacobi's anima/animus discussion explicitly touches partner choice and marriage. In her classical gendered model, a person may be attracted to someone who can carry a strongly constellated unconscious image. Development requires increasing differentiation between the real partner and the projected image.

This is useful but incomplete for contemporary couple work. It does not provide a model of attachment regulation, recursive interaction cycles, repair sequences, power asymmetry or dyadic co-construction. File 12 gives the primary Jung reconstruction of marriage; Files 18, 20, 21 and the applied files provide later relational frameworks.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 38-40; ch. III, pp. 131-144]

### 38. Religion and Symbolic Life

Religion is important in Jacobi because religious images provide historically developed symbolic forms for archetypal experience, Self symbolism, rebirth, sacrifice, quaternity and the union of opposites. She treats psychic reality as the psychologist's domain and argues against forcing metaphysical conclusions from psychological observations.

Her treatment is nevertheless strongly sympathetic to the religious function. She sees symbolic and religious traditions as repositories of forms through which deep psychic processes have been experienced and expressed. A modern person who has lost living access to inherited symbols may need to rediscover their psychological meaning rather than simply return to unexamined belief.

The Laboratory should retain Jacobi's distinction: psychology can study religious images as psychic facts without deciding their ultimate theological truth. File 13 should not be used as a theology file.

[Jacobi: ch. III, especially source pp. 145-169]

### 39. Synchronicity

Synchronicity does **not** receive a substantive place in Jacobi's main three-chapter architecture in this 1951 English edition. The term is not developed as a core component alongside psyche, type, complexes, archetypes, energy, dreams and individuation. It should therefore not be retrofitted into the map merely because synchronicity later became a well-known Jungian concept.

For Notebook B, synchronicity should be routed to Jung's primary work in CW 8 if a research question requires it. Its relative absence here is itself informative: Jacobi's classical systematisation is centred on psychological structure, dynamics, symbol and development rather than on the later psyche-matter problem.

[Jacobi: main text, chs. I-III; no substantive synchronicity section]
[Jung primary route: CW 8, "Synchronicity: An Acausal Connecting Principle"]


---

# Part IX - Jacobi versus Jung's Historically Developing Corpus

### 40. Jacobi's Classical Conceptual Hierarchy

The following table is designed for retrieval. "Definition in Jacobi" means Jacobi's 1951 systematisation, not an assertion that Jung used exactly the same formulation at every date.

| Concept | Definition in Jacobi | Relation to other concepts | Primary Jung anchor | Later development |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ego | Centre/subject of the conscious field; a relatively continuous complex of representations | Organises consciousness; must distinguish itself from persona and unconscious contents; included within Self | CW 6 "Definitions"; CW 9ii ch. I | Developmental ego theory in Fordham; relational revisions in later schools |
| Persona | Function-complex for adaptation to social/outer reality | Mediates ego-world relation; overidentification evokes compensatory inner one-sidedness | CW 7, "Persona as a Segment of the Collective Psyche" | Later differentiated from Winnicott's false self and social-construction accounts |
| Personal unconscious | Personally acquired forgotten, repressed and subliminal material | Contains complexes and shadow-related material; first depth ordinarily addressed in analysis | CW 7; CW 8 complex theory | Developmental/object-relational accounts add early relational organisation |
| Collective unconscious | Inherited foundation of typical psychic functioning | Basis of archetypal patterns and collective symbolism | CW 9i; CW 8 | Knox recasts archetypal organisation developmentally; cultural-complex theory adds a distinct level |
| Complex | Affectively charged, relatively autonomous constellation | Can organise symptoms, projection and association; may have archetypal core | CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory" | Developmental school links complexes more strongly to early object relations; cultural complexes extend level of application |
| Archetype | Inherited formal/structural possibility of typical experience and representation | Organises archetypal images; belongs to collective unconscious | CW 9i; CW 8 | Knox's emergent/image-schema account; archetypal school shifts emphasis toward image |
| Shadow | Unrecognised, rejected or undeveloped aspects of personality, personal and sometimes collective | Often first major individuation confrontation; projected; overlaps but is not identical with inferior function | CW 9ii ch. II | Later social/cultural shadow theories broaden contextual scope |
| Anima | In Jacobi's classical binary model, feminine soul-image in a man | Mediates inner world; carrier of projection; compensatory to persona | CW 7; CW 9ii ch. III | Major later gender and relational revisions |
| Animus | In Jacobi's classical binary model, masculine soul-image in a woman | Mediates inner world; carrier of projection; compensatory to persona | CW 7; CW 9ii ch. III | Major later gender and relational revisions |
| Self | Totality and ordering centre of the psyche, symbolically represented | Includes ego but exceeds it; culmination of classical individuation map | CW 9ii ch. IV; CW 9i mandala essays | Fordham places Self dynamics in infancy; later schools contest reification and hierarchy |
| Introversion | Attitude in which subjective factor takes priority in orientation | Paired with extraversion; conscious and unconscious attitudes compensate each other | CW 6 ch. X | Beebe later combines attitudes with functions into expanded eight-function dynamics |
| Extraversion | Attitude in which object/external factor takes priority in orientation | Paired with introversion; conscious and unconscious attitudes compensate each other | CW 6 ch. X | Later type systems operationalise differently; not identical to MBTI scoring |
| Functions | Thinking, feeling, sensation, intuition as basic forms of conscious orientation | Superior, auxiliary, less differentiated and inferior positions | CW 6 | von Franz elaborates inferior function; Beebe develops eight function-attitudes/archetypal positions |
| Symbol | Living image that expresses more than a known sign and can transform psychic energy | Mediates conscious-unconscious relation; vehicle of transcendent function | CW 6 "Definitions"; CW 8 | Later hermeneutic, semiotic and developmental accounts modify assumptions |
| Compensation | Unconscious contribution that balances or complements conscious one-sidedness | Fundamental to dreams, type and self-regulation | CW 8 dream essays | Later clinical schools often retain idea but frame process differently |
| Projection | Unconscious attribution/displacement of subjective psychic content into an object | Central to shadow, anima/animus, transference and relationship | CW 6 "Definitions"; CW 7; CW 16 | Schwartz-Salant emphasises reciprocal field and projective processes |
| Transcendent function | Symbol-forming transition in which opposed positions generate a new standpoint | Relates opposites; important to individuation and unifying symbols | CW 8, "The Transcendent Function" | Later clinical work often makes it more dialogical, embodied or relational |
| Individuation | Becoming the individual one is through differentiation and a widening relation to unconscious totality | Relativises persona and ego identification; in Jacobi roughly proceeds through shadow, anima/animus and Self | CW 7; CW 9i; CW 9ii | Fordham makes individuation developmental from early life; later relational theories contest purely intrapsychic sequencing |

[Jacobi: chs. I-III]
[Laboratory synthesis: primary and later routing columns]

### 41. Jacobi's Classical Psyche Map

[JACOBI CLASSICAL SYSTEMATISATION]

```text
                                  PSYCHE
            (totality of conscious + unconscious processes)
                                     |
             +-----------------------+------------------------+
             |                                                |
      CONSCIOUS FIELD                                  UNCONSCIOUS FIELD
             |                                                |
          [EGO]                               +----------------+---------------+
             |                                |                                |
  +----------+-----------+          PERSONAL UNCONSCIOUS           COLLECTIVE UNCONSCIOUS
  |                      |                    |                                |
PERSONA             TYPE/ATTITUDE         COMPLEXES                     ARCHETYPAL PATTERNS
outer adaptation    superior function     biographical affect            typical inherited forms
social role         auxiliary function    forgotten/repressed            numinous symbolic potential
                    inferior function     material                       |
                                                                            |
                                             +------------------------------+
                                             |
                                  INDIVIDUATION-RELEVANT FIGURES
                                             |
                                  shadow -> anima/animus ->
                                  further archetypal figures -> Self

CROSS-CUTTING DYNAMICS:
psychic energy/libido -> opposition -> compensation -> regression/progression
-> dream/symbol production -> amplification/interpretation -> transcendent function
-> possible new conscious standpoint
```

The map is deliberately asymmetrical. Persona belongs primarily to conscious outer adaptation. Complexes belong primarily to the personal unconscious but may be rooted in archetypal patterns. Shadow can include personal and collective dimensions. Anima/animus and Self are archetypal figures or ordering constructs in the classical account, not anatomical compartments.

[Jacobi: chs. I-III]

### 42. Outer and Inner Orientation

Jacobi's model distinguishes but does not moralise outer and inner orientation. **Outer adaptation** requires a differentiated conscious attitude, viable function, social role and persona. Without these, the individual may remain poorly anchored in reality. **Inner development** requires relation to the unconscious, recognition of projections, engagement with neglected functions and symbolic material, and eventual differentiation of a wider personality from collective identity.

The error is to convert this into "outer = false" and "inner = true." Jacobi treats a functioning persona as necessary and warns against prematurely entering deep unconscious work before ego adaptation is sufficiently established. Equally, she treats exclusive external adaptation as incomplete when it suppresses the counter-requirements of the psyche.

| Outer orientation | Inner orientation |
|---|---|
| Social adaptation and role | Relation to unconscious contents |
| Persona | Shadow, soul-image and deeper archetypal material |
| Conscious superior function and attitude | Compensatory inferior function/counter-attitude |
| Work, social reality, responsibilities | Meaning, symbolic life, individuation |
| Risk: persona identification and one-sidedness | Risk: inflation, withdrawal, archetypal possession |

A mature classical position requires relation to both.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 24-47; ch. III, pp. 123-173]

### 43. The Developmental Arc in Jacobi

The book does present a coherent developmental arc, though it is constructed across its three chapters rather than announced as one simple theory of development.

```text
childhood / relative undifferentiation
        -> ego formation and differentiation of consciousness
        -> preferred attitude and superior function
        -> persona and social adaptation
        -> increasing one-sidedness or conflict in later life
        -> encounter with personal unconscious / complexes / projections
        -> shadow confrontation
        -> anima/animus differentiation in classical formulation
        -> archetypal widening and confrontation with mana/inflation
        -> Self symbolism / unifying symbol / greater totality
        -> later-life meaning, mortality and relation to collective humanity
```

The first half and second half therefore have different emphases. This is not a modern lifespan-development model and does not describe infant attachment, mentalisation, affect regulation or dyadic co-development. It is a classical analytic arc from adaptation toward individuation.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 38-43; ch. III, pp. 123-173]

### 44. Jacobi versus Jung's Chronological Development

Jacobi's system gains **clarity, compression and relational visibility**. Jung's concepts are dispersed across early association experiments, the break with Freud, typology, energy theory, dream studies, later archetype theory, alchemy, religion and psychotherapy. Jacobi gathers these into one architecture. For teaching and retrieval this is a major advantage.

What the architecture can obscure is **chronology**. Jung's meanings change, expand and sometimes remain internally plural. "Archetype," "Self," "individuation," "libido" and even the relation between ego and unconscious are not historically static. The editorial notes to Jung's Collected Works show repeated revisions and additions. CW 7, for example, preserves earlier versions precisely because the formulations changed. CW 6's definition of Self was added later to a work originally published in 1921. A system map can hide those developments.

| Dimension | Jung primary corpus | Jacobi's 1951 systematisation |
|---|---|---|
| Form | Essays, monographs, lectures, revisions across decades | One compact organised exposition |
| Chronology | Historically layered and sometimes contradictory | Largely synchronic, concept-to-concept map |
| Definitions | Often context-dependent and revised | Stabilised for teaching |
| Individuation | Multiple descriptions across works | More explicit sequence of characteristic stages |
| Archetype | Several formulations and evolving distinctions | Organised as part of collective-unconscious architecture |
| Type | Full historical and phenomenological treatment in CW 6 | Condensed to usable structural model |
| Clinical method | Distributed across papers and cases | Summarised as four methods plus dream work and individuation |
| Advantage | Historical depth, ambiguity, primary authority | Orientation, teaching clarity, retrieval value |
| Risk | Difficult to integrate without extensive reading | Can look more final and systematic than Jung actually was |

Methodological conclusion: **use Jacobi to locate the problem; use Jung to settle what Jung actually argued in the relevant period.**

[Jung primary comparison: CW 6, 7, 8, 9i, 9ii, 16, 17]
[Laboratory routing: File 01]

### 45. Jacobi versus Jung on Complexes

Jacobi gives complexes a clear structural place between ego-consciousness and deeper archetypal organisation. Her exposition stresses autonomy, affect and the usefulness of association experiments, but the complex is integrated into a system whose later centre of gravity is archetype, Self and individuation.

Jung's primary complex theory is historically earlier and more empirically rooted. The association experiments and clinical observations show how complex autonomy was discovered before the later architecture of archetypes and Self was fully developed. Reading only Jacobi can make complex theory look like one settled component of a later system; reading Jung restores its foundational importance.

**Route:** File 03 for the autonomous psyche and complex theory.

[Jacobi: ch. I, source pp. 51-57; ch. III, pp. 86-101]
[Jung comparison: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory"]

### 46. Jacobi versus Jung on Archetypes

Jacobi organises archetype, collective unconscious, instinct, image and symbol into a relatively coherent hierarchy. This is especially useful for preventing the crude equation "archetype = mythological character." She shows that concrete images are manifestations of deeper patterning.

Primary Jung remains more variable. Across CW 8 and CW 9i he moves among descriptions of inherited form, pattern of behaviour, psychoid factor, analogy with instinct and the distinction between archetype-as-such and representations. Jacobi's map selects and harmonises these strands. It should not be taken as proof that all Jung's formulations resolve into one stable definition.

**Route:** File 06 for primary archetype, collective unconscious, symbol and active imagination.

### 47. Jacobi versus Jung on Self and Individuation

Jacobi's account is more sequential and more teachable than Jung's corpus taken as a whole. She places shadow, anima/animus, mana-related figures and Self into a recognisable progression and associates the Self with mandala and unifying-symbol imagery.

Jung supplies the primary foundations, but his treatments vary by text. CW 7 emphasises differentiation from collective psyche and relation to unconscious figures. CW 9i presents individuation through mandala and case material. CW 9ii treats ego, shadow, syzygy and Self in a more explicitly structured order. Later alchemical works develop totality and opposites in different symbolic languages. Jacobi turns this dispersed material into a relatively linear map.

**Route:** File 04 for ego/persona/unconscious/individuation; File 07 for shadow/anima-animus/projection/Self; File 08 for transcendent function and opposites.

### 48. Jacobi versus Jung on Psychological Type

Jacobi compresses CW 6 into a functional model: two attitudes, four functions, superior/auxiliary/inferior differentiation and unconscious compensation. This is an effective orientation but cannot replace Jung's extensive phenomenology of the eight function-attitudes or his historical discussion of the type problem.

Jacobi's treatment also makes type serve the larger developmental system. Type explains adaptation and one-sidedness; individuation explains why the excluded side later returns. That is useful for the Laboratory, but type research should still route to File 05 and CW 6.

### 49. Jacobi versus Jung on Psychotherapy

Jacobi gives a coherent summary of Jungian psychotherapy as dialectical, context-sensitive and oriented to both symptom relief and, where appropriate, individuation. Her four-method outline and treatment of dreams, amplification and analyst participation are highly useful for retrieval.

Jung's primary clinical writings are less reducible to one procedural schema. CW 16 includes different periods, different patient needs, distinctions among confession, elucidation, education and transformation, and detailed work on transference. Jacobi captures the classical centre but not the full range.

**Route:** File 10 for psychotherapy and dialectic; File 11 for transference.

### 50. Jacobi and the "Classical School"

The label **classical school** is a later post-Jungian taxonomy, not Jacobi's own self-description. Andrew Samuels, writing decades later, distinguishes classical, developmental and archetypal schools by their relative emphases in theory and clinical practice. Jacobi fits many features later associated with a classical orientation: strong weight on archetype, Self, symbolic material, amplification, individuation and the relation of ego to a transpersonal or collective psyche.

This placement should remain contextual rather than retrospective rebranding. Jacobi's book predates the mature school differentiation Samuels describes. It is better to say that her exposition became exemplary of what later writers would recognise as a classical Jungian organisation.

[Post-Jungian comparison: Samuels, *Jung and the Post-Jungians*, ch. 1]

---

# Part X - Later Post-Jungian Revisions

### 51. Jacobi versus Fordham

Jacobi's developmental centre of gravity is adult consciousness. Ego and persona must first become sufficiently differentiated; deeper individuation then becomes a later task, especially when first-half adaptation becomes one-sided. The Self appears most prominently as the totality and ordering centre disclosed through the later individuation process.

Fordham changes the developmental frame. In *Explorations into the Self* he investigates Self processes in infancy and develops **deintegration and reintegration**: an original or primary Self deintegrates into partial systems that meet objects and experience, then reintegrates in enriched form. The consequence is not a minor extension of Jacobi's map. It relocates individuation and Self dynamics into the earliest developmental life rather than treating them mainly as achievements of mature analysis.

| Classical Jacobi map | Fordham development |
|---|---|
| Strong first-half/second-half architecture | Individuation-related Self processes begin in infancy |
| Self most visible as goal/ordering totality in adult individuation | Primary Self is developmental starting point as well as organising totality |
| Ego formation precedes deeper individuation | Ego develops within repeated deintegration-reintegration processes |
| Archetypal system primarily intrapsychic/symbolic | Early object relations and infant development become central |

Fordham should therefore be read as a developmental revision, not as evidence that Jacobi secretly contained an infant model.

[Post-Jungian comparison: Fordham, *Explorations into the Self*, chs. 1-3]
[Route: File 15]

### 52. Jacobi versus Knox

Jacobi's archetypal architecture assumes inherited collective structures that express themselves in symbolic forms and typical patterns. It offers little account of how early relationships, cognitive development and environmental learning build specific psychic structures.

Knox directly addresses that gap. In *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis* she argues that Jung's writings contain several different archetype models and that they should not be fused uncritically. Drawing on developmental psychology, cognitive science and attachment theory, she proposes archetypal organisation as **emergent** from gene-environment interaction rather than as preformed inherited imagery. Image schemas provide one developmental model for the formal, non-representational aspect of archetype; internal working models and attachment experience explain how relational meanings acquire specific content.

| Classical Jungian map in Jacobi | Knox's later development |
|---|---|
| Collective unconscious as inherited psychic foundation | Developmental systems emphasis on emergence |
| Archetype framed primarily as inherited structural form | Archetype-as-such compared with image-schema scaffolding |
| Symbolic and mythic parallels central | Cognitive/developmental research central to explanatory model |
| Adult individuation foregrounded | Early attachment, internal working models, reflective function and agency foregrounded |
| Intrapsychic map is primary | Intrapsychic and interpersonal development are inseparable |

Knox is not merely "Jacobi updated." She changes the explanatory model and imports a different empirical literature.

[Post-Jungian comparison: Knox, chs. 2-7]
[Route: File 17]

### 53. Jacobi versus Beebe

Jacobi's typology remains close to the classical hierarchy of functions. One attitude and one superior function organise consciousness; auxiliary functions are partly available; the inferior function is least differentiated and closely tied to the unconscious. Her interest is how this asymmetry contributes to compensation and individuation.

Beebe retains Jung's eight function-attitudes but develops a much more elaborate model in which the eight forms of consciousness occupy **archetypal positions** and are carried by corresponding archetypal complexes. His work explicitly presents an eight-function, eight-archetype model and explores "shadow" function-attitudes beyond the four-function hierarchy normally foregrounded in classical presentations.

| Jacobi's classical typology | Beebe's later typology |
|---|---|
| Four functions plus introversion/extraversion | Eight explicit function-attitudes |
| Superior, auxiliary and inferior differentiation | Eight positions with archetypal role-patterns |
