Fordham sometimes brings development and individuation very close, but he does not make every developmental event equivalent to the highest Jungian meaning of individuation. The safest reading distinguishes levels.

At the broadest level, individuation names the becoming of an individual through differentiation of Self processes. At a more specific Jungian-clinical level, it can name conscious development of the ego's relation to unconscious totality. At a still more culturally elaborated level, it can include symbolic, ethical, spiritual, and historical meanings associated with adult Self realization.

The same word therefore carries developmental breadth and adult specificity. Fordham's theory is strongest when these levels remain distinguishable. Otherwise feeding, toilet training, adult moral conflict, and mystical experience would all become undifferentiated examples of the same process.

## 42. Lifelong Development

[LABORATORY RECONSTRUCTION BASED ON FORDHAM]

```text
early Self processes
        |
        v
infancy: repeated deintegration / reintegration in bodily and object experience
        |
        v
childhood: differentiation, play, Self representations, ego development
        |
        v
adolescence and adult adaptation: stronger ego, social roles, object relations
        |
        v
continuing Self processes and revision of established organization
        |
        v
adult individuation: conscious relation to unconscious, symbols, opposites, and Self
```

This is not Fordham's own stage diagram. It is a routing map that preserves the continuity he argues for while refusing to invent fixed developmental stages.

## 43. First Half and Second Half of Life

Jung's developmental writing often distinguishes the tasks of youth from those of the second half of life. Youth emphasizes ego formation, adaptation, work, family, and social position; later life requires a different orientation to the unconscious and to meanings that cannot be solved by extending youthful aims. [Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "The Stages of Life"]

Fordham modifies but does not erase this distinction. Early development matters profoundly because the Self and individuation are already active. Yet the mature form of individuation remains different from infancy or childhood. The practical correction is therefore not "Jung was wrong and individuation is only continuous". It is that second-half individuation rests on a developmental history and is one form of a more general Self process.

## 44. Early Development versus Adult Individuation

| Dimension | Early developmental process | Adult individuation |
|---|---|---|
| Ego | Emerging and consolidating | Already substantially differentiated |
| Main encounters | Body, caregiver, objects, perception, play, social environment | Unconscious contents, relationships, symbols, neglected potentials, existential limits |
| Self relation | Mostly enacted before reflective representation | Can become consciously symbolized and reflected upon |
| Deintegration | Opens primary organization to experience | Loosens established ego structures and identifications |
| Reintegration | Builds new organization and ego capacities | Reorganizes mature personality around wider Self relation |
| Symbolic capacity | Developing | Can be highly elaborated |
| Individuation meaning | Becoming an increasingly differentiated individual | Conscious engagement with totality and previously unconscious life |
| Main risk | Splitting, failed integration, fragile ego/object differentiation | Inflation, regression, disintegration, defensive refusal of unconscious material |

[Source: Fordham, chs. 2-6]

# Part VIII - Fordham and Psychoanalysis

## 45. Why Fordham Turns to Psychoanalysis

Fordham's engagement with psychoanalysis follows from a problem internal to analytical psychology. He believed Jungian work on archetypes and symbols was insufficiently grounded in childhood development and in the detailed processes of the analytic relationship. Psychoanalysis, especially the British object-relations traditions, had developed concepts and techniques precisely in those areas.

Fordham's Introduction is explicit. Klein's discovery of unconscious phantasy in small children gave him a parallel to Jung and provided tools for child analysis. Later, Bion offered further conceptual resources. Chapter 6 compares ego and Self concepts across analytical psychology and psychoanalysis. Chapter 10 imports a mature debate about countertransference. Chapter 11 uses object-relational accounts of splitting and projective identification to describe primitive defences. [Fordham: Introduction; chs. 6, 10, 11, 16]

This is selective integration, not conversion. Fordham does not surrender the Self, archetype, or individuation. He uses psychoanalysis to specify processes Jung left relatively undeveloped and then reinterprets those processes in a Jungian architecture.

## 46. Fordham and Klein

Klein is the most important named psychoanalytic influence on Fordham's early developmental work. Fordham treats her as a major innovator whose work on infantile unconscious phantasy, child analysis, objects, splitting, projection, and introjection opened a developmental field parallel to Jung's exploration of archetypes.

What Fordham borrows includes:

- the serious study of early infantile mental life;
- child analytic technique and the use of play;
- good and bad object relations;
- part objects and whole objects;
- projective and introjective identification;
- paranoid-schizoid and depressive organizations;
- the clinical importance of primitive anxieties and defences.

What he modifies includes Klein's developmental starting point. Fordham's infant begins as an integrated individual rather than as a set of instinctual part-object relations. He is skeptical of assuming that part-object experience must always precede any whole-person mode of relation. He also retains the Jungian Self as a superordinate organizer.

What he rejects or qualifies includes rigid developmental sequence and total explanatory reduction to early phantasy. Observation of real mothers and babies becomes an important corrective. In Chapter 11 he explicitly resists explaining an adult's whole pathology from a bad beginning alone.

| Dimension | Fordham | Klein |
|---|---|---|
| Starting organization | Primary integrated Self | Early psychic life organized through instinct, phantasy, and primitive object relations |
| Core developmental unit | Self deintegration-reintegration in relation to environment | Relations among ego, drives/phantasies, and internal/external objects |
| Part objects | Important but not necessarily the only earliest mode | Central to early paranoid-schizoid organization |
| Whole objects | Can be present in primitive whole-person engagement as well as later differentiation | More strongly linked to depressive-position integration |
| Projection | Jungian projection plus projective identification | Projective identification is a central primitive mechanism |
| Archetype | Retained as inherited/organizing potential | Not a Kleinian construct |
| Self | Superordinate Jungian concept | No equivalent Fordhamian total Self |
| Clinical value | Uses Klein to deepen Jungian developmental and transferential work | Independent psychoanalytic system |

[Fordham: Introduction; chs. 3, 6, 11, 16]

Fordham is therefore not accurately described as "Kleinian Jung". The influence is substantial, but the primary Self and archetypal organization change the architecture.

## 47. Fordham and Winnicott

Fordham's direct engagement with Winnicott is especially visible in Chapter 4 and Chapter 6. He uses transitional objects and transitional phenomena to understand the developmental roots of play and active imagination. He also compares Winnicott's true/false self concepts with Jungian Self/persona language while warning against straightforward equivalence. [Fordham: chs. 4, 6]

The primary Self is not Winnicott's true self. Fordham's primary Self is a theoretical original psychosomatic integration and organizing whole. Winnicott's true self concerns spontaneous aliveness and development under facilitating environmental conditions, contrasted with defensive false-self organization. The words "self" and the shared interest in infancy do not erase these differences.

| Dimension | Fordham | Winnicott |
|---|---|---|
| Primary concept | Primary integrated Self, deintegration, reintegration | True/false self, holding environment, transitional phenomena |
| Initial organization | Psychosomatic Self posited as primary | Development described through infant dependence and facilitating environment |
| Caregiver | Object and real environment with which Self engages | Environmental provision and holding are central |
| Play | Related to Self processes and roots of active imagination | Transitional space and playing are foundational |
| Symbolic intermediate area | Assimilated through transitional phenomena | Core Winnicottian concept |
| True self relation | Not equivalent to primary Self | Distinct construct concerning spontaneity and aliveness |
| Clinical implication | Interpret developmental level; holding may precede symbolic interpretation | Holding and capacity to play central in regression and treatment |

[Fordham: chs. 4, 6; comparison limited to Fordham's own engagement]

## 48. Fordham and Bion

Bion becomes increasingly important in Fordham's later clinical thinking. Fordham explicitly says that Bion presented material analytical psychologists needed to assimilate. Bion influences his understanding of analyst receptivity, projective processes, thinking, containment, and the distinction between different "vertices" from which experience can be approached. [Fordham: Introduction; chs. 1, 10, 14-16]

Fordham's use of Bion is not an abandonment of Jungian symbolic psychology. It gives him a language for primitive communication and for the analyst's capacity to receive unprocessed experience without prematurely converting it into a familiar interpretation.

## 49. Fordham versus Jung

Fordham's relation to Jung is best described as a developmental reformulation that claims continuity with the Self while openly correcting parts of Jung's developmental picture.

| Dimension | Jung | Fordham |
|---|---|---|
| Developmental starting point | Early consciousness and ego emerge gradually; child initially strongly embedded in parental psyche | Infant begins as an integrated individual Self distinct from mother |
| Self | Totality of conscious/unconscious and archetypal centre/order | Retains totality but distinguishes total Self from central archetype and posits primary integration |
| Ego development | Discussed but not developed into detailed infant-object theory | Central problem; ego nuclei emerge within Self processes and object experience |
| Infancy | Important but relatively under-theorized in mature system | Central empirical and theoretical field |
| Archetype | Inherited collective patterns expressed in images and behavior | Retained, brought into infant development through deintegrates and experience |
| Environment | Parents, culture, analysis matter; early relation less systematically theorized | Caregiver and objects are active developmental partners |
| Object relationship | Present in projection/transference but not core infant theory | Integrated with Klein, Winnicott, Bion, observation, and child analysis |
| Individuation | Most fully elaborated as adult, often second-half process | Lifelong process with qualitatively different early and adult forms |
| Analysis | Dialectical, symbolic, prospective, transference transformative | Adds detailed transference-countertransference and primitive developmental organization |

[Primary comparison: Jung CW 7, CW 8, CW 9ii, CW 16, CW 17; Fordham, *Explorations into the Self*]

## 50. Fordham versus a Classical Jungian Developmental Map

A simplified classical map can look like this:

```text
mature ego
  -> encounter with unconscious
  -> shadow and other archetypal figures
  -> differentiation of opposites
  -> symbolic transformation
  -> changing relation to Self
```

Jacobi's classical expositions can be read in this adult-oriented way, although they should not be caricatured as denying childhood or development. Fordham changes the starting point. Before the mature ego can confront the unconscious, the ego itself has a developmental history within Self and object relations.

A Fordhamian addition therefore looks like:

```text
primary integrated Self
  -> deintegration into early experience
  -> object relation and bodily differentiation
  -> repeated reintegration
  -> ego development and Self representation
  -> later capacity for classical adult individuation
```

[Laboratory synthesis based on Fordham; classical routing: File 13, Jacobi]

The change is structural. Fordham is not merely adding a childhood chapter before Jung. He is making the Self developmentally active before the adult symbolic sequence and thereby altering what later individuation rests upon.

## 51. Fordham and Samuels's School Model

Samuels's 1985 taxonomy helps locate Fordham historically. The "developmental school" weights personal development most strongly, then the Self, then archetypal imagery, and tends clinically to prioritize transference-countertransference. Samuels explicitly says his developmental school resembles Fordham's "London School". [Post-Jungian comparison: Samuels, *Jung and the Post-Jungians*, ch. 1]

The taxonomy should not be read backward. Fordham did not invent Samuels's three-school scheme, and individual developmental Jungians differ substantially. Fordham's own work remains anchored in his specific Self theory, child analysis, object relations, and analytic interaction.

# Part IX - Fordham in Relation to Knox and Attachment Theory

## 52. Fordham versus Knox

Jean Knox is a later developmental Jungian who shares Fordham's dissatisfaction with a purely adult, preformed-archetype model but revises the developmental foundations much more radically. Her *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis* draws explicitly on attachment theory, developmental psychology, cognitive science, image schemas, internal working models, reflective function, and emergent models of mind. [Post-Jungian comparison: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, chs. 1-8]

Knox acknowledges Fordham as a decisive predecessor. She discusses his deintegration-reintegration model as an account of how the infant opens to new experience and then consolidates it. She also describes his primary Self as an integrated psychosomatic potential interacting with the environment. But she does not simply restate him. She questions stronger biological or inherited-content accounts of archetypes and develops an emergent model of symbolic meaning.

| Dimension | Fordham | Knox |
|---|---|---|
| Historical position | Mid-century developmental reformulator of analytical psychology | Later developmental and attachment-informed Jungian revision |
| Initial Self | Primary integrated psychosomatic whole/potential | Emphasizes emergent mind and emergent selfhood; treats primary-Self language critically and abstractly |
| Main developmental process | Deintegration-reintegration | Emergence of representation through interaction, image schemas, internal working models, reflective function |
| Archetype | Retains inherited/archetypal organization and at times biological framing | Reworks archetype through emergent developmental structures and image schemas |
| Caregiver relation | Object relation and real mother-infant interaction important | Attachment theory becomes a central explicit framework |
| Representation | Self representations emerge through development | Representation and internal working models are central explanatory mechanisms |
| Agency | Present in active Self and ego development | Explored through developmental agency and reflective function |
| Analysis | Object relation, projection, transference/countertransference, Self processes | Attachment, reflective function, internal models, meaning, analytic relationship |

Continuity lies in the move toward development, actual relationship, and the analytic encounter. Difference lies in epistemology and mechanism. Knox is not simply "Fordham plus modern evidence". She changes what is innate, how archetype is conceived, and how relational representation develops.

## 53. Primary Self versus Knox's Emergent Selfhood

The key disagreement concerns what is present from the beginning. Fordham posits primary psychosomatic integration as a Self potential. Knox is more comfortable with models in which complex mental organization and selfhood emerge through developmental interaction, although she tries to show that Fordham's "potential" can be interpreted abstractly enough to coexist with emergence. That reconciliation is Knox's later proposal, not Fordham's own explicit move. [Knox: ch. 3]

| Question | Fordham | Knox |
|---|---|---|
| What is innate? | Organizing Self and archetypal potential | Developmental predispositions and embodied schemas without requiring fixed inherited symbolic content |
| What emerges? | Ego, differentiated Self representations, object relations, conscious symbolic realization | Symbolic meaning, internal working models, reflective capacities, complex selfhood |
| Environment | Activates and modifies inherited organization | Constitutive in formation of mental representations and meaning |
| Archetype | More nativist and biological in some formulations | Reinterpreted through emergence and image-schema theory |
| Representation | Later expression of Self processes | Central mechanism in developing mind |

This distinction prevents anachronism. One may find conceptual bridges between Fordham and Knox, but Knox cannot be used to tell us what Fordham "really meant".

## 54. Fordham versus Attachment Theory

Attachment theory is a different framework. Its central explanatory unit is an evolved attachment behavioral system organized around proximity to attachment figures under threat, the use of trusted others as safe havens and secure bases, and the formation of attachment-related expectations or internal working models. In adulthood, major constructs include security, anxiety, avoidance, hyperactivating strategies, deactivating strategies, and attachment-related emotion regulation. [Attachment comparison: Mikulincer and Shaver, *Attachment in Adulthood*, chs. 1-7]

Fordham's central unit is the Self and its integration-deintegration in relation to objects and experience. The caregiver matters, but not because Fordham organizes the theory around an attachment behavioral system. His model includes archetypal organization, ego formation, projection, introjection, splitting, Self representation, and individuation.

| Dimension | Fordham | Attachment theory |
|---|---|---|
| Basic developmental unit | Self undergoing deintegration and reintegration in relation to objects/environment | Attachment behavioral system operating in relation to attachment figures |
| Caregiver relation | Object relationship and real developmental encounter | Safe haven, secure base, proximity, availability, responsiveness |
| Innate organization | Primary Self and archetypal potential | Evolved attachment system and species-typical behavioral capacities |
| Regulation | Not the master construct; development includes organization of affect and objects | Emotion regulation is a major function and outcome of attachment security/insecurity |
| Internal representation | Self and object representations, introjection/projective processes | Internal working models and attachment-related representations |
| Separation/exploration | Considered through individuation, object differentiation, play, development | Explicit secure-base balance between attachment and exploration |
| Developmental change | Repeated deintegration-reintegration and ego differentiation | Updating of attachment strategies/models through experience and relationships |
| Adult implications | Continued Self processes, object relations, individuation, analytic interaction | Adult close relationships, security strategies, caregiving, intimacy, emotion regulation |

The models can be compared, and later authors such as Knox explicitly connect them. They should not be assimilated.

## 55. Deintegration-Reintegration versus Attachment Activation-Regulation

| Fordham process | Attachment process | Why they are not equivalent |
|---|---|---|
| Deintegration | Attachment-system activation | Deintegration is a general Self opening toward experience; attachment activation is specifically threat/separation-related activation of an evolved attachment system |
| Engagement with object | Proximity seeking or support seeking | Fordham's "object" is a psychoanalytic relational construct; attachment behavior has a defined security function |
| Reintegration | Soothing, deactivation, return to exploration | Reintegration reorganizes Self after experience; regulation may reduce arousal without the broader structural meaning |
| Repeated cycles | Repeated security regulation | Fordham applies the cycle across many developmental and analytic domains, not only attachment |
| Failure of integration | Insecure or dysregulated attachment | Different theories of disturbance; neither term can be inferred from the other |

This table is a conceptual firewall. Similar temporal shapes do not establish theoretical identity.

## 56. Primary Self versus Secure Base

The primary Self is an internal structural-developmental hypothesis about the infant as an integrated psychosomatic whole. A secure base is a function of an attachment relationship in which an attachment figure's availability supports exploration and return. One is not a property of the caregiver and the other is not an attachment status.

Therefore:

`primary Self != secure base`

An infant could, within a comparative discussion, be described as having Fordhamian Self processes while also participating in more or less secure attachment relationships. The concepts answer different questions.

## 57. Developmental Failure versus Insecure Attachment

Fordham's developmental disturbance can involve failures of integration, severe splitting, primitive defences, unstable ego boundaries, or distorted object relations. Attachment insecurity concerns organized patterns of expectation and strategy around attachment availability and threat. These may coexist or influence one another in a real person, but one cannot be inferred from the other.

The same adult behavior can support multiple hypotheses. Withdrawal in conflict might reflect deactivating attachment strategies, a defensive object relation, a constellated complex, ordinary anger, learned communication style, current relationship conditions, or several processes at once. A Fordhamian developmental story should not be privileged merely because it is deeper or earlier.

# Part X - Relationship Relevance and Applied Developmental Inquiry

## 58. Fordham and Relationship

Fordham is not primarily a couple theorist. His relationship relevance comes from developmental and analytic concepts: the Self develops through encounter; ego differentiation requires objects; projection and projective identification distort or communicate relational experience; dependence is not the opposite of individuation; and mature development requires the ability to remain related without losing psychic differentiation.

His model therefore complicates the opposition between autonomy and dependence. The infant begins as an individual but is profoundly dependent on care. Later differentiation does not mean becoming relation-free. Adult individuation can deepen relationship by making projection more conscious and by increasing the capacity to recognize the other as other.

Fordham's direct claims about adult intimate relationships are limited compared with Jung's marriage essay, later relational Jungians, attachment theory, and couples therapy. Any application to couple conflict must therefore be labelled as Laboratory synthesis rather than presented as a hidden Fordham couple model.

## 59. Dependence and Differentiation

Developmental dependence is not pathology. A newborn's biological and relational dependence is obvious even within Fordham's emphasis on primary individuality. Differentiation means that psychic systems and persons become more distinguishable, not that relationships cease to matter.

Similarly, adult individuation is not independence from others. A person may become more individuated while also becoming more capable of mutual dependence, care, and dialogue. The relevant opposite of individuation is not closeness but unconscious identity, rigid projection, or loss of differentiated relation.

## 60. Separation and Connection

Fordham's model allows both separation and connection because it begins with an individual Self that must enter relation. Development is therefore not a journey from fusion to isolation. It is a repeated negotiation between relative integration and encounter.

In later relationship language, one might use this to ask whether a person can remain psychologically differentiated while affected by another. That is a Laboratory application. Fordham's own technical language is the development of Self, ego, object relations, and analytic interaction, not modern couple differentiation theory.

## 61. Adult Intimate Relationship: Direct Claims versus Later Application

FORDHAM'S DIRECTLY SUPPORTED CLAIMS:

- persons develop through relations to objects and environment;
- projection and projective identification can distort or mediate interpersonal experience;
- ego boundaries can be flexible without collapsing;
- Self processes involve cycles of opening and renewed organization;
- analytic relationships can transform both participants while preserving role asymmetry;
- early experience matters but does not determine the whole later person.

[LATER LABORATORY APPLICATION]

In adult intimate relationships these ideas can be used as questions, not diagnoses. Does conflict require temporary withdrawal for thought, or is withdrawal a rigid defence? Is the partner being perceived as a whole person or mainly through a projected configuration? Does an encounter permit changed understanding, or does each person return to the same organization? Can dependence be tolerated without equating it with loss of individuality? These questions are compatible with Fordham but are not a Fordhamian couple protocol.

## 62. Self-Analysis Relevance

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

Fordham's model is especially useful as a corrective to adult-only views of individuation. It invites attention to developmental organization without licensing speculative reconstruction. For self-analysis, the most useful questions are not "What went wrong in infancy?" but "What is happening now, what developmental hypothesis might fit it, and what evidence would distinguish that hypothesis from alternatives?"

Useful lines of inquiry include whether current reactions show flexible differentiation or defensive withdrawal; whether a relationship is being experienced through a recurrent object configuration; whether an attachment explanation, complex theory, ordinary situational explanation, or Fordhamian developmental formulation best fits the evidence; and whether the idea of the Self is being used abstractly while concrete developmental and relational processes are ignored.

## 63. Developmental Inquiry Protocol

[LABORATORY APPLIED SYNTHESIS]

1. Describe the present episode before interpreting it.
2. Separate observable behavior from inferred motive.
3. Name the affect and bodily activation that are directly known.
4. Identify any recurring complex pattern supported by repeated evidence.
5. Ask whether a developmental formulation is actually needed to explain the episode.
6. If childhood history is invoked, distinguish remembered or documented history from reconstruction.
7. Ask whether object-relation language adds explanatory value beyond ordinary relationship description.
8. Consider attachment theory separately, using attachment constructs only when their evidence is present.
9. Consider Fordham's Self/deintegration framework only where organization, differentiation, and developmental encounter are genuinely at issue.
10. Generate at least one non-developmental alternative explanation.
11. Do not infer infant causation from adult behavior alone.
12. Preserve uncertainty and update the formulation when new evidence appears.

This protocol is not Fordham's checklist. It is a corpus method designed to prevent theoretical overreach.

## 64. Early-Development-to-Adult-Relationship Guardrail

[LABORATORY METHODOLOGICAL GUARDRAIL]

Adult relationship behavior must not be explained automatically by speculative infant development. A developmental hypothesis becomes more credible only when there is recurring evidence across contexts, relevant history, a plausible mechanism, present behavior consistent with the hypothesis, and serious consideration of alternatives.

The mere fact that a reaction feels "deep", "primitive", or disproportionate is not enough to identify its infant origin. Complex activation, attachment threat, current relational injury, temperament, stress, learned behavior, social context, and ordinary incompatibility can all produce intense states.

# Part XI - Misreadings, Limits, and Guardrails

## 65. Anti-Flattening Reference

This section is a compact conceptual firewall for the Laboratory.

**Primary Self != ego.** The primary Self is Fordham's hypothesized original psychosomatic integration. The ego is a differentiated centre of conscious organization that develops within Self processes.

**Primary Self != mature adult Self without qualification.** There is continuity of Self across life, but the newborn does not possess adult symbolic consciousness, a mature ego-Self relation, or the differentiated wholeness associated with later individuation.

**Primary Self != secure base.** The primary Self is an intrapsychic/psychosomatic structural concept. A secure base is a relational function of an attachment figure.

**Primary Self != true self.** Winnicott's true self concerns spontaneous aliveness and development under facilitating environmental conditions. Fordham's primary Self is his own Jungian developmental construct.

**Deintegration != disintegration.** Deintegration is a normal opening or differentiation of an organized Self; disintegration is breakdown of organization.

**Deintegration != dissociation.** Deintegration maintains functional relation to the whole and supports encounter. Dissociation can involve discontinuity or autonomous separation of psychic systems.

**Deintegration != fragmentation.** A deintegrate is a differentiated partial system in relation to the total Self, not a broken fragment severed from psychic organization.

**Deintegration != attachment activation.** Attachment activation is a specific response of the attachment behavioral system to threat, separation, or availability. Deintegration is a much broader Self process.

**Reintegration != emotional regulation.** Regulation concerns modulation of affective activation. Reintegration concerns renewed organization of Self after experience.

**Reintegration != ordinary integration.** Integration is a generic term. Reintegration specifies organization after a prior state has deintegrated into experience.

**Object relation != attachment bond.** Object relation belongs to psychoanalytic theory of psychic relations to objects. Attachment bond belongs to attachment theory of proximity, safe haven, and secure-base functions.

**Development != attachment alone.** Fordham's development includes archetypal organization, ego differentiation, bodily experience, object relation, representation, defence, and Self processes.

**Individuation != adult-only development.** Fordham extends the underlying individuating process across the lifespan while preserving distinctive adult forms.

**Individuation != independence.** Individuation concerns differentiation and relation to Self, not withdrawal from dependence, care, or mutual relationship.

**Early dependence != pathology.** The infant is biologically and relationally dependent even if Fordham treats it as an individual Self from the start.

**Archetypal organisation != fixed inherited imagery.** Fordham retains inherited archetypal potential, but experience affects manifestation; a specific cultural image need not be genetically preloaded.

**Fordham != Jung.** Fordham extends and revises Jung, especially on infancy, maternal fusion, ego development, object relations, and lifelong individuation.

**Fordham != Klein.** He borrows extensively from Klein but retains primary Self and archetypal organization and modifies her developmental assumptions.

**Fordham != Winnicott.** He uses transitional phenomena and holding but does not equate his primary Self with Winnicott's true self.

**Fordham != Knox.** Knox is a later developmental Jungian who adds attachment, cognitive-developmental, emergent-mind, and reflective-function models and revises the status of archetype.

## 66. Common Misreadings

### Misreading 1: "Fordham moved Jung into childhood by saying infants already have the adult Self."

Correction: Fordham proposes an original integrated Self, not an adult symbolic Self in miniature. Mature Self representation and ego-Self reflection are developmental achievements.

### Misreading 2: "The primary Self is the baby's ego."

Correction: The ego develops within the Self. Fordham's theory exists partly to explain how ego organization can emerge from a broader primary organization.

### Misreading 3: "Deintegration means the Self breaks apart."

Correction: Deintegration is functional differentiation or opening. Breakdown is better described as disintegration or pathological splitting.

### Misreading 4: "Reintegration means the psyche repairs itself."

Correction: Repair can be one clinical issue, but reintegration is the normal developmental reorganization of experience into the Self. It need not follow injury.

### Misreading 5: "Deintegration is basically dissociation."

Correction: Fordham uses deintegration as a normal process that preserves relation to the total Self. Dissociation is a different Jungian and clinical concept.

### Misreading 6: "Attachment activation is Fordhamian deintegration."

Correction: Attachment activation is system-specific and security-related. Deintegration applies to many forms of encounter, learning, object relation, fantasy, and analysis.

### Misreading 7: "Reintegration is secure attachment regulation."

Correction: A child may be soothed without a claim about Self reintegration, and a reintegrative process may concern experiences outside attachment entirely.

### Misreading 8: "Fordham proved individuation starts at birth."

Correction: Fordham proposed and clinically developed a lifelong model of individuation. This is a theoretical reconstruction grounded in clinical and observational work, not a modern experimental proof.

### Misreading 9: "Early development determines later adult relationships."

Correction: Fordham explicitly resists total explanation by a bad beginning. Later trauma, continuing development, present context, and new relationships matter.

### Misreading 10: "The mother creates the infant's Self."

Correction: Fordham posits the Self as primary. The caregiver profoundly shapes experience and development but does not manufacture the Self from nothing.

### Misreading 11: "Object relations and attachment theory are the same thing."

Correction: They have overlapping domains but different constructs, histories, mechanisms, and evidence traditions.

### Misreading 12: "Fordham's primary Self is Winnicott's true self."

Correction: Fordham himself compares Jungian and Winnicottian self concepts without equating them. Their theoretical functions differ.

### Misreading 13: "Fordham abandoned Jung for psychoanalysis."

Correction: He explicitly works with Jung's Self, archetypes, individuation, and dialectical analysis while using psychoanalytic developmental concepts to repair what he sees as gaps in analytical psychology.

### Misreading 14: "Fordham's developmental school rejects archetypes."

Correction: Fordham retains archetypal organization and at times gives it a strong biological basis. Later developmental Jungians such as Knox revise that basis more extensively.

### Misreading 15: "If adult relationships are difficult, reintegration failed in infancy."

Correction: This is an unsupported retrospective leap. Adult conflict has many possible explanations and cannot establish a specific infant process.

### Misreading 16: "Individuation means increasing autonomy from others."

Correction: Differentiation can support better relationship. Fordham's model makes encounter with objects and other people constitutive of development rather than obstacles to it.

## 67. Limits of Fordham's Model

Fordham's model has high historical and conceptual significance, but its limits should remain visible.

First, much of its evidence comes from clinical work, child analysis, and mother-infant observation interpreted within psychoanalytic and Jungian theory. This is not the same evidence base as contemporary developmental psychology, attachment research, or neuroscience.

Second, the concept of a primary integrated Self is a theoretical postulate. Fordham attempts to move it closer to observation, but it cannot simply be treated as an empirically observed organ of the psyche.

Third, Fordham's language sometimes shifts between total Self, central archetype, primary integrate, self representations, and ordinary senses of self. Chapter 1 is partly an attempt to discipline this ambiguity in Jung, but Fordham does not remove all ambiguity from his own usage.

# Samuels: Post-Jungian Schools, Debates, and Clinical Differences

## Source and Edition Note

The principal source for this file is the Project copy of Andrew Samuels, *Jung and the Post-Jungians*. The actual file was inspected rather than identified from its filename alone. The title page gives the title as *Jung and the Post-Jungians* and the author as Andrew Samuels. The copyright and publication page records first publication in 1985 by Routledge & Kegan Paul plc; a corrected reprint in 1985; first paperback publication in 1986; further reprints in 1986, 1987, 1990, 1991, 1994 and 1999; and the Project copy as a Taylor & Francis e-Library edition published in 2005. The listed ISBNs are 0-203-35929-1 for the master e-book, 0-203-37185-2 for the Adobe eReader edition, and 0-415-05904-6 for the print edition. Copyright is Andrew Samuels, 1985.

The book's actual contents are:

1. **Schools of analytical psychology** - source p. 1
2. **Archetype and complex** - source p. 19
3. **The ego** - source p. 44
   - Appendix: **developments in typology** - source p. 67
4. **The self and individuation** - source p. 71
5. **The development of personality** - source p. 106
6. **The analytical process** - source p. 139
7. **Gender, sex, marriage** - source p. 168
8. **Dreams** - source p. 187
9. **Archetypal psychology** - source p. 196
10. **Theory in practice: an illustration** - source p. 202
11. **Comparison and evaluation** - source p. 211

The volume then contains Practical information, Selected further reading arranged by school, References, and an Index.

The PDF is text-based rather than image-only and the extraction is generally strong. The entire 251-page digital file was rendered for visual inspection. The source includes diagrams and clinical drawings that plain-text extraction cannot represent adequately. The text layer contains occasional line-break, ligature, hyphenation and character-recognition errors. This file therefore uses chapter and verified source-page ranges as its normal locator and does not pretend that every extracted character is authoritative. The rendered pages control where text extraction and page appearance conflict.

[Samuels: *Jung and the Post-Jungians*, title/copyright pages, contents, Preface]

## Samuels's Role and Epistemic Status

Samuels writes as a practising analytical psychologist and as a historian-theorist of analytical psychology from within the post-Jungian field. In the Project copy's author note he is identified as a Professional Member of the Society of Analytical Psychology in London, in private practice and engaged in teaching. His book is therefore an important insider map of analytical psychology in the early 1980s, but it is not a primary source for what Jung himself meant.

This distinction governs the whole file. Five levels must remain separate:

1. **JUNG PRIMARY** - Jung's own published formulations.
2. **POST-JUNGIAN AUTHOR'S OWN POSITION** - for example Fordham's, Hillman's, or another author's actual writing.
3. **SAMUELS'S INTERPRETATION / CLASSIFICATION** - Samuels's reconstruction of Jung and his organization of later Jungians.
4. **LATER HISTORIOGRAPHY OR DEVELOPMENT** - material published after the 1985 book.
5. **LABORATORY SYNTHESIS** - routing, comparison, and applied use produced for this corpus.

Samuels himself warns that summarizing other writers necessarily does some violence to their views and explicitly hopes that readers will go on to their original writings. That warning is unusually important here because File 16 is designed as a router. It should make differences legible, not turn a historiographic map into a substitute for Fordham, Hillman, Jung, or later writers.

[Samuels: Preface; ch. 1]

## Coverage of This File

This file reconstructs Samuels's own map first. It then uses selected Project sources to verify or contextualize some of the most important positions he attributes to other thinkers. The reconstruction covers:

- what Samuels means by post-Jungian;
- why he thinks schools can be identified even though they are not formal parties;
- his Classical, Developmental, and Archetypal orientations;
- the six dimensions by which he distinguishes their emphases;
- their disagreements over archetype, complex, ego, Self, development, individuation, image, dreams, transference and countertransference;
- clinical consequences of those disagreements;
- Samuels's critique of rigid classification;
- his final attempt to identify a post-Jungian ethos without abolishing disagreement;
- later developments that reveal both the continuing usefulness and historical limits of the three-school map.

The file does **not** claim that there are exactly three Jungian schools in nature, that every analyst has one permanent membership, or that a theoretical label mechanically predicts technique. Those propositions are contrary to Samuels's own cautions.

## How to Use This File

Use File 16 as a **routing layer**. When the research question is historical or author-specific, proceed from Samuels to the original author. When the question is about Jung, route back to primary Jung. When the question is about later relational, attachment-informed, typological, or couple work, route forward beyond Samuels.

A useful operational rule is:

> **Samuels locates a debate; the original author establishes the position.**

The three-school model is most useful when it is treated as a map of *relative emphasis*. It becomes misleading when treated as a census of fixed identities.

---

# Part I - What "Post-Jungian" Means

## 1. The Problem Samuels Is Trying to Solve

Samuels opens with the claim that finding one's way around the contemporary Jungian world is difficult. By the early 1980s, analytical psychology was no longer adequately represented by repeating Jung's own formulations. Analysts had challenged Jung, adapted him, incorporated psychoanalytic work, and developed sharply different clinical practices. Yet the public image of analytical psychology often remained organized around the founder alone.

His book was conceived through three linked projects. First, he wanted to describe how analytical psychology had developed after Jung's death in 1961. Second, he realized that these developments could not be evaluated without establishing their starting point in Jung. Third, he placed both Jung and the post-Jungians in comparison with psychoanalysis. This is why the book alternates between history, theoretical criticism, clinical comparison, and attempts at synthesis.

Samuels's target is not merely ignorance about later writers. It is a structural problem in founder-centered traditions: handbooks may continue to summarize the patriarch while practitioners have already changed how they think and work. Samuels says that contemporary Jungian practice and the textbook version of Jungian psychology can differ markedly. The task is therefore to recover the internal debates through which the field moved on.

[Samuels: Preface; ch. 1, source pp. 1-18]

## 2. What Does "Post-Jungian" Mean?

For Samuels, *post-Jungian* does not mean merely "published after Jung". Nor does it mean anti-Jungian. Near the end of chapter 1 he explains that he prefers *post-Jungian* to *Jungian* because the term can indicate both **connectedness to Jung and distance from him**. That double movement is the key.

Post-Jungian work therefore includes several possible relations to Jung:

- conserving a major Jungian formulation while refining it;
- selecting one strand of Jung and giving it greater weight than Jung did;
- criticizing a concept while retaining the larger analytical-psychological project;
- combining Jungian ideas with psychoanalytic, developmental, cultural, or other theories;
- translating a Jungian concept into a different clinical register;
- exposing tensions already present in Jung and developing one side of the tension;
- abandoning a formulation judged unhelpful while retaining other parts of the tradition.

This is why *revision* and *continuity* are not opposites in Samuels's historiography. A school can be recognizably post-Jungian because its disagreements are organized around questions inherited from Jung. Indeed, Samuels later proposes a "post-Jungian vertex": a common field defined by active participation in a set of debates, rather than by assent to a creed.

Post-Jungian therefore means more than succession and less than orthodoxy. It marks an intellectual lineage in which the founder remains a reference point but no longer monopolizes valid development.

[Samuels: ch. 1]

## 3. Do "Jungians" Exist?

Jung repeatedly resisted the idea of followers who simply reproduced him. Samuels takes that resistance seriously but does not accept it as proof that no Jungian groupings developed. Jung participated in institutional and professional life, helped shape training arrangements, supported and criticized followers, and inevitably became an authority around whom a tradition formed.

Samuels's point is not biographical accusation. It is institutional realism. Once there are training institutes, journals, lineages of supervision, bodies of theory, disputes about technique, and a growing profession, schools can emerge even when the founder disliked the language of discipleship. Denial of schools can itself obscure real differences.

This is one reason Samuels distinguishes **school** from **formal organization**. The Classical, Developmental, and Archetypal schools are not three incorporated institutions with membership cards. They are configurations of emphasis, intellectual kinship, and recurrent clinical preference.

[Samuels: ch. 1, "Do 'Jungians' Exist?"]

## 4. Theory as Tool, and the Danger of Reification

Before classifying schools, Samuels spends time on theory itself. Jung often distrusted closed theory, but he also theorized constantly. Samuels presents Jungian concepts as working hypotheses derived from material, applied to material, and liable to modification when they no longer fit. Theoretical constructs are not literal objects in the psyche.

This warning is foundational for the school taxonomy. A model can help organize complexity without proving that the categories exist as natural kinds. Samuels is particularly alert to the temptation, encouraged by personified Jungian language, to reify concepts such as archetype, anima, Self, or school. The same caution applies to his own taxonomy.

His later phrase "creative falsehood" should be read against this background. Classification necessarily simplifies, but simplification can be useful when it is held consciously as a model rather than mistaken for psychic or social anatomy.

[Samuels: ch. 1]

## 5. Earlier Attempts to Classify the Field

Samuels does not invent classification from nothing. He reviews three prior efforts.

### 5.1 Gerhard Adler

Adler distinguished an orthodox or traditional tendency, neo-Jungians more open to psychoanalytic clinical work, and a middle position. In Samuels's reconstruction, the traditional tendency gives strong weight to symbolic transformation, amplification, active imagination, and the numinous. The neo-Jungian side gives more attention to infantile material, reductive interpretation, and transference understood in relation to early life. The middle group seeks combinations.

Samuels regards this as useful but also partisan and insufficiently able to register the later emergence of archetypal psychology.

### 5.2 Michael Fordham

Fordham used a largely geographical contrast between a Zürich tendency and a London tendency. The Zürich side emphasized Jung's later work on symbol, myth, and individuation. The London side, especially through the Society of Analytical Psychology, developed a more intensive engagement with transference, countertransference, early development, and psychoanalytic object relations, particularly Klein and Winnicott.

Samuels sees real descriptive value in this contrast but regards geography as an unstable basis for taxonomy because ideas travel and analysts do not remain confined to cities.

### 5.3 Naomi Goldenberg

Goldenberg distinguished "second" and "third" generation Jungians in an intellectual rather than merely chronological sense. Her third generation was especially relevant to the emergence of archetypal psychology and a more radical revision of the responsibility owed to Jung.

### 5.4 Why Samuels Revises Them

The three earlier classifications do not line up neatly. Some exclude developments others highlight. Some are geographically or politically colored. Samuels wants a model that can show both shared discipline and meaningful difference, while leaving room for individual variation.

[Samuels: ch. 1, sections reviewing Adler, Fordham, Goldenberg]

## 6. Why a School Classification?

Samuels states two purposes with unusual clarity:

1. to provide outsiders with access to post-Jungian developments; and
2. to provide enough structure for internal comparison, ordering, and mutual reflection.

The classification is therefore pedagogical and historiographic before it is sociological. It helps answer: *What are analysts arguing about? Where do their priorities differ? Why can two people both call themselves analytical psychologists yet hear the same clinical material differently?*

The six dimensions he chooses are not arbitrary. They identify recurring fault lines in both theory and practice.

Theoretical dimensions:

1. the definition of the archetypal;
2. the concept of the Self;
3. the development of personality.

Clinical dimensions:

1. analysis of transference-countertransference;
2. emphasis on symbolic experiences of the Self;
3. examination of highly differentiated imagery.

[Samuels: ch. 1, "A New Classification"]

## 7. The Three-School Model

[SAMUELS'S HISTORIOGRAPHIC MAP]

```text
                    ANALYTICAL PSYCHOLOGY
                           |
          -----------------------------------------
          |                  |                    |
      CLASSICAL         DEVELOPMENTAL         ARCHETYPAL
          |                  |                    |
  Self / symbolic      development /        image / imaginal
  transformation       relationship          differentiation
  archetypal order     transference          archetypal perspective
          |                  |                    |
          -------------------|--------------------
                              |
                   shared post-Jungian vertex
          (common questions, differing weightings)
```

The diagram must be read as a map of priorities, not a partition of persons.

### 7.1 Samuels's weighting of theoretical priorities

| Dimension | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| First theoretical priority | Self | Development of personality | Archetypal / imagery |
| Second | Archetypal | Self | Self |
| Third | Development | Archetypal | Development |
| Samuels's shorthand | 2, 1, 3 | 3, 2, 1 | 1, 2, 3 |

### 7.2 Samuels's weighting of clinical priorities

| Dimension | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Primary clinical emphasis | Symbolic experiences of Self | Transference-countertransference | Highly differentiated imagery |
| Secondary emphasis | imagery or transference-countertransference; Samuels is uncertain of order | Self experience or imagery; Samuels is uncertain of order | symbolic experiences of Self |
| Tertiary emphasis | the remaining secondary dimension | the remaining secondary dimension | transference-countertransference |

Samuels deliberately marks uncertainty in the Classical and Developmental second-place orderings. This is evidence against reading the model as a rigid scoring system.

[Samuels: ch. 1, source pp. 12-14]

## 8. Ideal Types, Not Boxes

Samuels's strongest guardrail is explicit: **"Any classification is, to an extent, a creative falsehood."** He immediately explains why. Few individuals exactly fit descriptions; classifiers have preferences; and analysts routinely use theories and clinical areas associated with more than one school.

His positive formulation is equally important. Taken together, the six dimensions make up a large part of the common discipline. What defines a school is **emphasis, weighting, preference, and individual choice**, not exclusive possession of concepts or techniques.

He gives Hubback as an example: even within the Developmental School, amplification and active imagination can receive serious attention. Patients can also pull analysts toward methods or concepts outside their usual emphasis.

The model is therefore best understood as an **ideal-typical matrix of attention**. It asks what an analyst tends to notice first, what theory tends to organize the material, and what kind of intervention is likely to become salient. It does not tell us what an analyst is permitted to think or do.

Samuels's Figure 2 places many individual analysts in overlapping zones. He cautions that names appearing in the same column indicate theoretical similarity, not necessarily public alliance. This file does not reproduce Figure 2 as a membership register because doing so would defeat the caution built into the figure.

[Samuels: ch. 1, source pp. 14-18]

---

# Part II - The Classical Orientation

## 9. Defining the Classical School

The Classical School is Samuels's name for the tendency whose priorities most closely resemble what he takes to be Jung's own general ordering. This does **not** make the school identical with Jung. It is already post-Jungian because it selects, systematizes, teaches, and clinically operationalizes Jung after him.

Samuels places the Self first in the Classical theoretical weighting, archetypal structures and imagery second, and early personal development third. Clinically, symbolic experiences of the Self lead, followed by a combination of particularized imagery and transference-countertransference whose exact order he does not claim to settle.

The Classical orientation is therefore characterized less by doctrinal literalism than by the following center of gravity:

- the reality and clinical importance of the collective and archetypal dimensions of psyche;
- the Self as an integrating and individuating principle;
- symbolic transformation;
- dreams, active imagination, and amplification;
- the relation of ego to unconscious and Self;
- individuation as a major framework for long-term psychological change;
- attention to mythic, cultural and religious parallels when they illuminate psychic material.

[Samuels: ch. 1; chs. 2-4, 6, 8]

## 10. Representative Classical Thinkers in Samuels's Map

Samuels's Figure 2 includes numerous analysts and should not be read as a fixed roster. Three figures are especially safe anchors for this corpus because their relation to the orientation is explicit or structurally clear in Samuels's discussion:

- **Gerhard Adler**, whom Samuels explicitly cites as Classical and whose statement gives primacy to symbolic transformation and the numinous;
- **Joseph Henderson**, whom Samuels explicitly says he would regard as a member of the Classical School, while also noting Henderson's openness to compatible psychoanalytic developments;
- **Jolande Jacobi**, whose systematic expositions of Jung provide a useful example of classical systematization, though Jacobi's own writings must be read in their own right rather than treated as a template for every classical analyst.

Marie-Louise von Franz, Barbara Hannah and other Zürich-linked analysts often appear close to the Classical pole in Samuels's historical map, but the point is theoretical affinity, not formal membership.

[Primary cross-check: Jacobi, *The Psychology of C. G. Jung*; von Franz, in *Lectures on Jung's Typology*]

## 11. Classical View of Archetype

Samuels's reconstruction of Jung provides the conceptual base. He stresses the distinction between **archetype as such** and **archetypal image**. The archetype is not a photograph inherited in the brain. It is closer to an inherited formal disposition or patterning potential. The image through which it appears is shaped by history, culture, situation and individual experience.

The Classical orientation tends to preserve the strong transpersonal force of this concept. Archetypal material is not reducible to childhood experience. Myth, religion, folklore and cultural images can amplify a personal dream or fantasy because similar formal patterns may be constellated beyond one biography.

Samuels nevertheless criticizes common classical temptations: turning archetypes into a catalogue of fixed mythological entities, confusing the abstract pattern with a particular image, and imposing pre-existing mythic schemes on a patient's material. These are risks *within* an archetypal theory, not arguments for discarding the archetypal level.

[Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "On the Nature of the Psyche"; CW 9i, essays on archetypes and collective unconscious]

## 12. Classical View of the Self

The Classical orientation gives the Self its greatest relative weight. In Samuels's reconstruction of Jung, the Self gathers several meanings: totality, ordering center, potential integration, relation of conscious and unconscious, pattern, balance, and a source of symbols that can carry numinous value.

A persistent Jungian paradox remains: the Self is described both as the totality or circumference of the psyche and as its center. Samuels does not erase the contradiction. He treats it as one reason later schools revise the concept.

Classically, ego development is not the final aim. The ego must become sufficiently differentiated to relate to an unconscious larger than itself. Symbolic experience can relativize ego positions and disclose a pattern that the ego did not author. Individuation is therefore often articulated through the ego-Self relation.

The risk, as Samuels later argues, is that the Self can become over-weighted, reified, or treated as a guarantee of resolution. Developmental and archetypal revisions both challenge that inflation, though in different languages.

[Samuels: ch. 4]

## 13. Classical View of Individuation

Samuels presents Jung's individuation as a process of differentiation and integration through which a person becomes more distinctly himself or herself while entering a more conscious relation to unconscious contents. It is not simply social adaptation, autonomy, happiness, or the elimination of conflict.

Classical descriptions often emphasize:

- differentiation of ego from collective identifications;
- confrontation with shadow;
- engagement with anima/animus in Jung's historical model;
- symbolic encounters with the Self;
- holding and transformation of opposites;
- the second half of life, where Jung often placed mature individuation.

Samuels resists converting this into a fixed staircase. He is especially interested in later writers who "earth" individuation by locating developmental processes from infancy onward and by questioning wholeness as an explicit psychological goal.

[Primary Jung comparison: CW 7, "The Relations Between the Ego and the Unconscious"; CW 17, "The Development of Personality"]

## 14. Classical Clinical Style

Samuels never claims that all classical analysts work alike. The Classical pole is better described by a cluster of likely emphases:

- close attention to dreams and spontaneous symbolic material;
- amplification when mythic or cultural parallels illuminate rather than overwrite the patient's material;
- active imagination where appropriate;
- attention to the ego's relation to unconscious figures and the Self;
- interpretation of transference at both personal and archetypal levels;
- use of symbols as living mediators rather than merely disguised representations of something else;
- long-range orientation toward individuation rather than symptom relief alone.

Classical work can still attend to childhood, defenses, and actual relationship. Samuels's taxonomy says only that these are less defining of the orientation than symbolic and Self-related work.

## 15. Strengths and Risks of the Classical Orientation

**Source-derived strengths in Samuels's account:** continuity with Jung's distinctive concepts; preservation of the transpersonal dimension; a rich language for symbols and meaning; resistance to reducing psychic life to biography; and strong resources for understanding experiences that patients experience as numinous or purposive.

**Risks identified or implied by Samuels:** reification of theoretical entities; over-systematization; treating canonical myths as templates into which patients are fitted; underweighting infancy and actual relational history; using symbolism to escape the immediacy of transference; and treating the Self or wholeness as if it guarantees a harmonious resolution.

These are tensions within a living orientation, not grounds for ranking it against the other schools.

---

# Part III - The Developmental Orientation

## 16. Defining the Developmental School

The Developmental School gives the development of personality first theoretical priority, followed by the Self and then the archetypal. Clinically, it gives transference-countertransference first priority. Samuels associates this tendency historically with the London Society of Analytical Psychology and especially with Michael Fordham, but he does not reduce it to Fordham alone.

Its distinctive question is: **How does a Jungian model of psyche operate in actual development, beginning in infancy and unfolding in relation to real objects, caregivers, defenses, and the analytic relationship?**

This produces a different center of clinical gravity:

- infancy and childhood matter in their own right;
- ego development must be theorized rather than assumed;
- actual object relations matter alongside archetypal images;
- transference and countertransference become major sources of evidence;
- psychoanalytic work from Klein, Winnicott and others can be incorporated without simply becoming Freudian;
- psychopathology can be understood in developmental and relational terms as well as symbolically;
- individuation can be understood as a lifelong process rather than an event reserved for mature adulthood.

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 5; ch. 6]

## 17. Fordham's Place

Fordham is central because he gives the Developmental School a specifically Jungian developmental metapsychology. Samuels draws particularly on Fordham's model of the primary Self, deintegration and reintegration, early ego formation, and the clinical centrality of transference-countertransference.

Fordham's own *Explorations into the Self* confirms the core of Samuels's account. The book's first part includes "The self in Jung's works", "Individuation and ego development", "Integration-deintegration in infancy", and "A possible root of active imagination". Its later chapters include "The ego and the self", "Countertransference", and "Defences of the self". The foreword describes Fordham's theory of an original undifferentiated Self containing archetypal and ego potential. Under internal and external stimulation, deintegrative processes occur; emerging elements engage objects and are subsequently reintegrated, progressively enriching the Self.

[Primary verification: Fordham, *Explorations into the Self*, chs. 1-3, 6, 10-11]

This is not merely a psychoanalytic substitution for Jung. It keeps a theory of the Self and archetypal organization, but relocates their operation into infancy and actual developmental transactions.

## 18. Relation to Psychoanalysis

Samuels treats the Developmental School as the post-Jungian orientation with the most sustained interchange with psychoanalysis. The important influences include Klein, Winnicott, object-relations theory, and the psychoanalytic literature on transference and countertransference. Freud remains an important interlocutor, but "psychoanalytic" is not a single position.

A useful three-column distinction is:

| Question | Borrowed or engaged from psychoanalysis | Revised in developmental Jungian work | Distinctively Jungian element retained |
|---|---|---|---|
| Early development | close observation/theory of infancy; object relations; defenses | early development interpreted through Fordham's Self model | primary Self and archetypal potentials |
| Transference | repetition and enactment of earlier object relations | combined with Jung's mutual/dialectical model | personal and archetypal dimensions may coexist |
| Countertransference | analyst's responses as data | made central to two-person process | compatible with Jung's view that analyst is affected |
| Psychopathology | developmental failures, defenses, internal objects | integrated with Self-regulation and archetypal organization | psyche not reduced to drive theory or biography |
| Interpretation | genetic/developmental meanings | here-and-now transference can matter more than historical reconstruction | symbols can remain irreducible to childhood causes |

[Samuels: chs. 5-6]

## 19. Developmental View of the Self

The developmental revision begins by refusing to leave the Self almost entirely to mature individuation. Fordham treats the Self as operative from the beginning of life. Ego and Self are therefore related developmentally rather than only vertically as lesser center and greater totality.

This creates a different question from the Classical one. Instead of asking principally how the adult ego encounters symbols of psychic totality, the developmental analyst asks how differentiated psychic organization emerges through repeated interactions with environment and objects.

The Self is not simply "built" by the environment. Fordham preserves an endogenous organizing principle. But that organization is not clinically separable from actual experience. Samuels sees this as one of the major ways the Developmental School moves Jungian theory toward infancy without abandoning its own distinctive conceptual inheritance.

## 20. Developmental View of Archetype

The Developmental School does not simply eliminate archetypes. Samuels's chapter on archetype shows a change of emphasis. Mythological amplification receives less automatic priority; analysts ask more directly how archetypal dispositions become actualized in early life and how archetypal and environmental factors interact.

Fordham's model is important here. Archetypal potentials do not need to appear first as elaborate mythological images. They can be understood as organizing tendencies expressed in early behavior and relationships. Samuels also criticizes any simple equation of an archetype with one mythic image or one inherited content.

This is distinct from Jean Knox's later emergent developmental theory. Knox's 2003 work explicitly reinterprets archetype using developmental psychology, cognitive science, attachment theory, image schemas and internal working models. Those are **later** developments and must not be read backward into Samuels's 1985 account.

[Later comparison: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, chs. 2-4]

## 21. Developmental View of Individuation

One of Samuels's most consequential claims is that post-Jungian developmental thought helps "earth" individuation. If psychologically meaningful differentiation and integration occur in infancy, then individuation cannot be confined to the second half of life.

Developmental individuation therefore includes:

- early differentiation of ego and objects;
- repeated cycles of separation, engagement and integration;
- growth of capacities for relationship and symbolic functioning;
- adult reworking of earlier organization;
- continuing movement rather than arrival at a final state of wholeness.

Samuels's chapter 11 finds a surprising convergence between Developmental and Archetypal critiques of a heavily teleological model of individuation. Both can shift attention away from achieving "wholeness" and toward differentiation of actual psychic contents.

## 22. Developmental View of Transference

Transference is the clearest clinical marker of the Developmental orientation in Samuels's model. He quotes the editorial introduction to a collection of developmental papers describing the sequence by which recognition of transference became central, then countertransference, and finally the transaction was conceptualized as transference/countertransference.

This does not reduce transference to a historical replay. In contemporary developmental practice as Samuels presents it, early material can become present in the analytic relationship here and now. The analyst studies how the patient experiences the analyst, how early object patterns organize current expectation, and what emerges between them.

The Developmental School therefore tends to ask of an interpretation:

- What relationship is being enacted now?
- What earlier organization does it resemble?
- What defense or anxiety is active?
- What is the patient attributing to the analyst?
- What is happening in the analyst that may be informative?
- How does the interpretation alter the relationship rather than merely explain it?

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 6]

## 23. Countertransference

Samuels devotes substantial space in chapter 6 to the development of countertransference theory. He compares Jungian practice with psychoanalytic writers including Racker and others, then returns to analytical psychology.

Jung had already departed from the image of the analyst as unaffected observer. Samuels emphasizes Jung's dialectical conception of analysis: both participants are implicated and the analyst can be changed. Jung could describe the analyst's own response as a significant source of information, while also warning about unconscious identification and psychic infection.

The Developmental School turns this possibility into a highly elaborated clinical instrument. Analyst feeling, fantasy, bodily response, irritation, idealization, rescue impulses or confusion may contain information about the patient's relational world, provided the analyst first subjects these reactions to self-scrutiny. Countertransference is not automatically truth about the patient. It may also arise from the analyst's own complexes.

This is one reason analyst training and personal analysis matter. To use countertransference, the analyst needs enough differentiation to ask: *What belongs primarily to me, what may have been evoked in this relationship, and what remains undecidable?*

[Samuels: ch. 6, especially the section "The use of the countertransference in analytical psychology"]

## 24. Developmental Clinical Style

A developmental clinical style may emphasize:

- minute attention to transference-countertransference;
- early relational history without assuming historical reconstruction is always the task;
- ego capacities and defenses;
- primitive anxieties and modes of relating;
- the distinction between real external objects and internalized object relations;
- the analyst's participation and the patient's experience of the analytic setting;
- interpretation that links present interaction with developmental meaning;
- symbolic material, but often read in relation to development and the therapeutic relationship.

The last point is essential. Developmental does not mean "non-symbolic". Samuels's own guardrail is that all schools participate in the common field and may use one another's methods.

## 25. Strengths and Risks of the Developmental Orientation

**Source-derived strengths:** a theory of infancy and childhood; close clinical attention; serious use of transference and countertransference; integration with object-relations thinking; richer accounts of ego formation, defenses and psychopathology; and a way to make individuation genuinely lifelong.

**Tensions or risks identified in Samuels's comparisons:** developmental language can appear to reduce symbolic or archetypal material to personal history; psychoanalytic borrowing can make distinctively Jungian features less visible; historical reconstruction can become overly causal if it forgets the present or symbolic child; and sophisticated two-person work can itself become a closed technical language.

Samuels's own later argument is that the strongest developmental work need not commit these reductions. It can recognize the psychological child as simultaneously historical, imaginal and archetypal.

---

# Part IV - The Archetypal Orientation

## 26. Defining the Archetypal School

The Archetypal School gives first theoretical priority to the archetypal, second to the Self, and least to developmental explanation. Clinically it gives greatest priority to highly differentiated imagery, then symbolic experiences of the Self, and least to transference-countertransference as an organizing method.

James Hillman is its central figure in Samuels's account, but neither Samuels nor this corpus should treat Hillman as the entirety of archetypal psychology. Samuels also discusses figures such as Patricia Berry and Rafael Lopez-Pedraza, among others, and traces a broader imaginal style.

The orientation is distinguished by a shift from asking primarily *what structure or developmental cause lies behind this image?* to asking *what does the image itself ask us to see?*

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 9]

## 27. Hillman's Place

Hillman's work is both continuous with and revisionary of Jung. It takes Jung's attention to fantasy, myth, images, personification and psychic reality with radical seriousness. At the same time it attacks tendencies within classical Jungian discourse toward a single center, heroic ego development, hierarchy, teleology and a monotheistic symbolism of the Self.

Samuels cites Hillman's proposition that fantasy images are at the most basic level of psychic reality and refers to Hillman's language of the "primacy of images". This is not the claim that images are decorative contents generated by a deeper mechanism. Image is treated as a primary form of psychic presentation.

Hillman's earlier work on typology, including his treatment of feeling, should not be confused with the whole of archetypal psychology. File 14 addresses the typological material separately.

[Primary verification: Hillman section of *Lectures on Jung's Typology* for feeling; Samuels ch. 9 for archetypal psychology]

## 28. Archetypal View of Archetype

Samuels detects an important shift from archetype as an abstract structure behind images to **archetypal as a quality or perspective within imaginal experience**. By the end of his own argument he suggests that an image need not conform to a pre-existing mythological checklist before it can be experienced as archetypal.

This supports a more phenomenological stance. Archetypal significance may be inferred from the depth, grip, numinosity, recurring pattern, emotional force, bodily resonance, and imaginal density of what occurs. The archetypal is not automatically located in a named god, myth or universal motif.

Samuels's own formulation that the archetypal is in the "eye of the beholder" is best understood as an attempt to prevent reification. It does **not** mean that any private association can be declared archetypal without discipline. The point is that archetypal quality is relational and experiential rather than a taxonomic property stamped on a fixed image.

[Samuels: ch. 2; ch. 11]

## 29. Archetypal Critique of the Self

Archetypal psychology is one of the strongest sources of Samuels's "dethroning" of the Self. The critique is not simply that the Self does not exist. It targets the psychological effects of making unity, centrality, totality and wholeness the supreme organizing values.

From an archetypal perspective, a single central Self can appear "monotheistic": it privileges one unifying standpoint over a plurality of psychic figures. Hillman's polytheistic language seeks instead to preserve differences among psychic persons, images and styles of consciousness.

The clinical concern is that a rush to synthesis may erase what is distinctive in a symptom, dream figure, mood, fantasy or conflict. The analyst may ask not how the many will be unified, but how the many can become more fully differentiated.

Samuels later finds a surprising parallel with Fordham's "part-selves". The theories are not identical, but both can weaken an overly totalizing notion of wholeness.

[Samuels: ch. 4; ch. 9; ch. 11]

## 30. Archetypal View of Ego

Archetypal psychology tends to decenter the ego rather than eliminate it. Samuels's chapter on the ego shows how Hillman and related writers criticize heroic, developmental and managerial images of ego. Ego itself can be seen as one psychic perspective among others rather than the sovereign organizer of all experience.

Personification becomes important. Instead of translating a figure into an ego category, the analyst may allow figures to retain voice, intention and ambiguity. This can loosen literal identification with one conscious standpoint.

The risk of caricature is obvious. Archetypal psychology does not require that ordinary reality, responsibility or coherent agency become irrelevant. Samuels's interest is in the psychological value of decentering, not in abolishing the ego.

## 31. Archetypal View of Individuation

The archetypal revision tends to pluralize individuation. It resists a linear ascent from lesser to greater integration and a final image of completed wholeness. Development may instead be imagined as deepening into particular images, differentiating psychic persons, and becoming capable of inhabiting contradiction without forcing premature synthesis.

Samuels's chapter 11 therefore states that both Developmental and Archetypal wings "earth" individuation and are suspicious of striving for wholeness as a goal. In the archetypal idiom, polytheism provides the image of differentiated multiplicity.
