This does not make individuation meaningless. It changes what counts as movement: from arrival at a center to a more complex relation to the many forms of psyche.

## 32. Image and Imagination

For the Archetypal School, image is not merely a sign that must be decoded into a concept. The task is often to stay close to image, metaphor, fantasy and myth long enough for their internal articulation to emerge.

This differs from classical amplification in emphasis. Amplification may connect an image to cultural parallels. Archetypal work is more likely to resist translating the image into a generalized archetypal meaning. It seeks precision within the image itself.

Samuels's discussion of Hillman's dream theory is especially clear. The "nightworld" of the dream should not automatically be converted into the "dayworld" of ego advice. The dream can work on the ego rather than merely provide a message for ego to implement.

This is also distinct from Jung's active imagination. Active imagination is a Jungian method for consciously engaging unconscious images. Archetypal psychology is a broader orientation to psychic reality in which images are primary. The two can overlap but are not synonyms.

## 33. Archetypal Clinical Style

Where Samuels's source supports clinical implications, the Archetypal pole tends toward:

- fidelity to the exact image, metaphor and fantasy presented;
- personification rather than immediate abstraction;
- mythological or cultural imagination without reducing an image to one canonical meaning;
- deepening rather than causal explanation;
- suspicion of interpretations that convert image into developmental history too quickly;
- attention to multiplicity and psychic figures;
- understanding pathology as something that may belong to the image or archetypal pattern itself, rather than only a deficit to be removed.

Samuels does not present this as a uniform technique, and he does not deny that archetypal analysts have transferences or developmental histories. The classification concerns emphasis.

## 34. Strengths and Risks of the Archetypal Orientation

**Source-derived strengths:** preservation of imaginal depth; resistance to over-literal causal explanation; sensitivity to metaphor and myth; protection of psychic multiplicity; critique of totalizing Self models; and an ability to remain with difficult or pathological imagery without instantly converting it into deficit language.

**Tensions in Samuels's treatment:** archetypal rhetoric can caricature developmental work as crude reductionism; deliberate distance from causal developmental theory can underplay actual childhood events and relationships; poetic language can appear obscure or insufficiently clinical; and a celebration of multiplicity can itself become a doctrine.

Samuels's final position is not to resolve these tensions in favor of another school. He looks for specific bridges while preserving real differences.

---

# Part V - Cross-School Conceptual Debates

## 35. Archetype

Archetype is one of Samuels's six defining dimensions because each school can accept the term while meaning something importantly different by it. The dispute is not simply belief versus disbelief. It concerns the relation among inherited pattern, image, development, culture and experience.

| Question | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| What is most important? | The transpersonal organizing pattern and its symbolic expression | How archetypal potential becomes actualized through development and relationship | The imaginal event and archetypal quality of experience |
| What is innate? | Formal predisposition or archetype as such, not a fixed inherited picture | Organizing potential, understood in interaction with the infant's environment | Not normally formulated as a developmental inheritance question; emphasis is on archetypal perspective and image |
| Role of image | Image manifests an underlying archetypal pattern and may be amplified | Image may express developmental and archetypal organization together | Image is primary psychic presentation; avoid treating it merely as a pointer to an abstract structure |
| Role of development | Important but lower relative priority | Central to understanding how dispositions become organized | De-emphasized as explanatory hierarchy; historical causation is not the primary lens |
| Main danger | Reifying fixed archetype-image correspondences | Reducing archetypal material to biography or object relations | Treating imaginal language as exempt from developmental or relational reality |

[Samuels: ch. 2; ch. 11]

The major anti-flattening rule is: **archetype is not an identical concept across the three orientations**. The Classical School tends to protect the transpersonal formal pattern; the Developmental School asks how such potential becomes embodied in early relationship; the Archetypal School radicalizes the image and the act of imagining.

## 36. Collective Unconscious

The collective unconscious remains most explicit in the Classical orientation because it is one of Jung's principal ways of naming the impersonal dimension of psyche. Developmental writers do not necessarily reject it, but their clinical attention is more likely to move toward how archetypal organization appears within early development and actual relationships. Archetypal psychology can retain an impersonal or transpersonal psyche while becoming less interested in a structural map of collective unconscious contents.

Samuels does not provide a single, symmetrical three-school doctrine of the collective unconscious. Therefore any stronger claim would exceed the source. What he does show is a redistribution of attention from **where psychic forms come from** toward **how they emerge in development** or **how they present as images**.

[Samuels: ch. 2]

## 37. The Self

The Self is a central fault line because it combines theory, symbolism, development, religious experience and clinical goal.

| Question | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Basic emphasis | Self as totality, ordering center, integrating and individuating principle | Self operative from infancy, dynamically involved in development and ego formation | Suspicion of a single central totality; multiplicity and polytheistic psyche emphasized |
| Ego-Self relation | Ego is a center of consciousness within a larger Self | Ego differentiation develops within processes of Self organization and relationship | Ego and Self are both decentered as singular sovereign principles |
| Developmental status | Often most visible in mature individuation and symbols of totality | Present from the beginning of life; deintegration/reintegration gives developmental articulation | Developmental status is less central than imaginal differentiation |
| Clinical use | Symbolic experiences of Self may orient change and meaning | Self concepts are integrated with early development and analytic relationship | Self language may be useful but is critiqued when it becomes totalizing or teleological |
| Main risk | Reification, spiritualization, presumption of resolution | Turning Self into a developmental mechanism that appears overly explanatory | Caricaturing any unifying experience as monotheistic or oppressive |

[Samuels: ch. 4; ch. 11]

Samuels's own working movement is toward a **relative concept of Self**. He does not simply discard it. He asks whether the concept can retain integrative force without becoming an ontological guarantee that conflict will resolve into unity.

## 38. Ego

| Dimension | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Main question | How does ego relate to unconscious and Self? | How does ego emerge, differentiate and defend itself in development? | How can ego be decentered so other psychic perspectives can speak? |
| Development | Important but not normally the first explanatory axis | Central, including infancy and early object relations | Developmental hierarchy is treated skeptically |
| Clinical concern | Ego strength sufficient to engage unconscious without inflation or collapse | Ego capacities, defenses, relational organization, primitive anxiety | Literalism, heroic ego, one-perspective dominance |
| Image of change | differentiated ego in relation to larger psyche | more differentiated and resilient ego-object functioning | more plural and imaginal consciousness |

[Samuels: ch. 3]

One of Samuels's useful cross-school contributions is to prevent a false binary between "strong ego" and "no ego". Classical and developmental writers both require enough ego organization for analysis, while archetypal writers ask what psychic costs arise when ego becomes the unquestioned measure of reality.

## 39. Individuation

| Question | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Typical temporal emphasis | Often adult, especially second half of life | Lifelong, beginning in infancy | Not primarily staged; may be reframed as differentiation and deepening |
| Organizing image | Ego-Self relation and increasing integration | Developmental differentiation and reintegration across life | Plurality, personification, deepening of images |
| Goal language | Wholeness, totality, becoming oneself | Ongoing developmental process; wholeness less final | Suspicion of wholeness as monotheistic/teleological goal |
| Role of conflict | Opposites can be held and transformed | Conflict may express developmental organization, defenses, relational patterns | Conflict may belong to irreducible plurality; do not force synthesis |
| Shared post-Jungian revision identified by Samuels | Classical baseline | "Earth" individuation into ordinary development | "Earth" individuation into ordinary, multiple psychic life |


Samuels's chapter 11 is explicit that the two wings both challenge an over-weighted classical picture of potential, resolution and wholeness. Their reasons differ. The Developmental wing does so by moving individuation into the whole life cycle and differentiating part-selves. The Archetypal wing does so through polytheism and imaginal multiplicity. Their shared target does not make their positive theories identical.

[Samuels: ch. 4; ch. 11]

## 40. Development

Development is the axis on which the Developmental School most clearly separates itself.

| Dimension | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Infancy | acknowledged but not the dominant center of theory | central theoretical and clinical field | not a privileged causal origin |
| Childhood | relevant to complexes and ego development | major source of object-relational and defensive organization | may appear as literal history, imaginal child, mythic child, or psychic figure |
| Adult development | individuation central | continuous with earlier developmental processes | less linear; emphasis on deepening/differentiation rather than stages |
| Linearity | not necessarily linear, but can be presented teleologically | processual and developmental, though not necessarily simple stage theory | actively suspicious of developmental hierarchy |
| Environment | meaningful but often secondary to symbolic-archetypal frame | constitutive interaction with innate organization | meaningful as part of psychic reality but not privileged as explanatory cause |

Samuels's crucial reconciliation is not "development is true" versus "development is false". He distinguishes the **historical child**, the **psychological child**, and the **archetypal child** and argues that sophisticated developmental work can move among these without crude causality.

[Samuels: ch. 5; ch. 11]

## 41. Symbol and Image

Classical Jungian practice tends to distinguish sign and symbol. A sign points to something already known; a living symbol expresses more than consciousness can presently formulate and can mediate between opposites. Amplification may place the symbol in a larger mythic or cultural context.

Developmental work does not have to reject symbol, but it often asks what developmental organization, relationship or transference context accompanies symbolic material. A symbol that appears in a dream during a rupture with the analyst may be studied as symbol **and** communication.

Archetypal psychology shifts the center of gravity from **symbol of what?** to **what does this image do, show, personify or demand?** The image is not simply an encoded message awaiting translation into Self, development or diagnosis.

| Question | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Primary unit | symbol within psyche's compensatory/individuating movement | symbolic material in developmental and relational context | image/fantasy/personification in its own right |
| Typical operation | amplification and symbolic interpretation | interpretation linked to history, defense and transference | staying with, deepening, personifying, elaborating image |
| Main risk | canonical amplification can overrun personal material | causal interpretation can flatten symbolic excess | image can become insulated from relational and material conditions |

[Samuels: chs. 2, 6, 8-9]

## 42. Dreams

Samuels devotes chapter 8 to modifications of Jung's dream theory. He preserves several classical propositions: dreams are not deliberate deceptions; they can compensate the conscious attitude; they may have prospective significance; both subjective and objective levels can matter; and interpretation must attend to the dreamer's conscious situation and associations.

The post-Jungian modifications are clinically important.

First, **Dieckmann's work on the dream ego** stresses continuity between waking and dream personality. The dream ego can show defenses, affects and relational positions rather than serving only as a passive recipient of unconscious correction.

Second, **Kenneth Lambert** warns against analyzing the dream rather than the patient. Analysts can elicit the kinds of dreams their theories reward. Dream material can also participate in transference, for example through what is brought, withheld, emphasized or forgotten in the session.

Third, **Hillman** argues that the dream should not be translated too quickly into dayworld advice. The dream may work on ego consciousness by drawing it into the nightworld of image.

| Dimension | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Main dream function | compensation, symbolization, prospective movement, individuation | dream as psychic material within development and transference | dream as imaginal nightworld; image should not be reduced to ego message |
| Associations | central | central, especially relational and developmental associations | used without dissolving the image into explanatory prose |
| Amplification | often important | selective; may be less central | mythic material may deepen image but should not convert it into fixed symbol code |
| Clinical question | What is unconscious compensating or symbolizing? | Why this dream, now, with this analyst and this history? | What world is this image asking consciousness to enter? |

[Samuels: ch. 8]

## 43. Active Imagination

Active imagination is not one of Samuels's main three-school discriminators, so it should remain proportionate. It belongs strongly to Jung's clinical-symbolic tradition and appears naturally in Classical work. Samuels also points out, however, that developmental analysts such as Hubback have used active imagination, directly undermining the notion that schools own techniques.

Archetypal psychology shares a serious commitment to imagination but is not identical with Jung's specific active-imagination method. Its imaginal orientation concerns the ontology and phenomenology of psychic images more broadly.

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 6; ch. 9]

## 44. Psychological Type

Samuels treats typology in an appendix to chapter 3 rather than as a central axis of school differentiation. He reviews empirical and conceptual developments that complicate rigid typological schemes. This placement matters: type is part of analytical psychology, but it is not one of the six dimensions by which he defines his schools.

The practical implication is that type should not be forced into every comparison. A Classical analyst may use type extensively or rarely; a developmental or archetypal analyst may also use it. Later John Beebe's work makes this cross-school character even clearer by combining Jungian typology with archetypal complexes, developmental reflection and clinical use.

[Later comparison: Beebe, *Energies and Patterns in Psychological Type*]

## 45. Shadow, Anima/Animus and Persona

Samuels does not give these three concepts a symmetrical school-by-school taxonomy. Their relevance is distributed across chapters on ego, gender and analysis.

**Shadow** remains tied to the ego's relation to disowned or unrecognized contents. A Classical reading may emphasize integration of shadow in individuation. Developmental work may ask how unacceptable affects or identifications became defended against in relationship. Archetypal work may ask which psychic figure or image has been made shadow by the dominant standpoint.

**Persona** belongs mainly to the differentiation of ego from collective role. Samuels's school map does not justify assigning a unique persona theory to each school.

**Anima/animus** receives substantial criticism in chapter 7 because Jung's historical gender assumptions are entangled with the concept. Post-Jungian revision tries to separate valuable ideas about inner otherness, contrasexual imagery, projection and psychological function from rigid claims about what men and women inherently are.

[Samuels: chs. 3, 7]

## 46. Gender and Anima/Animus

Samuels's chapter 7 is explicitly revisionary. Jung's historical language frequently assigns Logos to men and Eros to women, or treats anima and animus through a binary contrasexual model. Samuels does not silently modernize this. He shows both the psychological insight and the stereotype risk.

A careful reconstruction separates at least four layers:

1. **Jung's historical model:** a man tends to personify unconscious relational/feeling dimensions as anima; a woman tends to personify unconscious logos/opinion dimensions as animus.
2. **The symbolic function of the model:** anima/animus can name experiences of inner otherness and projection that exceed ego identity.
3. **The problem:** Jung often binds these functions to culturally and biologically generalized claims about masculinity and femininity.
4. **Post-Jungian revision:** functions, values and images associated with gender can be treated as psychic possibilities not mechanically determined by sex.

Samuels also notes criticism of simple symmetry. Men and women cannot be assumed to have mirror-image psychic structures simply because a model is elegant.

For the Laboratory, the key rule is historical visibility: **do not silently translate anima/animus into contemporary gender identity, and do not repeat Jung's gender generalizations as timeless facts.**

[Samuels: ch. 7]

## 47. Sexuality

Samuels includes sexuality because the Jung-Freud split and later Jungian gender theory can create the false impression that analytical psychology has little to say about sexual life. His treatment is narrower than a contemporary sexuality theory and should not be expanded beyond the source.

He considers how eros, bodily life, projection, anima/animus, relationship and symbolic meaning intersect. He also challenges the tendency to spiritualize psychic development in ways that leave sexuality under-theorized. Archetypal psychology's attention to embodied images and developmental psychology's attention to early object relations both complicate a purely elevated, spiritual model.

[Samuels: ch. 7]

## 48. Psychopathology and Neurosis

Jung did not build analytical psychology around a diagnostic nosology comparable to later psychiatric classification. Samuels presents neurosis in Jung partly as division, conflict and loss of meaning, but also as potentially purposive because symptoms can force psychological development.

The schools redistribute explanatory weight:

- **Classical:** disturbance may be understood through imbalance, complexes, failed symbolic relation, one-sidedness, and blocked individuation.
- **Developmental:** greater emphasis on developmental failures, defenses, object relations, ego organization and transference.
- **Archetypal:** pathology may be approached as belonging to the archetypal image itself, resisting the idea that every symptom is simply deficit or malfunction.

Samuels does not provide a neat three-column psychopathology manual. Where the source is not specific, the correct answer is **not clearly established in this source**.

---

# Part VI - Clinical Differences

## 49. What Counts as Therapeutic Change?

| Orientation | Typical change emphasis in Samuels's map | Qualification |
|---|---|---|
| Classical | greater relation between ego and unconscious; symbolic transformation; individuation; meaningful experience of Self | does not imply symptom relief or relationship history are ignored |
| Developmental | change in transference/object relations; ego development; defenses; capacity to relate; developmental reorganization | symbols and archetypal meanings can remain important |
| Archetypal | changed relation to image, fantasy and psychic multiplicity; deepening rather than immediate cure | not a rejection of practical improvement or actual relationship |

[Samuels: chs. 6, 9-11]

The differences concern **what is regarded as psychologically primary**, not three incompatible definitions of improvement. Samuels's chapter 10 deliberately demonstrates a practice that moves among these registers.

## 50. Therapist Stance

| Dimension | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Typical stance | interpreter and dialectical partner attentive to symbol and unconscious autonomy | participant-observer within transference-countertransference; developmental object as well as interpreter | imaginal companion who helps images differentiate and deepen |
| Analyst subjectivity | acknowledged through Jung's dialectical method | intensively scrutinized as countertransference data | may enter through image, metaphor and shared imaginal field |
| Authority risk | symbolic expertise can become interpretive authority | developmental/technical expertise can become explanatory authority | poetic or mythic fluency can become its own authority |

Samuels's preferred corrective across all three is self-reflection. No standpoint exempts the analyst from examining how theory shapes perception.

## 51. Transference

Jung's own approach is broader than a strictly infantile repetition model. He can treat transference as personal projection, archetypal constellation, mutual psychic involvement and, in *The Psychology of the Transference*, through alchemical symbolism.

Samuels then shows a difference of emphasis:

| Question | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Centrality | important but not necessarily the organizing center of every analysis | highest clinical priority in Samuels's model | lower relative priority than image and imaginal work |
| Typical reading | personal and archetypal projection; symbolic transformation | historical and here-and-now object relations; defenses; repetition and interaction | may be understood imaginally, but Samuels does not give a single archetypal transference doctrine |
| Interpretive focus | meaning of projections and symbols in ego-Self relation | what is enacted between analyst and patient, including early patterns | images/personifications arising within or around the relationship |

[Samuels: ch. 6]

## 52. Countertransference

| Question | Classical | Developmental | Archetypal |
|---|---|---|---|
| Source status | Jung recognizes analyst involvement, psychic infection and informative response | elaborated into a central clinical instrument | no equally systematic doctrine established by Samuels's school model |
| Analyst response | must be differentiated from analyst's own complexes | monitored closely for concordant/complementary and interactional meanings | may be experienced through fantasy and imagery; not clearly standardized in this source |
| Main danger | unconscious identification | mistaking analyst material for patient material, or technique becoming totalizing | assuming imaginal resonance is self-validating |

For the archetypal column, "not clearly standardized in this source" is the more accurate formulation than inventing symmetry.

## 53. Interpretation

A useful shorthand is:

- **Classical:** symbolic interpretation and amplification.
- **Developmental:** developmental and transference interpretation.
- **Archetypal:** imaginal deepening and attention to metaphor/personification.

But Samuels's whole book warns that this shorthand can mislead if made exclusive. Classical analysts interpret development; developmental analysts work with symbols; archetypal analysts inevitably encounter transferences. The clinical difference is what tends to organize attention and what is considered explanatory enough.

## 54. Analyst Authority

Samuels's survey exposes three different possible forms of authority:

1. **symbolic authority** - the analyst knows myths, archetypes, symbolic parallels;
2. **developmental authority** - the analyst knows the patient's defensive and relational organization, perhaps before the patient does;
3. **imaginal authority** - the analyst appears able to see the "real" image or myth operating beneath ordinary speech.

None is automatically legitimate. Jung's dialectical model and Samuels's emphasis on theory as hypothesis both work against the fantasy of the omniscient analyst. Developmental use of countertransference adds another check because the analyst must consider his or her own contribution to the field.

[Samuels: chs. 1, 6, 10-11]

## 55. Regression

Samuels discusses regression mainly in connection with Jung's view of the analytical process and psychoanalytic comparisons. Regression is not necessarily failure. It can be a movement back toward material that consciousness has not integrated, a reactivation of developmental needs, or a condition in which symbolic transformation becomes possible.

The schools may emphasize different questions:

- Classical: what unconscious material or symbolic potential becomes available through regression?
- Developmental: what earlier relational organization or unmet developmental process is reactivated, and how is it held in the analytic relationship?
- Archetypal: what image of descent, return, dissolution or underworld is present?

The last formulation is a comparative inference consistent with Samuels's map, not a formal three-part doctrine stated by him.

[Laboratory comparative synthesis based on Samuels chs. 5-6, 9]

## 56. Relationship in Analysis

Jung's dialectical model already undermines a one-person psychology. Analyst and patient affect one another. Samuels's developmental material intensifies this into close scrutiny of transference-countertransference. His chapter 11 then proposes a bridge to the imaginal by discussing countertransference and the **mundus imaginalis**.

This leads to an important post-Jungian possibility: interpersonal interaction and imagery need not be competitors. The relationship can generate images, and images can organize the relationship. Samuels can therefore imagine a **shared, two-person imaginal world** without collapsing it into either a literal interpersonal transaction or an impersonal archetype.

This should not be conflated with Nathan Schwartz-Salant's later fully developed theory of the interactive field. Schwartz-Salant's 1998 *The Mystery of Human Relationship* explicitly theorizes a "third area" or field between people that has its own dynamics. That is a later development and deserves separate treatment in File 18.

[Samuels: ch. 11; later comparison: Schwartz-Salant, *The Mystery of Human Relationship*]

## 57. Theoretical Orientation versus Clinical Technique

This distinction is mandatory because Samuels repeatedly undermines a simple one-to-one mapping.

| Proposition | Samuels-compatible interpretation |
|---|---|
| "Classical analysts amplify dreams." | Often, but not all dreams and not all Classical analysts. |
| "Developmental analysts interpret transference." | Transference is a defining priority, but developmental analysts also work symbolically and imaginally. |
| "Archetypal analysts avoid transference." | False as a general rule. Samuels assigns it lower relative priority, not absence. |
| "Technique reveals school membership." | Only imperfectly. The same analyst can change method with patient, phase and material. |
| "A school is a method." | False. It is a pattern of theoretical and clinical weighting. |
| "Borrowing a technique changes theoretical identity." | Not necessarily. Analysts can use techniques across traditions. |
| "Hybrid practice is incoherent." | Not automatically. Samuels argues that experienced analysts often use private combinations, though unexamined eclecticism can be a problem. |

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 10; ch. 11]

---

# Part VII - Samuels's Pluralism and Meta-Position

## 58. Is Samuels Merely Classifying?

No. Classification is the entry point, but the book is evaluative and synthetic. Samuels criticizes Jung, compares analytical psychology with psychoanalysis, identifies convergences between apparently opposed schools, and eventually sketches what he calls a post-Jungian ethos.

Yet his synthesis is not a declaration that all views are equivalent. He repeatedly insists on the value of conflict and choice. Disagreement gives the field shape. An analyst who combines approaches without having confronted their differences may simply avoid the intellectual and personal demands of theory.

Samuels is therefore best described as a **pluralist with integrative interests**. He wants productive movement among perspectives while retaining enough discrimination that the perspectives do not dissolve into one another.

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 11]

## 59. Pluralism

Pluralism in this context means that more than one post-Jungian perspective can illuminate psyche and clinical work, and that analysts may move among them without pretending they are identical.

Samuels's strongest image is that schools can exist not only as social groupings but as **strands in the mind of an analyst**, sometimes competing and sometimes synthesizing. This moves the taxonomy inward. The important question becomes not "Which box am I in?" but "Which theoretical voice is organizing my attention now, and what is it causing me to miss?"

This is why the six-dimensional grid can be used for professional self-analysis. It reveals the analyst's habitual weighting.

Pluralism therefore demands more rigor, not less. To hold several models one must know where they differ.

## 60. Productive Tension between Schools

Samuels's chapter 11 begins with "The Two Wings and the Centre." His central claim is that differences let the discipline see itself as a whole. Common ancestry is insufficient; a living field also needs dialogue capable of avoiding both the illusion of consensus and the illusion that disagreement requires schism.

He identifies several productive tensions:

- **structure versus development:** Is psychic form best understood as archetypal pattern or as organization unfolding through relationship?
- **Self versus multiplicity:** Is integration best represented by a center/totality, or does that image suppress necessary plurality?
- **archetype versus experience:** How much of form is inherited and how much emerges in interaction?
- **symbol versus relation:** Is the main task to interpret symbolic process or the analytic relationship in which meaning appears?
- **adult individuation versus infancy:** Does individuation begin only when mature ego consciousness turns inward, or is it a lifelong process?
- **causality versus prospectivity:** Does interpretation explain origins, disclose purpose, or hold both?
- **science versus metaphor:** Are developmental and imaginal languages actually as opposed as their rhetoric suggests?

[Samuels: ch. 11]

## 61. Can the Schools Be Integrated?

Samuels does not offer a simple yes. His chapter 11 shows specific bridges rather than a grand merger.

He notices, for example, that Developmental and Archetypal writers can both criticize classical wholeness, both emphasize differentiation, and both loosen the requirement that archetypal images fit pre-existing canonical forms. He argues that the developmental "child" can be psychological and imaginal as well as historical. He also uses countertransference as a possible route into a shared imaginal world.

But the schools still differ. Hillman's suspicion of developmental causality is not Fordham's developmental theory. Fordham's primary Self is not Hillman's polytheistic psyche. Agreement in criticism does not prove agreement in ontology.

The right distinction is:

| Term | Meaning in this file | What it is not |
|---|---|---|
| **Pluralism** | holding distinct perspectives in active dialogue | treating all views as equivalent |
| **Integration** | building an argued bridge between concepts while preserving their differences and provenance | blending terms until origins disappear |
| **Eclecticism** | selecting from several theories or techniques, sometimes pragmatically | automatically good or bad; Samuels worries about combinations without conviction or discrimination |

[Laboratory synthesis from Samuels chs. 1 and 11]

## 62. What Samuels's Map Gains

Samuels's taxonomy has several genuine gains.

**Historical orientation.** It makes visible that analytical psychology after Jung is not one doctrine repeated unchanged.

**Conceptual differentiation.** It separates disagreements that are otherwise hidden by shared vocabulary. "Self", "archetype" and "individuation" can mean different things in different post-Jungian contexts.

**Clinical comparison.** It shows that theoretical disagreements matter because they organize what the analyst attends to in the room.

**Dialogue.** It gives analysts a way to argue about priorities without first deciding that opponents have left the tradition.

**Professional self-analysis.** The six-dimensional grid can expose an analyst's habitual selection and blind spots.

**Routing value.** For this Laboratory it offers a first-pass route from a research problem to the kind of source most likely to illuminate it.

[Samuels: ch. 1; ch. 11]

## 63. What Samuels's Map May Obscure

[LABORATORY HISTORIOGRAPHIC ANALYSIS]

The same strengths generate limitations.

1. **Historical dating.** The map describes a field as Samuels saw it in the early 1980s. Later relational, attachment-informed, neuropsychological, cultural, feminist, queer, embodied and field-oriented developments cannot be assumed to fit it neatly.
2. **Internal diversity.** Each school contains substantial disagreements. "Developmental" does not mean one theory of development; "Classical" does not mean one technique; "Archetypal" does not mean one Hillman doctrine.
3. **Mixed thinkers.** Some analysts deliberately work across traditions. Figure 2 already contains overlaps.
4. **Asymmetry of concepts.** The three schools are not parallel theories built from identical components. For example, countertransference is far more explicitly systematized in the Developmental School than in Samuels's Archetypal account.
5. **Founder effect.** Calling one orientation "Classical" can unintentionally make it sound more authentic, even though Samuels uses the label for relative proximity to his reconstruction of Jung's priorities, not as a rank of legitimacy.
6. **Taxonomic feedback.** Once a classification becomes influential, later analysts may describe themselves through its categories, making the map partly constitutive of the territory.
7. **Geography and institutions.** London/Zürich histories matter, but ideas cross institutes and national boundaries.
8. **Clinical heterogeneity.** Actual technique is shaped by patient, phase, setting, analyst personality and private theory, not school alone.

Later historiography confirms both durability and contingency. The 2008 second edition of *The Cambridge Companion to Jung* still contains separate chapters on Classical, Archetypal and Developmental schools and even a single case treated from all three perspectives. Yet later handbooks increasingly organize the field by topics such as archetype, Self, complex, transference field, active imagination, body, gender, culture and social issues rather than by three exhaustive schools. The heuristic survived; the field outgrew any implication of completeness.

[Later historiography: Young-Eisendrath and Dawson, eds., *The Cambridge Companion to Jung*, 2nd ed., 2008; Papadopoulos and Garratt, eds., *The Second Handbook of Jungian Psychology*, 2026]

---

# Part VIII - Later Developments beyond the Original Taxonomy

## 64. Fordham after Samuels's Classification

Fordham remains the strongest primary anchor for the Developmental orientation in this corpus, but File 15 should be used for Fordham himself. The main routing points are:

- Self in infancy;
- deintegration and reintegration;
- ego development;
- early object relations;
- defenses of the Self;
- active imagination in developmental perspective;
- countertransference.

Samuels uses Fordham historiographically. File 15 should be activated when the research question is "What does Fordham actually argue?"

## 65. Knox

[Later comparison]

Jean Knox's *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis* was first published in 2003, eighteen years after Samuels's book. Knox explicitly interweaves analytical psychology and attachment theory and reframes the emergence of symbolic meaning through developmental psychology, cognitive science, image schemas, internal working models, trauma, reflective function, and the analytic relationship.

This clearly continues concerns associated with Samuels's Developmental School, but it also changes the theoretical terms. Knox's account of archetype is not merely Fordham repeated, and attachment theory is not simply another name for object relations. Her work is therefore best treated as **developmental-post-Jungian with major later interdisciplinary revision**, not as evidence that Samuels's 1985 Developmental category already contained attachment theory.

[Later comparison: Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, chs. 1-8]

## 66. Schwartz-Salant

[Later comparison]

Nathan Schwartz-Salant's *The Mystery of Human Relationship* (1998) develops the idea of an **interactive field** or "third area" between people. It combines alchemical imagination with close attention to mutual subjectivity, projective processes, chaos, embodiment and the transformation of relationship.

This later work crosses Samuels's earlier axes. It is developmental/relational in its attention to mutual influence; classical in its use of alchemy; and archetypal/imaginal in its treatment of symbolic field phenomena. Forcing Schwartz-Salant into one of the three schools would therefore lose exactly what his theory is designed to describe.

---

# Jean Knox: Archetype, Attachment, Development, and the Emergent Mind

## Source status and evidence note

This file reconstructs Jean Knox's *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis: Jungian Psychology and the Emergent Mind* as a major post-Jungian developmental argument. It is not a paraphrase of Jung, and it should not be used as evidence for what Jung himself originally meant unless a claim is separately checked against Jung's primary texts. Knox's book is valuable precisely because it revises several classical assumptions while retaining a recognisably depth-psychological concern with unconscious organization, symbolic meaning, fantasy, psychic multiplicity, and transformation.

Five levels of claim must remain distinct throughout this file:

1. **[JUNG PRIMARY]** Jung's own formulations in the Collected Works.
2. **[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]** earlier developmental Jungian work, especially Michael Fordham.
3. **[KNOX]** Knox's own reconstruction, criticism, and synthesis.
4. **[ATTACHMENT / DEVELOPMENTAL THEORY]** the developmental, cognitive, attachment, and neuropsychological research traditions on which Knox draws.
5. **[LABORATORY SYNTHESIS]** comparisons and practical research heuristics created for this corpus.

A later framework may illuminate a similar phenomenon without being identical to it. An internal working model is therefore not treated here as a Jungian complex; an attachment expectation is not automatically a projection; reflective function is not Jungian individuation; image schema is not simply the classical Jungian archetype; secure attachment is not the Self; and developmental explanation does not automatically exhaust symbolic meaning.

[Knox: *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis*, whole work]

## Source and Edition Note

**Exact source inspected:** Jean Knox, *Archetype, Attachment, Analysis: Jungian Psychology and the Emergent Mind*. First published in 2003 by Brunner-Routledge, Hove and New York; this Project copy is the Taylor & Francis e-Library edition published in 2005. Copyright © 2003 Jean Knox. The book contains a foreword by Peter Fonagy and eight chapters followed by bibliography and index.

The source is a 247-page PDF. Text extraction is strong and the printed pagination is stable enough to support page-level provenance. The table of contents gives the following sequence:

1. **Introduction**, pp. 1-10
2. **Jung's various models of archetypes**, pp. 11-38
3. **Archetypes and image schemas: a developmental perspective**, pp. 39-68
4. **The making of meaning: the formation of internal working models**, pp. 69-102
5. **Trauma and defences: their roots in relationship**, pp. 103-136
6. **Reflective function: the mind as an internal object**, pp. 137-164
7. **The process of change in analysis and the role of the analyst**, pp. 165-198
8. **Conclusions: science and symbols**, pp. 199-204

Bibliography begins at p. 205 and the index at p. 221. Page references in this reconstruction use the book's printed page numbers, not the PDF viewer's page count.

The role of this source in the Laboratory is specific. It is one of the main bridges between mature analytical psychology and contemporary developmental/attachment models, but it is not an empirical validation of Jung. Knox's distinctive contribution is to ask which Jungian ideas can be reformulated in ways compatible with evidence about development, cognition, memory, affect regulation, and relationship, and which formulations must be abandoned or sharply revised to achieve that compatibility.

[Knox: title page; copyright page; contents; pp. 1-204]

## Coverage of This File

This file reconstructs the full conceptual movement of Knox's monograph and then builds controlled comparisons with Jung, Fordham, adult attachment theory, Emotionally Focused Couple Therapy (EFCT), and Leslie Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy. It covers:

- Knox's critique of inherited symbolic contents and her four-model analysis of Jung's archetype concept;
- self-organization, emergence, image schemas, and representational redescription;
- internal working models, implicit memory, and attachment as a bridge between intrapsychic and interpersonal explanation;
- complexes, projection, trauma, defences, splitting, dissociation, and defensive fantasy;
- reflective function, narrative competence, intentionality, appraisal, agency, and psychological separateness;
- the analytic relationship, transference, countertransference, enactment, repair, new relational experience, and the formation of new internal working models;
- Knox's position in relation to Jung and Fordham;
- research-oriented implications for self-analysis and couple dynamics, clearly marked as Laboratory synthesis rather than Knox's own protocol.

## How to Use This File

For the question **"What does Knox argue?"**, prioritize sections labelled [KNOX] and the chapter-by-chapter reconstruction. For the question **"What did Jung say?"**, route to the primary Jung files rather than treating Knox as a substitute. For questions about attachment as a contemporary empirical field, route onward to the dedicated adult attachment source. For couple treatment process, route to Johnson's EFCT and Greenberg's Emotion-Focused Therapy files. For applied personal inquiry, use the Laboratory protocols only as hypothesis-generating structures, not as diagnostic instruments.

A useful reading discipline is:

**reconstruct Knox first → identify which prior theory she is revising → identify which empirical/developmental model she imports → compare constructs without declaring them equivalent → only then build an applied hypothesis.**

---

## Part I - Knox's Intellectual Project

### 1. The problem Knox is trying to solve

[KNOX]

Knox opens with the image of **fault lines**. Analytical psychology and psychoanalysis were historically separated by the Jung-Freud rupture, and each subsequently developed further schools whose theoretical assumptions can diverge sharply. Her concern is not primarily historical reconciliation. It is clinical epistemology: how can a depth psychology remain sensitive to the subjective, symbolic, poetic, and unconscious dimensions of experience while also taking seriously evidence about how minds actually develop, register information, store experience, and construct meaning?

She therefore rejects two comfortable positions. The first is that the analytic consulting room alone is a sufficient research method and that repeated clinical confirmation of a theory establishes its truth. She points out that the analyst's pre-existing model shapes what is noticed and how it is interpreted. The second is that experimental science can replace the hermeneutic task of analysis. Human beings do not live only as information-processing systems; they live in worlds of meaning. Knox's project is to connect these levels rather than choose one and discard the other.

This is why the book is not adequately described as "Jung plus attachment." The larger project joins at least four bodies of thought:

- analytical psychology, especially archetype, complex, symbol, individuation, and analytic relationship;
- developmental psychoanalytic thinking, particularly Fordham and post-Freudian work on internal objects;
- attachment theory, especially internal working models, secure/insecure patterns, and the development of affect regulation;
- cognitive/developmental science, especially self-organization, implicit memory, image schemas, representational redescription, theory of mind, and reflective function.

The organizing claim across the book is that much of what depth psychology has treated as pre-given psychic content can instead be understood as **emergent organization**. The mind has biological predispositions and species-typical developmental constraints, but complex symbolic meanings need not be genetically pre-inscribed. They can emerge reliably through the interaction of an embodied nervous system with a human relational environment.

[Knox: ch. 1, pp. 1-10]

### 2. Why the empirical question matters clinically

[KNOX]

Knox's epistemology has a direct clinical edge. An analyst's theory is not neutral because it informs the explanation offered to a vulnerable patient. If a clinician assumes that every inaccessible memory has been actively repressed, for example, the clinician may misread ordinary limits of memory or implicit encoding as evidence of hidden traumatic content. Conversely, a purely behavioural or cognitive account may miss the symbolic and emotional meanings through which the patient experiences the world.

Knox therefore treats information-processing knowledge as a **constraint on interpretation**, not as a replacement for interpretation. A depth-psychological explanation should remain compatible with what is known about memory, attention, representation, and development. This is an important methodological principle for the Laboratory: symbolic interpretation gains credibility when it does not require false claims about how memory or development works.

[Knox: ch. 1, especially pp. 2-7]

### 3. The architecture of Knox's argument

[KNOX]

The eight chapters form a deliberate sequence.

- Chapter 1 identifies the epistemological fault line between hermeneutic depth psychology and empirical developmental/cognitive research.
- Chapter 2 disaggregates the word **archetype** into several different models found in Jung and shows that they cannot all be simultaneously maintained.
- Chapter 3 proposes **emergent image schemas** as a developmental replacement for inherited representational content and links this with self-organization and Fordham's developmental thinking.
- Chapter 4 asks how later, more personal unconscious meanings are formed and stored, turning to **internal working models** and implicit memory.
- Chapter 5 uses those models to reconstruct **defence and trauma** as relationally rooted processes of affect regulation and meaning-making.
- Chapter 6 develops **reflective function** as a capacity to experience self and others as minded, intentional, separate subjects, linking this to narrative, agency, appraisal, and a developmental use of "individuation."
- Chapter 7 translates the model into a theory of **analytic change** involving activation of implicit relational expectations, narrative integration, new internal working models, repair, and the analyst's reflective participation.
- Chapter 8 argues for a non-reductive reconciliation of **science and symbols** through emergence.

The logic of the book is therefore cumulative: archetype is reconstructed first, then personal meaning and representation, then defence and reflective capacity, and finally treatment and symbolic life.

[Knox: ch. 1, pp. 7-10; chs. 2-8]

### 4. What Knox retains from Jung

[KNOX]

Knox's revision is substantial, but it is not a rejection of analytical psychology. Several enduring Jungian commitments remain central.

**First, unconscious organization is real and consequential.** People are shaped by patterns outside conscious awareness. Knox changes the proposed mechanism and developmental history of many such patterns, but she does not reduce psyche to deliberate cognition.

**Second, symbolic meaning matters.** Dreams, fantasy, metaphor, and image are not epiphenomenal decoration. What changes is the account of how symbolic capacity develops and how recurrent symbolic patterns arise.

**Third, psyche is plural and partially dissociable.** Jung's complex theory remains useful because states of mind can organize themselves around emotion and operate with relative autonomy. Knox connects this more explicitly with schemas, implicit memory, and dissociative organization.

**Fourth, psychological development involves increasing differentiation and dialogue.** Knox finds points of contact between Jung's differentiation of conscious and unconscious positions, the transcendent function, and the developmental achievement of recognizing independent subjectivity. She does not make these concepts identical.

**Fifth, the analytic relationship is transformative rather than merely didactic.** Jung's dialectical model of psychotherapy, in which analyst and patient affect one another, is compatible with Knox's emphasis on actual relational experience and therapist participation. Jung's own description of psychotherapy as a reciprocal dialectical process is an important point of continuity. [JUNG PRIMARY: CW 16, "Principles of Practical Psychotherapy," §1]

**Sixth, fantasy and symbolic transformation can carry forward a self-regulating tendency of psyche.** Knox does not deny constructive unconscious processes; she tries to explain their developmental and cognitive conditions.

These continuities should prevent a crude reading in which Knox is treated as replacing Jung with attachment theory. She is instead attempting to preserve the clinically and phenomenologically powerful parts of Jung while changing their explanatory foundations.

[Knox: chs. 1-3, 6-8]

### 5. What Knox revises or rejects

[KNOX]

Knox's strongest criticism concerns the claim that complex symbolic meanings or images are biologically inherited as psychic content. She distinguishes a biological predisposition from a representational instruction. Genes can influence neurodevelopment and orient attention; they do not need to carry images such as "mother," "hero," or mythic narratives in encoded symbolic form.

Her revisions include:

- **Inherited content → emergent structure.** Species-typical forms can reliably emerge without being stored as ready-made images.
- **Static archetypal contents → dynamic organizing patterns.** What is archetypal is increasingly located in patterns of relation, spatial organization, and transformation rather than in a fixed cast of inherited figures.
- **One archetype concept → several distinct models.** Jung's usage must be disaggregated before it can be evaluated.
- **Development as unfolding of predetermined psychic forms → development as self-organization in interaction.** Biological constraints, embodiment, and relationship all matter.
- **Internal objects as relatively static person-representations → representations of dynamic self-other relationships.** Attachment theory helps make this shift.
- **Unconscious as mainly repressed content → unconscious also as implicit, procedurally encoded information.** Lack of conscious access does not by itself establish repression.
- **Change through interpretation alone → change through interpretation, new experience, regulation, reflective capacity, narrative integration, and new implicit models.**

Knox also resists metaphysical inflation. In Chapter 2, one of the four archetype models she detects in Jung treats archetypes as eternal or transcendent realities independent of biological embodiment. That model cannot, in her view, simply be combined with a scientific developmental account. Her goal is not to disprove metaphysics but to prevent metaphysical claims from masquerading as empirical psychological mechanisms.

[Knox: ch. 2, pp. 11-38; ch. 8, pp. 199-204]

### Table 1. Jung primary and Knox reinterpretation

| Question | Jung primary, in broad terms | Knox's reconstruction | Non-equivalence to preserve |
|---|---|---|---|
| Archetype | A priori formal disposition/pattern of the collective unconscious, with Jung's texts also sometimes speaking in biological, representational, and metaphysical registers | Emergent, content-poor organizing structure best modelled as early image-schematic pattern | Knox is selecting and revising one strand in Jung, not merely translating his settled doctrine |
| Archetypal image | Concrete image produced when archetypal pattern enters representation | Metaphorical and representational elaboration built from image-schema organization plus experience | Recurrent imagery does not, for Knox, prove inherited imagery |
| Complex | Feeling-toned autonomous psychic organization | Retained, but read alongside schema, implicit memory, and relational models | Complex is not reduced to IWM or attachment style |
| Unconscious | Personal and collective, containing autonomous processes | Includes implicit information unavailable to awareness without requiring repression | Implicit memory is not the whole Jungian unconscious |
| Symbol | Best possible expression of something not yet fully known; transforms relation to unconscious | Symbolic meaning emerges through embodied and relational development and remains psychologically potent | Symbol is not reduced to ordinary sign or representation |
| Development | Individuation includes differentiation and integration across life; childhood less systematically theorized by Jung | Development begins in embodied relational organization from infancy and remains open throughout life | Developmental acquisition is not synonymous with individuation |
| Self | Archetype/totality and ordering centre beyond ego | Knox uses developmental selfhood, agency, and reflective representations without simply adopting the capital-S Self as explanatory mechanism | Everyday/developmental self cannot be substituted for Jung's Self |
| Transference | Projection and reciprocal analytic transformation are central | Activated implicit relational models, actual therapist behaviour, enactment, and new experience all matter | Transference is not only IWM activation and not every perception is distortion |

[Primary comparison: Jung CW 7, CW 8, CW 9i, CW 9ii, CW 16; Knox chs. 2-8]

---

## Part II - Archetype and Emergence

### 6. Jung's various models of archetypes

[KNOX]

Chapter 2 is the conceptual hinge of the book. Knox argues that "archetype" does not name one fully coherent thing across Jung's writings. She identifies **four broad models**.

#### Model 1: inherited biological entity or instruction

In this usage, archetypes are closely linked to inherited dispositions and can sound as though genetic inheritance contains information that specifies characteristic psychic forms. Knox accepts that biology supplies evolved constraints and predispositions, but rejects the idea that genes encode complex symbolic representations.

#### Model 2: abstract organizing framework or rule

Here the archetype is content-poor. It organizes experience without itself being an image or symbolic narrative. This is the Jungian strand Knox finds most compatible with developmental science. It can be reconstrued in terms of self-organizing patterns and image schemas.

#### Model 3: core symbolic meaning with representational content

In this usage, the archetype appears to contain or generate specific meanings or images. Knox argues that such content is better explained as developmentally constructed through embodied experience, metaphorical elaboration, culture, and relationship than as genetically transmitted psychic representation.

#### Model 4: metaphysical or eternal entity

At points, archetypes take on a reality that is not merely biological or psychological, appearing as eternal principles independent of the individual brain. Knox treats this as a metaphysical model. It may have philosophical or religious significance, but it cannot simply be asserted as an empirical developmental mechanism.

The importance of the four-model analysis is methodological. Knox is not saying that Jung was merely inconsistent or careless. She is showing that his archetype concept bears several explanatory burdens at once: biological inheritance, formal organization, recurrent symbolic meaning, and metaphysical universality. A contemporary reconstruction has to decide which claims can be retained and which require separate status.

[Knox: ch. 2, pp. 11-38]

### 7. Classical Jung archetype versus Knox's emergent archetype

| Dimension | Classical / broad Jungian formulation | Knox's emergent formulation |
|---|---|---|
| Basic status | A priori psychic pattern of the collective unconscious | Early emergent organizing pattern produced by self-organizing development |
| Innateness | Archetypal disposition is innate; some passages invite stronger readings of inherited content | Biological starting conditions are innate, but complex representational contents are not |
| Content | Archetype-as-such is formally distinct from archetypal image, though Jung's usage sometimes blurs form and content | Content-poor image schema is the proposed developmental analogue of archetype-as-such |
| Mechanism | Often described phenomenologically rather than through a detailed developmental mechanism | Embodied perception + self-organization + image schema + representational redescription + experience |
| Universality | Collective because rooted in species-typical psychic structure | Relative universality arises because similarly embodied humans develop under recurrent physical and relational constraints |
| Symbolic imagery | Archetypal pattern manifests in images and symbols | Imagery emerges through metaphorical elaboration of image-schematic structure and lived experience |
| Relation to genes | Jung sometimes uses biological inheritance analogies | Genes are catalysts/constraints, not symbolic blueprints |
| Relation to culture | Culture gives particular form to archetypal expression | Cultural and relational experience substantially shapes representational elaboration |
| Metaphysics | Jung sometimes moves toward psychoid or metaphysical formulations | Knox brackets or rejects metaphysical claims as scientific explanations |
| Clinical value | Amplification and symbolic interpretation locate experience in wider archetypal patterns | Symbolic work remains valid, but developmental history and relational organization must also be examined |

[Knox: ch. 2; ch. 3, especially pp. 63-67]

### 8. Emergence, self-organization, and the gene as catalyst

[KNOX]

Knox's alternative to both rigid innatism and blank-slate learning is **emergence**. A complex system can develop reliable forms through interaction among simpler components without those final forms being explicitly pre-programmed. The developing brain is biologically constrained, but its mature psychological organization is not a literal unfolding of symbolic instructions stored in genes.

This lets Knox preserve a species-typical dimension. Human infants share bodily architecture, perceptual biases, neural systems, dependency needs, and recurrent patterns of caregiver interaction. Similar organizing structures can therefore emerge across individuals and cultures without assuming that symbolic stories are inherited as content.

She uses the language of self-organization to shift attention from **objects as fixed contents** to **patterns of relationship**. The recurring form may lie in containment, force, linkage, path, balance, separation, part-whole organization, or other dynamic relations. Such patterns can then be elaborated into images, myths, dreams, and interpersonal expectations.

The gene in this model acts more like a **catalyst or constraint** than a blueprint containing a finished psychic picture. Development is neither purely endogenous nor purely imposed by the environment. It is relational in the broadest sense: organism and environment continually co-produce the resulting organization.

[Knox: ch. 3, pp. 39-53]

### 9. Image schemas

[KNOX]

Knox borrows the concept of **image schema** from developmental/cognitive work associated with Jean Mandler, Mark Johnson, George Lakoff, and related thinkers. Image schemas are very early, abstract patterns derived from the infant's perceptual and bodily organization of physical experience. They are not detailed pictures. They are dynamic Gestalts that capture recurrent relations such as:

- **PATH**
- **UP-DOWN**
- **CONTAINMENT**
- **FORCE**
- **PART-WHOLE**
- **LINK**
- related spatial and relational organizations.

Their importance is twofold. First, they are **preconceptual or minimally conceptual organizers**: they make experience intelligible before sophisticated language or autobiographical narrative exists. Second, they can be **metaphorically extended**. A bodily-spatial pattern such as containment can later organize emotional and symbolic meanings, for example being emotionally "held," "contained," "overwhelmed," or "empty."

Knox's strongest claim arrives in the conclusion of Chapter 3: developmental evidence suggests that archetypes **can be equated with image schemas in her reconstructed model**. She proposes that the image schema corresponds to the archetype-as-such, while archetypal images correspond to the many metaphorical and representational extensions that can be built from that underlying schema.

This statement must be handled precisely. It is **Knox's theoretical identification within her revised analytical psychology**. It is not a claim that cognitive science itself recognizes image schemas as Jungian archetypes, and it is not equivalent to saying that every classical use of "archetype" in Jung is reducible to an image schema. Her own Chapter 2 has already shown why the classical term carries multiple meanings.

[Knox: ch. 3, pp. 53-67, especially pp. 64-67]

### Figure note: Knox's containment diagram

[FIGURE: **Diagram of containment**, Knox, ch. 3, p. 66]

Knox includes a simple schematic figure to represent **containment** as an image-schematic relational pattern. Its conceptual function is more important than its graphic detail: the point is that an abstract pattern of inside/outside or containing/contained can precede and organize later emotional and symbolic elaborations. Knox uses this to argue that an experience of a caregiver as psychologically containing can be understood as a metaphorical extension of a more primitive image-schematic organization.

Interpretive status: **author's explanatory figure**, not a Jung diagram and not empirical proof that containment is an archetype.

### 10. Archetype versus image schema

| Dimension | Archetype in classical Jungian discourse | Image schema in Knox's developmental reconstruction | Critical guardrail |
|---|---|---|---|
| Innate predisposition | Yes, as a formal disposition of collective psyche | Biological/perceptual capacities enabling schema formation are innate; schema itself emerges | Do not call detailed symbolic imagery genetically inherited |
| Inherited image | Jung explicitly distinguishes archetype-as-such from image, though later readers often conflate them | No inherited image is required | "Archetype = inherited image" is the target of Knox's critique |
| Embodied basis | Present but not always developmentally specified | Central: bodily-spatial experience is foundational | Embodiment does not by itself prove archetypal status |
| Representation | Archetype-as-such is irrepresentable; images represent its manifestation | Image schema is an abstract representational Gestalt, usually nonconscious and content-poor | Knox's vocabulary crosses cognitive and Jungian registers |
| Development | Archetype precedes personal experience as disposition | Image schema develops very early through self-organization and interaction | Emergent does not mean arbitrarily learned |
| Universality | Collective/species-wide psychic pattern | Similar schemas can recur because humans share bodies, environments, and developmental regularities | Universality may be graded rather than absolute |
| Cultural variation | Archetypal patterns take culturally specific forms | Cultural experience shapes metaphorical and symbolic elaboration | Similar pattern does not imply identical symbolic meaning |
| Symbolic elaboration | Archetypal images, myths, dreams, symbols | Metaphorical extensions of image schemas plus personal/cultural experience | Symbolic richness cannot be read directly from the primitive schema |
| Knox's position | One historical source of the question | Proposed developmental reformulation | This is a post-Jungian revision, not Jung's own settled theory |

[Knox: ch. 3, pp. 53-67]

### 11. Archetype-as-such and archetypal image

[JUNG PRIMARY]

Jung's important distinction is between the **archetype-as-such**, which is not directly representable, and the **archetypal image**, which is the form in which an underlying disposition appears in consciousness, dream, fantasy, myth, or symbol. The distinction was intended to prevent the naive claim that a literal mythic image is inherited in the same way as a physical trait.

[KNOX]

Knox argues that Jung nevertheless frequently shifts among formal, biological, representational, and metaphysical registers. Her image-schema reformulation gives the distinction a developmental mechanism: the relatively abstract schema supplies an organizing Gestalt; later metaphorical extensions and experience create concrete imagery and meaning.

This has a significant consequence. Similar motifs across people need not prove that a specific motif was inherited. Similarity may arise because similar embodied patterns and relational structures are repeatedly elaborated in similar ways. What remains "archetypal" for Knox is the recurrent **dynamic pattern of relationship**, not a fixed stock of images.

A provocative example is the "mother archetype." Knox suggests that rather than positing a content-bearing archetypal mother, one can locate the archetypal level in a pattern such as **containment**. The child's experience of a particular mother as physically and psychologically containing is a personal and relational elaboration of that more abstract pattern. This does not mean that all mother imagery is reducible to containment; it illustrates Knox's shift from inherited object-content to emergent relational form.

[Knox: ch. 3, pp. 64-67]

### 12. Representational redescription

[KNOX]

A second developmental mechanism is **representational redescription**, drawn from Annette Karmiloff-Smith. The central idea is that mental knowledge can be reorganized and re-represented across development. Early knowledge may be implicit, procedural, or domain-specific. Through repeated processing it can become more flexible, accessible across domains, explicit, and eventually verbally articulable.

Knox uses this mechanism to explain how primitive embodied organizations can become rich symbolic meaning without assuming that the richness was present at the start. Image schemas provide an early scaffold; representational redescription enables increasingly complex symbolic, conceptual, and self-reflective forms.

This process becomes especially important in Chapter 7, where Knox extends it beyond childhood. Analysis can facilitate new ways of representing previously implicit patterns, making them available to language, narrative, reflection, and alternative action. Development therefore remains open in adulthood.

[Knox: ch. 3, pp. 55-63; ch. 7, pp. 195-198]

### 13. Knox and Fordham on emergence

[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT]

Michael Fordham's developmental analytical psychology is the most important Jungian precursor for Knox. Fordham proposed an original or primary psychosomatic Self and processes of **deintegration and reintegration** through which the infant engages objects and incorporates experience. His model makes development and relationship integral to analytical psychology rather than treating childhood merely as a prelude to later individuation.

[KNOX]

Knox sees a meaningful analogy between Fordham's deintegration-reintegration and representational redescription: both describe repeated opening to experience followed by reorganization into more differentiated forms. But she does not simply inherit Fordham's metapsychology. She is more cautious about treating a primary Self as a pre-existing psychic totality with rich archetypal content. She can reinterpret it as **psychosomatic potential** that becomes organized in interaction.

The difference is consequential. Fordham remains within a strongly Jungian Self vocabulary; Knox increasingly translates developmental organization into terms such as self-organization, image schema, representation, attachment, and reflective function. Her model therefore inherits Fordham's developmental impulse while changing the explanatory machinery.

[Fordham: *Explorations into the Self*, chs. 2-3; Knox: ch. 3, especially pp. 56-58]

### Emergent Archetype Map

**[LABORATORY HEURISTIC MAP based on Knox, not a literal diagram from the book]**

```text
species-typical bodily / neural predispositions
                +
recurrent interaction with the physical and relational environment
                ↓
self-organizing perceptual analysis
                ↓
early image-schematic Gestalts
(PATH, CONTAINMENT, FORCE, LINK, etc.)
                ↓
representational redescription + lived experience
                ↓
metaphorical extension across domains
                ↓
personal + cultural representation
                ↓
symbolic elaboration, fantasy, dream imagery, archetypal meaning
```

Guardrail: the final symbolic image is not assumed to have existed in the genome or in the early image schema. The map is intended to show Knox's developmental route from embodied constraint to symbolic richness.

[Knox: ch. 3, pp. 39-68]

---

## Part III - Development, Attachment and Representation

### 14. From image schema to personal meaning

[KNOX]

Chapter 3 addresses early, relatively impersonal organizing forms. Chapter 4 asks a different question: **how does a person acquire the particular unconscious meanings that characterize his or her relational life?** The answer cannot be supplied by image schemas alone. Containment, force, path, and linkage are too abstract to explain why one person expects closeness to end in rejection while another expects a caregiver to be available.

Knox therefore turns to **mental representation**, **implicit memory**, and **internal working models**. The key shift is from generic patterns of physical and relational organization to generalized models built from actual experience with caregivers and later relationships.

[Knox: ch. 4, pp. 69-102]

### 15. Mental representation and internalization

[KNOX]

Knox reframes the psychoanalytic language of "internal objects." Internalization is not the literal movement of another person into the mind. It is an information-processing process in which patterns of interaction are encoded, generalized, and stored as models of self, other, and relationship.

This matters because an internal object is easily imagined as a static inner picture of "mother" or "father." Knox prefers a more dynamic representation: **what tends to happen between me and someone like this; what I can expect; what I must do; what emotions are safe; what response follows my need; what kind of person I become in this relationship.**

The representation is therefore relational and predictive. It organizes perception before conscious deliberation. It may influence which cues are noticed, what they mean, what emotion is mobilized, and which defensive or attachment strategy follows.

[Knox: ch. 4, pp. 69-81]

### 16. Internal working models

[ATTACHMENT / DEVELOPMENTAL THEORY]

Bowlby's concept of the **internal working model (IWM)** gives Knox a way to articulate the internalization of repeated attachment experience. An IWM is a generalized model of self and attachment figure in relationship. It carries expectations about availability, response, protection, rejection, need, and worth. It is "working" because it guides prediction and action rather than functioning as a passive memory archive.

[KNOX]

Knox stresses several features:

- IWMs are built from actual patterns of relationship rather than from instinctual fantasy alone.
- They can operate outside conscious awareness.
- They generalize beyond individual episodes and therefore persist even when no single autobiographical memory can explain them.
- They shape current perception by assimilating new relationships to established expectations.
- Different and even contradictory models can coexist and become dominant in different emotional states.
- Strong affect can retrieve older, less reflective models and narrow the range of interpretation.
- They integrate cognitive, emotional, and behavioural tendencies rather than being purely intellectual beliefs.

The IWM becomes one of Knox's main bridges between attachment theory and depth psychology because it explains how relational history can become an unconscious organizing structure.

[Knox: ch. 4, especially pp. 78-87]

### Developmental Relational Map

**[LABORATORY HEURISTIC MAP based on Knox]**

```text
repeated caregiver interaction
          ↓
perception + affect + regulation experience
          ↓
implicit generalization of self-other pattern
          ↓
internal working model
          ↓
expectations of availability / rejection / response
          ↓
affect regulation strategy + perception of new relationships
          ↓
behaviour that may confirm, challenge, or revise the model
```

This map is intentionally narrower than the psyche as a whole. It describes one major relational learning pathway. It does not replace complex theory, symbolic interpretation, culture, temperament, current circumstances, or conscious choice.

[Knox: ch. 4]

### 17. Implicit memory and implicit relational knowledge

[KNOX]

A central argument of the book is that **unconscious does not mean repressed**. Information can remain outside awareness because of the format in which it was encoded and stored. Early relational learning may be implicit, procedural, affective, and generalized rather than episodic and verbally recallable.

This has several consequences.

First, a person can repeatedly enact a relational expectation without possessing an autobiographical narrative that explains it. Lack of a childhood memory is not evidence that nothing happened, but neither is it evidence that a traumatic memory has been repressed.

Second, interpretation aimed at "recovering" an event may miss the relevant level. The clinically important structure can be the generalized pattern itself: for example, "need leads to humiliation," "dependence invites abandonment," or "anger makes the other disappear." Such expectations may be activated as present experience rather than remembered as past knowledge.

Third, implicit models are embodied and affective. They are expressed through timing, approach/withdrawal, bodily arousal, attention, tone, and action as well as through conscious beliefs.

Fourth, change can occur at the implicit level through repeated new relational experience. Insight may support change, but insight alone does not automatically overwrite a procedural expectation.

This is one of Knox's strongest clinical correctives to a theory of the unconscious based exclusively on repression and interpretation.

[Knox: ch. 1, pp. 4-7; ch. 4, pp. 82-98; ch. 7, pp. 178-198]

### 18. Internal working model versus Jungian complex

[KNOX]

Knox explicitly brings attachment IWMs into dialogue with Jung's **feeling-toned complex**. She notes real similarities. A complex is organized around affect; it gathers perceptions, memories, fantasies, and attitudes; it can become relatively autonomous; and when constellated it can organize consciousness and behaviour. In information-processing language, it therefore resembles a schema that is partly or wholly outside awareness.

Yet Knox does not need the two constructs to be identical. The complex belongs to Jung's dissociation-based model of psychic organization and often carries a broader symbolic and archetypal dimension. An IWM is more specifically a generalized relational model derived from attachment experience. A complex can incorporate attachment-based expectations, but it can also organize material whose meaning cannot be exhausted by attachment lineage.

[Knox: ch. 4, section "Analytical psychology and internal working models," pp. 87-94]

### Table 2. Complex versus internal working model

| Dimension | Jungian complex | Internal working model in attachment theory / Knox's use | Overlap without identity |
|---|---|---|---|
| Theoretical lineage | Jung's association experiments, dissociation theory, analytical psychology | Bowlby/Ainsworth attachment theory; cognitive-developmental representation | Both describe organized patterns that can operate outside awareness |
| Core construct | Feeling-toned cluster of representations with relative autonomy | Generalized predictive model of self, attachment figure, and relationship | Both organize perception and response |
