| Origin | Personal experience plus, in Jungian theory, possible archetypal organization | Repeated attachment and caregiving experience, constrained by development | Attachment experience can contribute to a complex |
| Affect | Constitutive: complex is feeling-toned and can seize affect | Integral: model includes affective expectations and regulation strategies | Strong affect can activate either formulation |
| Memory | Can gather memories, images, fantasies, bodily responses | Often implicit/generalized; may be linked to episodic memories but does not require conscious recollection | Both can persist without clear autobiographical explanation |
| Relational expectation | Often present but not required as the defining feature | Central to the construct | A relational complex may substantially overlap an IWM |
| Symbolic content | May include dreams, fantasy, archetypal imagery, symbolic meaning | Need not be symbolic; primarily predictive/relational representation | Symbolic material may elaborate an attachment model but is not required by IWM theory |
| Archetypal dimension | Can be understood as having an archetypal nucleus or relation to collective patterns | Not part of attachment theory | Knox may place both in one developmental system without making attachment theory archetypal theory |
| Activation | "Constellation" can disrupt ego control and narrow consciousness | Relational cue or affective state activates model, often automatically | A single episode may be described at both levels if evidence supports both |
| Behavioural effect | Can produce stereotyped, autonomous, disproportionate responses | Guides proximity, avoidance, hyperactivation, deactivation, interpretation, and action | Both can create self-confirming patterns |
| Conscious access | Variable; often partly unconscious | Often implicit and not directly verbalizable | Reflection can increase access but not instantly dissolve the pattern |
| Change process | Differentiation, awareness, symbolic integration, withdrawal of projection, broader ego-Self relation | New relational experience, revised expectation, integration, affect regulation, reflective function | Analytic work can pursue both levels simultaneously |
| Evidence base | Clinical observations, association experiments, analytical theory | Developmental/attachment observational and experimental research plus clinical extension | Different evidence traditions should not be treated as interchangeable |

**Bottom line:** an IWM is not a complex. A relational IWM can be one important information-processing component inside a complex constellation, and a complex formulation can add affective autonomy and symbolic depth that IWM language by itself does not specify.

[Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory"; Knox ch. 4]

### 19. Complex constellation versus IWM activation

This distinction is mandatory because the two events can look similar in lived experience.

**Complex constellation** describes a state in which an emotionally charged psychic organization becomes active and temporarily reorganizes attention, memory, interpretation, affect, and will. The person may feel "taken over," narrowed, or unlike his or her more reflective state. [JUNG PRIMARY: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory"]

**IWM activation** describes retrieval of a learned relational model that predicts how an attachment-relevant interaction will unfold. The activation may be subtle and need not feel dramatic. It can bias perception toward signs consistent with the model and mobilize attachment regulation strategies. [KNOX: ch. 4; ch. 7]

A cue such as delayed contact from a partner could therefore be understood in several ways:

- as a present, factually meaningful event requiring no deep interpretation;
- as activation of an IWM expecting abandonment;
- as constellation of a broader abandonment-related complex;
- as both IWM activation and complex constellation;
- as a symbolic trigger with meanings not reducible to attachment;
- or as some combination whose relative importance must be investigated rather than assumed.

The constructs answer different questions. IWM language asks, **"What learned self-other prediction has been retrieved?"** Complex language asks, **"What affectively autonomous psychic organization has become constellated, and what wider material does it gather?"**

### 20. Attachment theory in Knox

[KNOX]

Attachment theory gives Knox more than a typology of secure and insecure relationships. It provides a model of how **interpersonal regulation becomes intrapsychic organization**.

The infant does not begin with mature self-regulation. Regulation is initially dyadic: caregiver and infant co-regulate arousal, distress, proximity, exploration, and recovery. Repeated experiences become generalized expectations. Over time, the child increasingly carries a model of whether another person is available, whether signals will be noticed, whether distress can be soothed, and whether his or her own needs are legitimate.

This model also makes cognition and emotion inseparable. An attachment expectation is not simply a proposition like "people leave." It carries affective readiness, bodily arousal, attentional bias, and a strategy for what to do next. This is why attachment theory is useful to Knox's attempt to explain complexes and defences without reducing them to conscious beliefs.

Knox's attachment evidence base is early-2000s attachment science. The Laboratory should therefore not treat her 2003 account as a complete statement of the later adult attachment literature. The dedicated attachment file should control claims about the broader empirical field.

[Knox: ch. 4; chs. 5-7]

### 21. Affect regulation

[KNOX]

Affect regulation is distributed across the whole developmental model. In infancy, regulation is interpersonal before it becomes increasingly internalized. Attachment patterns are partly strategies for managing distress in relation to expected caregiver response. In later life, similar strategies can shape whether emotion is amplified, suppressed, dissociated, transformed into action, or made available to reflective thought.

This intersects with Jungian complex theory because a complex is affectively charged and constellation can reduce ego flexibility. But the explanatory emphasis differs. Jung's language highlights the autonomy of the activated psychic organization. Attachment language highlights the history and function of a regulation strategy in relationship.

For Knox, both perspectives become clinically useful when they prevent moralizing. A defensive pattern may be maladaptive in the present while still having originated as a protective solution to a relational environment. Understanding the function of the pattern does not imply that it should remain unchanged.

[Knox: chs. 4-5]

### Comparative Psyche Map

**[LABORATORY HEURISTIC MAP, not a literal diagram from Jung or Knox]**

```text
JUNGIAN LEVEL
archetypal disposition / psychic pattern
            ↓
complex constellation + archetypal image / symbol
            ↓
conscious-unconscious tension, projection, differentiation, integration

KNOX DEVELOPMENTAL-RELATIONAL LEVEL
early embodied self-organization
            ↓
image schemas + repeated relational experience
            ↓
implicit internal working models / defensive organization
            ↓
representation of self-other + affect regulation
            ↓
symbolic elaboration / narrative / reflective function
            ↓
new relational experience + revised meaning + increased agency
```

The maps are **complementary lenses**, not a conversion table. The same clinical episode can be described in both vocabularies, but the explanation is not the same merely because the descriptions overlap.

---

## Part IV - Self, Agency and Reflective Function

### 22. Development of selfhood

[KNOX]

Knox's language of "self" is more developmental and relational than Jung's capital-S **Self**. She is interested in how a person comes to experience himself or herself as a psychological subject: someone who has emotions, intentions, preferences, memories, continuity, agency, and a mind that is separate from other minds.

This developmental selfhood depends on relationship. The infant discovers mind partly through being treated as minded. A caregiver who responds not merely to behaviour but to the state of mind that behaviour expresses helps the child construct a representation of himself or herself as an intentional being. Over time, the child internalizes not only a caregiver but a caregiver **who has a mental representation of the child**.

This is the background to reflective function. Development is not simply accumulation of content. It is the construction of higher-order representations: models of one's own models, awareness that perceptions may be interpretations, recognition that other minds differ, and capacity to place immediate emotion within a larger narrative.

[Knox: ch. 6, pp. 137-164]

### 23. Reflective function and mentalisation

[KNOX]

Knox uses the concept of **reflective function**, drawing especially on Peter Fonagy and colleagues. The basic capacity is to experience behaviour as arising from mental states and to recognize oneself and others as psychological subjects with beliefs, feelings, intentions, desires, and perspectives.

Knox emphasizes that this capacity is not simply cold "theory of mind." It includes the emotional and intentional world. She identifies four interrelated elements:

1. **Narrative competence**: linking events through psychological cause and effect, creating coherence and a basis for agency.
2. **Intentionality**: possessing and recognizing desires, purposes, and goals, what Knox describes as a capacity for mental appetite.
3. **Appraisal**: evaluating the significance of an event rather than merely registering it.
4. **Individuation**, in Knox's developmental usage: recognizing one's own and other people's independent subjectivity.

This fourth term requires strict routing discipline. Knox is using "individuation" for an achievement of psychological separateness and recognition of distinct minds. It resonates with parts of Jungian individuation but is not identical to Jung's lifelong process of differentiation from collective identification and relation between ego and Self.

[Knox: ch. 6, especially pp. 141-143]

### 24. Reflective function as an internal relational achievement

[KNOX]

The caregiver's reflective capacity is crucial because the child's mental states initially require external recognition. Knox draws on research suggesting that caregivers who can think about the child's mind help the child develop secure attachment and reflective capacity. Mirroring is not mere copying. The parent responds in a modulated, marked way that signals, in effect, "I recognize this feeling as yours and can represent it without being overwhelmed by it."

The child thereby acquires two related representations:

- **I have a mind with identifiable states.**
- **Another mind can hold my state in representation without becoming identical with it.**

This is a developmental root of psychological separateness. It also becomes a clinical template: the analyst's capacity to represent the patient's mind can help the patient develop a more differentiated model of his or her own states.

[Knox: ch. 6, pp. 139-145; ch. 7, pp. 190-194]

### 25. Narrative competence and agency

[KNOX]

Narrative is not merely storytelling in the aesthetic sense. For Knox, a narrative links events through minds. Desire, intention, choice, fear, interpretation, and action create psychological cause and effect. Without such links, experience can feel random and meaningless.

A person with stronger narrative competence can ask not only "what happened?" but also "what was I expecting, wanting, fearing, or trying to prevent?" This introduces **agency**. Agency does not mean omnipotent control. It means being able to represent oneself as one causal participant in a field of events, capable of intention and revision.

Knox describes psychotherapy as a **narrative dialogue**. The therapist may initially hold a coherent account that the patient cannot yet construct. The patient can then take over, modify, reject, and refine that narrative. A successful theory is therefore not one that the patient passively accepts as the analyst's truth. It supports the patient's own capacity to make and revise meaning.

This is one reason Knox is wary of dogmatic interpretation. An imposed theory can substitute the analyst's mind for the patient's agency. A useful interpretation increases the patient's ability to think.

[Knox: ch. 6, pp. 145-150]

### 26. Intentionality and mental appetite

[KNOX]

Intentionality refers to the capacity to have purposes and to recognize them as one's own. Knox uses this to understand states in which a person appears unable to know what he or she wants and instead lives mainly in response to other people's demands.

She distinguishes **appetite** from indiscriminate greed. Appetite presupposes a mind that can identify an object of desire, seek it, evaluate satisfaction, and stop. In Knox's formulation, this is a reflective achievement. A collapse of intentionality can leave behaviour feeling "mindless," not because there is literally no motivation, but because the person cannot represent the motivation and regulate it as an owned mental state.

In Laboratory terms, this suggests a useful distinction between **intense affect** and **owned intention**. The former can be powerful without the latter being available. Increasing agency may therefore involve making intention representable rather than simply increasing willpower.

[Knox: ch. 6, pp. 148-151]

### 27. Appraisal

[KNOX]

Appraisal is the process by which an event acquires significance. Attachment distress is not generated by physical events alone; it depends on what the event means in relation to safety, availability, rejection, and expected response. The same separation can therefore produce different levels of distress depending on context and expectation.

This is important for understanding both defence and couple conflict. A cue such as a quiet tone, delay, disagreement, or withdrawal is not psychologically potent only because of its objective form. It is appraised through existing models. The appraisal can be accurate, distorted, defensive, symbolic, or some mixture. Knox's model therefore avoids a simple opposition between "fact" and "projection": subjective meaning is constructed, but construction does not imply falsity.

[Knox: ch. 6, pp. 142-144; ch. 7, pp. 179-181]

### 28. Jung's Self, Fordham's developmental Self, and Knox's developmental selfhood

| Dimension | Jung's Self | Fordham's developmental Self | Knox's developing selfhood / agency |
|---|---|---|---|
| Basic meaning | Totality and ordering centre encompassing conscious and unconscious; archetypal and not reducible to ego | Primary psychosomatic totality/potential present from the beginning; develops through deintegration and reintegration | Emergent representations of self as minded, intentional, separate, affective, and agentic subject |
| Developmental starting point | Self is not simply acquired by development; it is a structural/archetypal principle | Primary Self is foundational and active in infancy | Selfhood develops through embodied interaction, attachment, representation, and reflective function |
| Relation to ego | Ego is centre of consciousness and only part of total psyche | Ego develops within Self processes | Reflective self-representation and agency become increasingly explicit; no simple capital-S equivalent is required |
| Relation to others | Individuation requires differentiation from projections and collective identifications but remains relational | Object engagement is built into deintegration/reintegration | Relationship is constitutive: caregiver recognition helps generate mind-as-internal-object |
| Symbolic dimension | Self appears in symbols of totality, mandalas, coniunctio, etc. | Self can organize developmental and symbolic processes | Symbolic capacity emerges developmentally; selfhood can participate in symbolic life without being identical to Jung's Self |
| Clinical aim | More adequate ego-Self relation; individuation | Development through renewed deintegration/reintegration and integration | Increased reflective function, narrative coherence, revised relational models, affect regulation, and agency |
| Key risk if conflated | Everyday authenticity gets mistaken for transpersonal/total psychic Self | Primary Self becomes treated as the same as adult Jungian Self | Developmental self-improvement gets mislabeled as individuation |

[Primary Jung comparison: CW 7; CW 9ii; Fordham: *Explorations into the Self*, chs. 1-6; Knox: chs. 3, 6-8]

### 29. Individuation versus developmental / attachment change

| Process | Central question | Typical mechanism | What improvement might look like | Why it is not a synonym for the others |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Jungian individuation | How can ego become more differentiated from collective identifications and enter a more conscious relation with unconscious totality? | Differentiation, confrontation with shadow/anima/animus, symbolic work, transcendent function, withdrawal of projection | Broader personality, greater integration of opposites, less collective or projective identification | Includes an archetypal/ego-Self dimension beyond attachment functioning |
| Increased reflective function | Can I represent my own and another person's mental states while remaining aware that representations can be revised? | Mental-state representation, narrative, appraisal, separateness | More perspective-taking, psychological causality, flexible interpretation | A metacognitive-relational capacity, not a full theory of individuation |
| Increased attachment security | Can closeness, need, exploration, separation, and repair be handled with greater trust and flexibility? | New relational experience, revised working models, regulation | More flexible proximity seeking, less rigid hyperactivation/deactivation | Security does not imply symbolic integration or confrontation with the wider unconscious |
| Changed internal working model | Has a specific predictive self-other schema been updated? | Repeated disconfirming experience and new representation | Less automatic expectation of abandonment/rejection/unavailability | A model-level change can occur without broad personality transformation |
| Increased agency | Can I experience myself as having intentions, choices, and effects without omnipotence? | Narrative competence, intentionality, reflection, action | More owned desire, responsibility, and choice | Agency is one developmental achievement, not psychic totality |

[Knox: ch. 6; ch. 7; primary Jung comparison: CW 7, CW 8]

---

## Part V - Trauma, Defence and Dissociation

### 30. Trauma and defences as relationship-rooted processes

[KNOX]

Chapter 5 is where Knox's developmental model becomes clinically sharper. Her aim is not to abolish psychoanalytic defence concepts but to relocate their roots. Instead of treating defence chiefly as a response to instinctual conflict, she asks how defensive organization develops when relationship itself becomes a source of threat, shame, abandonment, intrusion, or unmanageable affect.

This changes the central question from **"What instinctual wish is being defended against?"** to a wider set of questions:

- What meaning did the relationship acquire for the child?
- Which affect became intolerable?
- What prediction about self and other was formed?
- What strategy protected attachment, self-worth, or psychological coherence?
- What had to be excluded, split off, distorted, or transformed in fantasy for the relationship to remain survivable?

Attachment theory is especially useful because a child cannot simply leave an attachment system that is necessary for protection and regulation. A strategy can therefore be paradoxical: it may preserve attachment by suppressing awareness of need, amplifying distress, idealizing a caregiver, blaming the self, or compartmentalizing incompatible representations.

Knox's model is functional rather than moralistic. Defence is not merely irrational resistance. It can be an adaptation that protected a developing mind under conditions in which a more integrated representation was too painful or destabilizing. The same strategy may later become rigid and costly.

[Knox: ch. 5, pp. 103-136]

### 31. Repression, splitting, projection, projective identification, and dissociation

[KNOX]

Knox reviews several traditional defence concepts and interprets them through information processing and attachment.

**Repression** is not the only reason material is unavailable to awareness. Some information was never encoded in verbally accessible form. Where repression is invoked, Knox prefers a developmental account in which conscious or semi-conscious avoidance can become increasingly automatic. The key guardrail is that **absence of conscious memory does not prove active repression**.

**Splitting** can be understood as the maintenance of incompatible representations in insufficiently integrated systems. A person may have one self-other model in one state and a contradictory model in another. This gives attachment theory a way to understand abrupt shifts without assuming that a single coherent representation is always active.

**Projection** involves locating in the other what is not recognized as one's own psychic contribution. Knox's attachment model can explain part of the process as the activation of an established relational expectation that structures perception. Yet she does not abolish the psychodynamic meaning of projection, and Chapter 7 explicitly allows that a patient may sometimes perceive something real in the analyst.

**Projective identification** adds an interpersonal component: unconscious expectations can exert pressure on the other person to occupy the predicted role. In analysis, the therapist may feel pulled to become rejecting, rescuing, controlling, helpless, admiring, or punitive. The mechanism can be described as an enacted script carried partly by subtle behaviour, affect, timing, and expectation.

**Dissociation** is especially important because it links Jung's early complex theory with developmental trauma. Knox distinguishes different routes into dissociation rather than treating every discontinuity as the same mechanism. Trauma may directly fragment experience; repeated defensive exclusion can also produce separated clusters of memory, affect, and self-representation.

[Knox: ch. 5]

### 32. Defensive processes as higher-order working models

[KNOX]

One of Knox's useful innovations is to treat defence not as a detached mental "mechanism" but as part of the relational model itself. The person may carry not only a prediction of what the other will do but also a prediction of **what must be done internally to survive the expected response**.

A working model can therefore contain both interpersonal and defensive information:

```text
If I show need → I expect rejection.
Therefore I suppress need before it becomes visible.
If another person approaches → I expect engulfment.
Therefore I detach, intellectualize, or attack before dependence develops.
If disagreement means loss of love → I amplify distress until the other re-engages.
```

These are schematic examples for explanation, not Knox quotations and not attachment diagnoses. Their purpose is to show why a defensive strategy can become self-confirming. The action generated by the model changes the relationship, which may then produce the very response the person feared.

This relational-functional view is especially important for couple analysis. A protective action can be simultaneously intrapsychic and interpersonal: it regulates the actor's affect while becoming a cue that activates the partner's own expectations.

[Knox: ch. 5; ch. 7]

### 33. Trauma, meaning, and defensive fantasy

[KNOX]

Knox does not eliminate fantasy in favour of literal developmental history. Instead she asks what defensive work fantasy may perform. A traumatic or humiliating relationship can be psychically reorganized into a story that is less unbearable than the original meaning.

For example, it may be less threatening for a child to construct a grandiose or persecutory explanation than to know that a necessary caregiver is unreliable, cruel, or indifferent. The constructed fantasy can preserve continuity, attachment, or self-esteem at a price. It may distort reality, but its distortion has a regulatory function.

This is where Knox's position differs from a crude environmentalism. She does not say that psychic life is a transparent copy of what literally happened. Experience is **interpreted, appraised, defended against, symbolized, and reconstructed**. Internal reality is actively made.

Fantasy can therefore be both **defensive and creative**. It can conceal an intolerable meaning while also providing an imaginative structure through which the psyche survives. Analysis must ask what a fantasy does, not merely whether it is historically true or false.

[Knox: ch. 5, sections on trauma and defensive fantasy]

### 34. Kalsched, archetypal defences, and Knox's developmental revision

[POST-JUNGIAN DEVELOPMENT / KNOX]

Knox engages Donald Kalsched's idea of archetypal defences in severe trauma. Kalsched describes powerful inner figures that can become persecutory or protective in a way that appears larger than personal experience. Knox takes seriously the phenomenology of these figures but seeks a developmental explanation for their organization.

Her image-schema model offers one route. Severe trauma may disrupt ordinary links among affect, image, autobiographical context, and reflective meaning. Primitive organizing patterns can then become elaborated into compelling defensive images. The resulting figure can feel autonomous and numinous without requiring the conclusion that its representational content was innately present.

This is a good example of Knox's broader method. She does not dismiss a Jungian clinical observation because its classical explanation is questionable. She tries to preserve the phenomenon and replace the mechanism.

[Knox: ch. 5]

### 35. Dissociative and constructive functions of defence

[KNOX]

Defence has two broad faces in Knox's account.

**Dissociative defence** keeps incompatible or intolerable meanings apart. A person may be unable to hold love and anger toward the same object, strength and dependence in the same self-representation, or safety and danger in one account of a caregiver. Separate internal working models can alternate instead of being integrated.

**Constructive defence** creates a new meaning that is less threatening. The psyche does not merely cut connections; it can build a story, fantasy, idealization, or explanatory system that protects continuity and self-worth.

These functions can coexist. A defensive story may be highly coherent within itself while keeping another body of experience dissociated. This makes clinical work more complex than "discover the repressed fact." The task can involve recognizing why one model was necessary, increasing tolerance of the disowned affect, and building a representation capable of holding contradiction without collapse.

[Knox: ch. 5]

### 36. Dissociation: Jungian and developmental perspectives

| Question | Early / primary Jungian emphasis | Knox's developmental-attachment emphasis | Guardrail |
|---|---|---|---|
| What dissociates? | Feeling-toned complexes and psychic systems can gain relative autonomy | Memories, affects, self-representations, relational models, and meanings may remain insufficiently integrated | "Dissociation" is a family of processes, not one mechanism |
| Why? | Psychic conflict, affect, incompatibility with ego attitude; autonomy of complexes | Trauma, defensive exclusion, attachment threat, intolerable appraisal, failures of integration | A developmental account can supplement rather than erase complex theory |
| What does it look like? | Being "possessed" by a complex, narrowed consciousness, gaps in voluntary control | State-dependent shifts, contradictory self-other models, discontinuities of affect and narrative | Similar phenomenology does not establish identical mechanism |
| Role of relationship | Present in Jung but less systematically developmental | Central: relational environments shape what must be excluded or compartmentalized | Not all dissociation is attachment-caused |
| Treatment emphasis | Bring complex into relation with consciousness; symbolic integration | Integrate models, build narrative, increase reflective function, create safer relational experience | Both interpretation and new experience may matter |

[Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "A Review of the Complex Theory"; Knox chs. 4-7]

### 37. Trauma and neurobiology: explanatory pluralism

[KNOX]

Knox also brings neurobiology into the discussion, but she does not use it as a trump card. Trauma can produce rapid, automatic affective responses that precede reflective appraisal. Later avoidance can reinforce the separation between bodily-emotional activation and coherent autobiographical account. Such models help explain why a person may react intensely without being able to say why.

The methodological lesson is pluralist. One episode can be described at several levels:

- neural threat response;
- implicit memory retrieval;
- attachment-system activation;
- defensive appraisal;
- dissociated self-state;
- complex constellation;
- symbolic image or fantasy;
- conscious interpersonal meaning.

A responsible formulation does not assume that one level invalidates all others. It asks which level explains which feature of the evidence.

[Knox: ch. 5; ch. 7, pp. 194-196]

---

## Part VI - Symbol, Imagination and Meaning

### 38. Symbol formation in Knox's model

[KNOX]

A common misreading of Knox is that developmental explanation demotes symbol to a secondary by-product. Chapter 8 makes the opposite ambition clear. She wants a model that can explain **how symbolic meaning becomes possible** without requiring inherited complex images.

The sequence is developmental but not reductive:

1. embodied perception provides recurrent spatial-dynamic structure;
2. image schemas organize those structures in a primitive representational form;
3. representational redescription makes knowledge increasingly flexible and cross-domain;
4. metaphor extends bodily-spatial relations into emotional, social, and abstract domains;
5. relational experience provides personal affective meaning;
6. culture supplies languages, narratives, rituals, myths, and symbols;
7. fantasy and dream recombine these materials into new formations;
8. reflective function allows the person to recognize and elaborate meaning rather than merely enact it.

The resulting symbol can be psychologically deeper than its developmental ingredients. Saying that symbolic capacity has developmental conditions does not mean that a symbol's meaning is exhausted by tracing those conditions.

[Knox: ch. 3; ch. 4; ch. 6; ch. 8]

### 39. Jungian symbol versus ordinary representation

[JUNG PRIMARY]

In Jung's technical usage, a symbol is not merely a sign that points to a known meaning. It is an expression that carries more than consciousness can fully formulate and can mediate between opposed or partially unknown psychic positions.

[KNOX]

Knox uses cognitive-developmental language of representation without thereby reducing every Jungian symbol to a representation. Her model concerns the **conditions and processes through which symbolic thought develops**. A representation can encode a learned relation; a symbol can become a site where multiple levels of meaning are held together and transformed.

For the Laboratory, this distinction is essential. A dream image of a locked room can be investigated as:

- a memory-linked representation;
- an attachment-related expectation of inaccessibility;
- a defensive metaphor;
- an image-schematic elaboration of containment/boundary;
- a Jungian symbol with meanings that exceed the available causal story;
- a response to present circumstances.

These are hypotheses at different levels. Deeper-sounding interpretations are not automatically better.

[Knox: chs. 3, 6-8; Primary Jung comparison: CW 6 definitions; CW 8, "The Transcendent Function"]

### Table 3. Symbolic meaning versus developmental explanation

| Question | Symbolic / Jungian lens | Knox developmental lens | Integrative discipline |
|---|---|---|---|
| What does the image mean now? | Explore associations, compensatory function, opposites, symbolic surplus | Explore representations, appraisals, relational history, embodied schemas | Present meaning may be multiply determined |
| Where did the form come from? | Archetypal pattern may organize recurring imagery | Image schemas and representational redescription supply developmental mechanisms | Recurrent form does not by itself prove inherited image |
| Why is it emotionally charged? | Complex constellation and archetypal numinosity | Attachment threat, implicit memory, trauma, affect regulation | Affect can be overdetermined |
| How does culture matter? | Archetypal patterns are clothed in cultural images | Culture participates in representational and metaphorical elaboration | Culture is not decorative detail |
| Does causal history exhaust meaning? | No; symbol may point beyond known cause | No; development explains formation conditions, not necessarily total significance | Causal and prospective meanings can coexist |
| What is the clinical danger? | Literalizing or reductively explaining away the symbol | Mystifying developmental mechanisms as timeless archetypal truth | Preserve symbolic openness while respecting evidence |

[Knox: chs. 2-3, 8]

### 40. Fantasy and imagination

[KNOX]

Fantasy is a meeting place of several processes in Knox. It can organize affect, defend against unbearable knowledge, extend image schemas metaphorically, express implicit relational expectations, and generate new symbolic possibilities. It is therefore neither a direct photograph of unconscious truth nor mere fabrication.

The developmental reconstruction is especially useful because it explains why fantasy can feel both personal and impersonal. Its concrete characters and stories may be personal, while the dynamic relations organizing them can draw on very early image-schematic patterns. A fantasy of being enclosed, pursued, cut off, engulfed, or carried along a path may therefore be read at several levels without assuming that its imagery was genetically inherited.

Knox also preserves an important Jungian insight: imagination can be **productive**, not only defensive. The mind can reorganize itself by generating new representations and meanings. Representational redescription gives a cognitive-developmental vocabulary for part of that process; Jung's active imagination and transcendent function provide a phenomenological and clinical vocabulary for consciously engaging emerging forms.

[Knox: chs. 3, 5, 7-8; Primary Jung comparison: CW 8, "The Transcendent Function"]

### 41. Reflective function, transcendent function, and inner dialogue

[KNOX / JUNG PRIMARY]

Knox explicitly sees resonance between reflective function and Jung's emphasis on dialogue with unconscious positions. Jung's transcendent function depends on allowing conscious and unconscious standpoints to become sufficiently differentiated that a new position can emerge. Knox's reflective function depends on representing mental states as mental states rather than treating them as unquestioned reality.

The overlap is real but limited.

- Reflective function is a developmental capacity to recognize minds, intentions, appraisals, separateness, and psychological causality.
- The transcendent function is a Jungian process for mediating tension between conscious and unconscious opposites and generating a new symbolic attitude.

Reflective function may make transcendent-function work more possible because a person can hold more than one perspective without immediately collapsing them. But reflective function is not the transcendent function, and gaining mentalization does not by itself constitute individuation.

[Knox: ch. 6; Primary Jung: CW 8, "The Transcendent Function"]

### 42. Romantic fusion and the loss of mind as an internal object

[KNOX]

One of the more relationally important consequences of Chapter 6 is Knox's treatment of romantic fusion. Intense love can be accompanied by a fantasy that separateness has been overcome. In its healthier forms this can coexist with awareness of two minds. In more problematic forms, the partner becomes experienced as if he or she should know, feel, want, and interpret exactly as oneself does.

From a reflective-function perspective, conflict then becomes more than disagreement. It can feel like proof that the relationship has ceased to exist. The partner's independent subjectivity is experienced as betrayal or withdrawal rather than as the condition of a real relationship between two people.

This is not a diagnosis of romantic intensity. It is a conceptual warning: **mutuality requires separateness**. A relationship that can tolerate two minds has more room for dialogue, correction, and repair than one organized around the demand for psychic merger.

[Knox: ch. 6]

---

## Part VII - Therapeutic Relationship and Change

### 43. The therapeutic relationship as developmental and symbolic process

[KNOX]

Chapter 7 asks what follows clinically if the preceding model is accepted. Knox does not choose between "interpretation" and "relationship." She argues that attachment and information-processing theory can explain why both matter.

Interpretation can create explicit representation, narrative coherence, and alternative appraisal. Relationship can provide new implicit experience, regulation, repair, and a revised model of self and other. The two processes interact: a relationship without reflection can repeat old patterns, while interpretation without new experience may remain intellectually correct but procedurally powerless.

The analyst is therefore not merely a neutral screen on which the patient's inner world is projected. The analyst is an actual other person whose reliability, failures, timing, emotional participation, and capacity for repair become part of the patient's experience. At the same time, the patient inevitably experiences the analyst through prior working models and complexes. The analytic field contains both **old model and new reality**.

[Knox: ch. 7, pp. 165-198]

### 44. Interpretative and relational traditions

[KNOX]

Knox reviews the long debate about whether change occurs primarily through insight/interpretation or through a new relationship. Her attachment-based answer is not that one side wins. The two address different information formats.

- Interpretations can help build explicit links, increase narrative competence, and make previously implicit patterns representable.
- Repeated responsive interaction can revise implicit relational knowledge even before the patient can formulate the change verbally.
- Transference activates old models in the present, creating an opportunity to observe them and also to experience alternatives.
- Repair after inevitable mismatch can become more important than an ideal of perfect attunement.

This account helps explain why theoretically different therapies can sometimes produce change: their explicit narratives differ, but they may all provide enough reflective responsiveness, relational safety, and opportunity for reorganization. Knox does **not** conclude that theory is irrelevant. A theory can still harm if it systematically misrepresents the patient's mind or forecloses alternative meanings.

[Knox: ch. 7]

### 45. The three-part attachment/information-processing model of analytic change

[KNOX]

Knox summarizes an attachment-oriented account of change in three interlocking processes.

#### 45.1 State-dependent retrieval

The analytic situation activates internal working models of earlier important relationships. The patient experiences the analyst through expectations and affects that feel present and self-evident. This is one basis of transference.

#### 45.2 Development of narrative competence and integration

Implicitly organized patterns can be connected with episodic memories, affect, current enactments, and a more coherent story. Dissociated working models can become representable together instead of alternating without dialogue.

#### 45.3 Formation of new internal working models

Repeated experience of a reliable, responsive, reflective other can create new expectations. In Knox's language this supports movement from insecure toward more secure attachment and an increase in reflective function.

These are not sequential stages that always occur in a neat order. They are mutually reinforcing. A new relationship makes reflection safer; reflection clarifies the old model; narrative gives the pattern continuity; repair provides disconfirming evidence; repeated evidence strengthens the new model.

[Knox: ch. 7, pp. 178-180 and subsequent sections]

### 46. State-dependent retrieval and transference

[KNOX]

The analytic relationship is unusually capable of activating attachment models because it combines emotional disclosure, asymmetry, uncertainty about the analyst, dependence on regular contact, and repeated attention to inner states. Under these conditions, the patient may draw heavily on prior models to interpret the analyst.

Knox describes this as **state-dependent retrieval**. A relational state cues a matching body of implicit and explicit information. The patient may suddenly feel as if the analyst is disappointed, unavailable, intrusive, or idealizing, even when the belief cannot be traced to a deliberate thought.

The crucial clinical question is not simply whether the perception is "projection." Knox explicitly adds that the analyst may actually be behaving in a way the patient accurately detects. A relational model therefore requires humility. The task is to discriminate among:

- retrieval of an old relational expectation;
- distortion produced by that expectation;
- a defensive projection;
- accurate perception of the analyst;
- an interpersonal enactment in which both participants contribute;
- a symbolic meaning that exceeds the immediate interpersonal facts.

This is a major safeguard against using transference theory to invalidate the patient's perception.

[Knox: ch. 7, pp. 179-181]

### 47. Projection versus attachment expectation

| Dimension | Jungian / psychodynamic projection | Attachment expectation / IWM activation | Accurate perception | Combined formulation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Core process | A psychic content not recognized as one's own is experienced as belonging to the object | A learned predictive model organizes what is expected from the other | The other actually displays the perceived feature | More than one process can operate at once |
| Requires distortion? | Often, though projection may find a "hook" in the object | No. Expectations can be accurate, partly accurate, or outdated | No | Degree of distortion is an empirical question |
| Requires defence? | Projection often has a defensive function but not every Jungian projection is reducible to defence | Not necessarily; predictive models are normal cognitive structures | No | A model can become defensive under threat |
| Historical source | Personal unconscious, complex, archetypal factors | Repeated attachment/relational experience | Present behaviour | History and present facts should both be examined |
| Conscious access | Usually limited until projection is withdrawn | Often implicit and automatic | Can be consciously observable | Reflection can separate expectation from evidence |
| Change | Recognition and withdrawal of projection; integration of content | Updating prediction through new experience and reflection | Better discrimination of the other's actual behaviour | A good formulation may include all three |

**Knox's distinctive point:** in Chapter 7 she explicitly suggests that projection can often be envisaged as activation of internal working models, but she also insists that the analyst may have real characteristics that the patient perceives correctly. This is a **developmental reinterpretation of one pathway into projection**, not a proof that all projection is simply attachment expectation.

[Knox: ch. 7, pp. 179-181; primary Jung comparison: CW 7, CW 9ii, CW 16]

### 48. Jung transference versus Knox's relational-developmental formulation

| Question | Jung primary emphasis | Knox's formulation | Continuity / difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| Why does transference occur? | Projection of unconscious contents; personal and archetypal factors; analytic relationship becomes container for transformation | Attachment-relevant context retrieves implicit working models and old relational expectations; actual interaction also matters | Both see the present relation as carrying more than conscious intention |
